   1| 3
   2|                                                             In Memoriam
   3|                                                     Lance Douglas May
   4| Lance was born in Fitchburg and was a resident of Lunenburg for 69 years. He graduated from
   5| Lunenburg High School in 1968 and Mt. Wachusett Community College in 1971. He also at-
   6| tended Holy Cross College and Fitchburg State University. He worked for the Commonwealth
   7| of Massachusetts, Department of Revenue and the Department of Conservation and Recrea-
   8| tion. Throughout his life he was a devoted public servant to the Town of Lunenburg, serving
   9| on numerous boards and commissions. His public service began in 1971 when he was elected
  10| to the Park Commission at the age of 22. Lance’s career continued to include the Board of Se-
  11| lectmen where he served as Chairman. He also served on the Lunenburg Housing Authority,
  12| Lunenburg Cable Advisory Committee, Lunenburg High School Athletic Hall of Fame Com-
  13| mittee and the Lunenburg Alumni Association. At the time of his death, he was the Chairman
  14| of the Lunenburg Cemetery Commission. Lance coached Little League baseball and soccer in
  15| town for many years.  He was a former member of the Board of Trustees for Mt. Wachusett
  16| Community College and a longtime member of the Lunenburg Historical Society. Lance
  17| served as a former State Committeeman and Chairman of the Lunenburg Republican Party and
  18| worked on many campaigns. Lance was a member of the United Parish of Lunenburg, the Au-
  19| rora Lodge of Masons and was an Aleppo Shriner. He was a devoted family  man and a true
  20| “Lunenburger” in every sense of the word.

   2| LUNENBURG PROFILE
   3| YEAR INCORPORATED- 1728
   4| REGISTERED VOTERS-7870
   5| FY’18 TAX RATE-$19.70
   6| OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER BOARD OF SELECTMEN
   7| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
   8| ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   9| AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES
  10| ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100
  11| Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It  occupies a  land area of
  12| approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
  13| approximately 43 miles from Boston.  It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the
  14| City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east
  15| by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend.
  16| Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meetin g consisting of all registered voters
  17| in the t own. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeti ng, the affairs of the t own
  18| are generally administered by a five member Board of S electmen and a  Town Manager. Local
  19| school affairs are administered b y an elected seven member School Committee while local taxes
  20| are assessed by a three member Board of A ssessors, all are appointed on an at -large basis for
  21| staggered three-year terms.
  22| Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County.  Three water bodies
  23| account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the t own's historical development.  Lake
  24| Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the
  25| remainder of Lunenburg.  This district was developed as a hous ing base for  many factory
  26| workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg.   In many ways the
  27| characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these cities than of
  28| the rest of Lunenb urg. The Hickory Hills  area of t own, around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  29| developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages.  The third body of
  30| water is Shirley Re servoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages
  31| that have now been modifi ed to year -round residences. Commercial and industrial development
  32| is permitted in several areas of town , primarily along the two major arteries (Rout es 2A and 13),
  33| and the t own's peripheries , due largely to the close proximity of Leominster's and Fitchburg's
  34| sewer and water systems.  Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed into single
  35| family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg.  At the Annual Town Meeting on May 4 th,
  36| 2013, the Town voted to establish an Agricultural Commission t o represent, preserve, and
  37| enhance farming in the community and to administer the “Right to Farm” bylaw.

   1|                                                               ADMINISTRATION

===PAGE 18=== (plain, 39 lines)
   1| 18
   2| REPORT OF THE
   3| TOWN MANAGER
   4| The calendar year of 2018 was an eventful year for the Town of Lunenburg.  The Town Manager
   5| and Board of Selectmen continue to work hard on serving the best interests of the Town and
   6| providing a high level of services while remaining fiscally responsible to the taxpayers.  During
   7| 2018 there were many topics addressed and accomplishments to highlight:
   8|  Increasing communication and outreach opportunities was a main theme throughout
   9| 2018.  During the month of January 2018 the Town Manager launched a Town Facebook
  10| page that was created as another means of getting information out to the public on
  11| upcoming events, topics being discussed by local officials, public notices, and other
  12| relevant information. At the request of other departments hoping to increase
  13| communication to residents through social media, the Town Manager created a DPW
  14| Facebook page in May and a Cemetery Commission Facebook page in November.
  15| The Board approved the Other Post Employment Benefit (OPEB) policy drafted by the
  16| Town Manager that designates 10% of the previous year’s certified free cash be
  17| deposited into the OPEB Trust Fund for this liability. This policy was created to continue
  18| to put aside a conservative amount each year for the Town’s growing liability that is also
  19| recognized by bond rating agencies in terms of credit worthiness.
  20| On January 9th the Board held a Complete Streets workshop to listen and learn from
  21| attendees on their ideas for road projects that would improve accessibility for all modes
  22| of transportation and all different ages and abilities in order to create a Project
  23| Prioritization Plan under Massachusetts Department of Transportation’s (MassDOT)
  24| Complete Streets program.  As part of the Complete Streets program, towns need to
  25| create a Prioritization Plan, that is then reviewed by MassDOT, and if approved, makes
  26| them eligible to apply for construction funding for projects on the plan. In July, the
  27| engineering firm hired with a grant through the Complete Streets program, presented a
  28| project Prioritization Plan that was adopted by the Board.  This plan was submitted to the
  29| MassDOT and was approved in September, which made the Town eligible to submit a
  30| “Tier 3” project application for construction funding in the amount up to $400,000.  In
  31| September World Tech Engineering presented a project application that was submitted to
  32| MassDOT in October for a request in the amount of $375,602.   The Town received
  33| notice that the grant was not approved in January of 2019 due to the large number of
  34| applications and limited funding.  However, the Town is eligible to continue applying for
  35| this grant assuming funding from the State for this program continues.
  36| In February, the DPW and the Town Manager, working with the Town’s Pay-As-You- Throw
  37| bag vendor WasteZero, began a retail distribution program so that PAYT bags transitioned from
  38| being ordered and sold by the DPW to the retail stores to the new model having retailers
  39| purchase directly and receive bags directly from WasteZero. The

===PAGE 19=== (plain, 35 lines)
   1| 19
   2| goal of the program was to eliminate the need to monitor inventory and the liability of
   3| having inventory on town premises.
   4|  The Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2019 to the Finance
   5| Committee and Selectmen in mid-February for a total of $36,813,954.63.  Part of the
   6| Town Manager’s recommendation was to continue to fund the Other Post-Employment
   7| Benefits Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the amount of
   8| $100,000, continue funding the Special Purpose Stabilization Account for known large
   9| capital items in the amount of $200,000, and fund the regular Stabilization fund in the
  10| amount of $100,000.  The FY2019 budget also included the hiring of an additional Full
  11| Time Patrolman, an additional Full Time Firefighter, and Assistant Town Mechanic. The
  12| Annual Town Meeting was held on Saturday, May 5th and approved a total budget for
  13| FY19 of $36,867,903.83.
  14| As part of both the Town Manager and Board of Selectmen’s goals surrounding
  15| economic development, the Board endorsed an Economic Development survey in
  16| February that was advertised on the town website, Town Facebook page, and hard copies
  17| at town buildings.
  18| Other business in February included an initial discussion on creating a Special Events
  19| Permit. The discussion was continued at a meeting in June and then again in October.
  20| Also in June, the Board discussed a proposed Noise Bylaw drafted by Police Chief
  21| Marino and Town Counsel and discussed ongoing noise and safety concerns due to
  22| visitors to Shady Point Campground on Lake Shirley. Later in the year, a proposed noise
  23| bylaw was an article on the November Special Town Meeting warrant but was ultimately
  24| defeated.
  25|  During the month of March a group of 7th grade boys presented their idea of creating a
  26| Skate Board park on town property to the Board of Selectmen.  This project was borne
  27| from a school program called “Genius Hour” where students meet weekly to develop an
  28| idea.  The students proposed raising funds through private donations in order to build the
  29| park on public land.  In April the Board voted to approve the proposed use of land to the
  30| east of Kid’s Kingdom and near the TC Passios building as the preferred option for a
  31| future skate park.  The Skateboard Park group provided monthly updates throughout the
  32| year on their fundraising efforts and park design.  At the end of year, the Board met with
  33| the Parks Commission and Skateboard Park group about the scope of the skateboard park
  34| and received additional input from the Parks Commission, who would ultimately be
  35| responsible for the upkeep and maintenance of this park.

===PAGE 20=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 20
   2| In March the Town Manager announced that after seeking quotes for optional dental
   3| insurance for employees, the current provider, Altus Dental, responded with a significant
   4| reduction to the monthly premium and rate guarantee for 2 years.  The Selectmen signed
   5| paperwork to refund general obligation bonds for roads and USDA Water and Sewer
   6| Loans in order to save interest costs over the remaining term of the bonds.
   7| As part of the March 2017 Air Quality Report, various improvements were made at the
   8| Town Hall including cleaning of the HVAC system and the town records were removed
   9| from the Town Hall attic and moved to the TC Passios building due to problems with bird
  10| waste and weight of records in the attic.
  11|  The Town received District Local Technical Assistance for three projects that were
  12| submitted by the Town Manager for the expansion of the “Formation of a Business/Town
  13| Partnership” report, creation of a business brochure to promote the local economy and to
  14| create a permitting guidebook to provide residents and businesses a guide to a
  15| streamlined permitting process.  Throughout the year the Town Manager and the Land
  16| Use Director continued to hold “Business-Town Partnership” meetings that promoted
  17| Lunenburg businesses to form a business association and ways the Town could help
  18| promote local businesses such as the creation of the business brochure that was finalized
  19| by the end of the calendar year.  The brochure has been added to the Town website and
  20| hard copies will be made available in the future.  The Town Manager facilitated a
  21| meeting of the Pepperell Business Association and the Lunenburg Business Association
  22| that resulted in creating a regional business alliance that the Lunenburg Business
  23| Association continued outside of the Town-Business Partnership meetings.
  24| The Board authorized the final payment and signed the Massachusetts School Building
  25| Authority paperwork to closeout the completion of the Middle/High School project.
  26|  This year was an incredible year for road projects in Lunenburg.  Besides the
  27| continuation of the $4M Pavement Management Plan on local roads, the Town was the
  28| recipient of two major Transportation Improvement Projects through MA Department of
  29| Transportation for the reconstruction of Summer Street and the milling and repaving of
  30| Route 13/Chase Road.  The Summer Street Road Reconstruction Project to perform
  31| reconstruction of Summer Street through Lunenburg, Fitchburg, and Leominster was
  32| awarded to Baltazar Contractors in March, who began the project in June and continued
  33| construction throughout the remainder of the year.  Funding for this project was realized
  34| through a Transportation Improvement Project (TIP) and came after many years of
  35| advocacy by prior Selectmen Paula Bertram and DPW Director Jack Rodriquenz.  The
  36| total award for this project was $7 million.  At the end of the calendar year, the project
  37| was ahead of the construction schedule.  The Route 13/Chase Road project began and
  38| was completed in 2018.  The total award for this project was $1 million.

===PAGE 21=== (plain, 34 lines)
   1| 21
   2|  In April, the Selectmen approved the Comcast Cable License Agreement as negotiated by
   3| the Cable Advisory Committee for a ten year agreement, included a capital allocation of
   4| $170,000, increased subscriber fees from 2.5% to 3%, a new fiber video return line to the
   5| Middle/High School, and a third channel dedicated to Education within 2 years.
   6|  During the month of May, Selectman Ebersole and the Town Manager were appointed to
   7| the Superintendent Screening Committee that ultimately put forth candidates for the
   8| School Committee to interview.  The Town Manager reported that after working with the
   9| Public Employee Committee over the course of many months, a new Public Employee
  10| Committee Agreement was finalized that will make health insurance plan changes
  11| beginning on July 1st.  The Town Manager, Finance Director, and Treasurer/Collector
  12| presented a new Tax Title Policy to the Board that outlined a procedure for tax title
  13| properties and moving properties to foreclosure.
  14|  In June, the Treasurer/Collector held a Tax Lien Sale on June 13th that redeemed four tax
  15| liens for a total of $126,232.  During the advertisement period, the Treasurer/Collector
  16| collected a significant amount of delinquent taxes on properties that were made current.
  17| As a follow-up to an article on the Annual Town Meeting, the Selectmen and
  18| Conservation Commission met to discuss the sale of town owned land on Gilchrest Street
  19| to a private party and the acquisition of land for conservation purposes on Gilchrest
  20| Street.  The Selectmen finalized the acquisition of a portion of land at 171 Gilchrest
  21| Street and disposed of town owned land at 161 Gilchrest Street as authorized by a vote
  22| taken at the Annual Town Meeting in the fall of 2018.
  23| On June 5th the Selectmen had a joint meeting with the Finance Committee to discuss the
  24| long term staffing plans for the Police and Fire Departments, moving to Advanced Life
  25| Service, and a discussion about taxing to the levy.
  26| As part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed their goals from FY18
  27| and formalized their goals for FY19.  The goals the Board identified for FY2019 included
  28| a focus on the present and future maintenance and capital replacement of all town assets,
  29| improving public satisfaction through community involvement and public outreach, and
  30| increasing alignment with committees appointed by the Board of Selectmen.  The Town
  31| Manager’s goals including increasing public outreach and improving public relations,
  32| focusing on a comprehensive infrastructure plan, facilitate business and economic
  33| development, advance human resource management, and create written procurement
  34| process and procedures.

===PAGE 22=== (plain, 35 lines)
   1| 22
   2| As part of a Regionalization and Efficiency grant the Town received to perform a
   3| feasibility study on forming a regional animal facility, Roy S. Brown Architects worked
   4| with the Town Manager, Police Chief Marino, the Townsend Police Chief Bailey,
   5| Lunenburg Animal Control Officer Kathy Comeau, and Townsend Animal Control
   6| Officer Mary Letourneau to determine space needs, identify a town owned site to locate a
   7| regional facility, develop a design and provide a cost estimate over the course of the year.
   8| The land behind the Public Safety Building was identified as the primary site for a future
   9| building and the final design that incorporated the space needs of both Townsend and
  10| Lunenburg, the estimated cost of the project was $1.2 million.
  11|  The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY
  12| 2019.
  13|  The Town Manager announced the award of a Green Communities Competitive grant in
  14| the amount of $198,867 for a variety of energy efficiency projects at the school and town
  15| buildings.
  16|  In July, as part of an annual review of the Selectmen’s Policies and Procedures, the Town
  17| Manager presented various updates to the Board’s Manual including a new Flag policy
  18| and new Right of First Refusal procedure for Chapter 61, 61A, and 61B land in July and
  19| the Board adopted certain revisions in August and in October.
  20|  The Board held a Earth Removal Bylaw Workshop with the Planning Board in August to
  21| discuss the Board of Selectmen’s current role in issuing removal permits, the in-depth
  22| review of all current permits done by the Building Commissioner in 2017, and the
  23| proposal to discuss an earth removal bylaw that would transfer the permitting process
  24| over to the Planning Board.  The Selectmen renewed and/or extended a variety of earth
  25| removal permits in August based on recommendations made by the Building
  26| Commissioner.
  27|  Throughout the year the Town Manager worked with the IT Director, staff at the Town’s
  28| website design company, and staff to redesign the Town’s website to become a more user
  29| friendly site that promoted citizen engagement.  The new website launched in August of
  30| 2018. New features included a new page called “How Do I Stay Connected?” which
  31| brings users to all the town social media accounts.  The new website also allows users to
  32| subscribe to updates to information they are interested in and email notifications.
  33| Various updates continued after the website launched including a Communications
  34| Questionnaire on the various ways residents receive information and new ways the Town
  35| could communicate to the public.

===PAGE 23=== (plain, 37 lines)
   1| 23
   2|  In September the Land Use Director provided the Board an update on the status of the
   3| Town’s MS4 Phase II Permit and the submittal of the Notice of Intent that tells the
   4| Environmental Protection Agency how the Town will meet the storm water management
   5| plan goals.  The Town Manager announced the award of MA Department of
   6| Environmental Protection’s Recycling Dividends grant in the amount of $7,200 that can
   7| be used to maximize reuse, recycling, and waste reduction.  The Town purchases rain
   8| barrels, compost bins, and kitchen compost bins and resells them at a reduced rate.
   9|  The Town Manager presented a finalized DPW Union agreement to the Board for a three
  10| year agreement.  The Town Manager asked the Board to review their annual license and
  11| permit fees, since a review had not taken place since July 2016.  A public hearing on the
  12| fee schedule was held in November; the Board created a fee for Sunday Entertainment
  13| Licenses, a fee for Annual Sunday Entertainment Licenses and a fee for changes to
  14| alcohol licenses after their annual renewals.  The October workshop was a joint meeting
  15| with the Finance Committee to review the Town Manager’s proposed amendments to the
  16| Financial Policies and various updates were approved.
  17|  As part of one the Town Manager’s goals related to Economic Development, the Town
  18| Manager presented and the Board approved a charge to create an Economic Development
  19| Committee in November.
  20| As part of ongoing discussions with Casella about the global changes in the recycling
  21| market due to China’s National Sword Policy in January of 2018, the Town Manager
  22| initiated an effort to start a Recycling Campaign to reduce the contamination ratio in
  23| Lunenburg’s recycling materials.  Due to the change in the recycling market the Town
  24| began discussions with Casella about renegotiating the portion of the contract related to
  25| recyclables.  The Board authorized the Town Manager to renegotiate the Town’s contract
  26| with Casella that would result in the Town paying for recycling beginning in July of
  27| 2019.
  28|  In November Selectman Bob Ebersole announced he would be stepping down from the
  29| Board of Selectmen after the Special Town Meeting due to his impending relocation to
  30| another town.  The Board voted in December not to call a Special Election to fill
  31| Selectmen Ebersole’s seat that would end in May.
  32|  The Special Town Meeting on November 13th included a number of articles such as
  33| amending the FY 2019 budget to capture additional tax growth in both real estate and
  34| personal property, a citizen’s petition to ban the use of plastic bags, fund the first year of
  35| the DPW Union Agreement, a proposed Noise Bylaw, revisions to the Americans with
  36| Disabilities Committee, and zoning bylaw changes including extending the moratorium
  37| on recreational marijuana regulations until May of 2019.

===PAGE 24=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 24
   2|  In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler,
   3| weekday entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses.  The
   4| Planning Board came before the Board to request the placement of 5 ballot questions to
   5| prohibit establishments for the sale, transport, research, cultivation, and product
   6| manufacturing of recreational marijuana to be placed on the May 2019 Annual Election
   7| ballot.  Also in December, the Board heard from the Open Space and Recreation
   8| Committee regarding an updated Open Space and Recreation Plan and provided a letter
   9| of support to submit the Plan to the State.
  10| The development of a plan for town facilities, specifically the Town Hall, the Ritter Building, the
  11| old Primary, the Brooks House, and the Passios continued to be a consistent topic at many
  12| Selectmen meetings, the Annual Town Meeting and the Special Town Meeting.  On February 6th
  13| a public forum was held with the senior community to discuss town facilities.  The input
  14| received at this meeting included a majority of participants agreed that the old Primary building
  15| and Brooks House should be demolished and that the space be used as green space.  Most also
  16| agreed that retaining the Ritter to lease, not sell, for small retail space or a restaurant was a
  17| preferred option.  On February 13th Vertex, the firm hired with funds approved at the November
  18| 2017 Special Town Meeting to perform property condition assessments for the Brooks House,
  19| Town Hall, Ritter Memorial, the old Primary building, TC Passios, Turkey Hill Elementary, and
  20| the Eagle House, presented their findings. The goal of the property condition assessments was to
  21| identify the immediate and five year projection of repairs needed to those buildings by looking at
  22| the current condition and current use of the building, not the impact of changing the use of the
  23| building.  Another goal for performing these assessments was to incorporate facilities capital
  24| projects into the Five Year Capital Plan that would assist in developing a long term facilities plan
  25| that addresses necessary repairs and maintenance of those town buildings. In April, the Board
  26| held an Economic Development Workshop with the Agricultural Commission and Planning
  27| Board to discuss future use of the town facilities to promote the farming community and
  28| agriculture and ecotourism as the focus for economic development. The Board held a Workshop
  29| on Town Facilities to discuss the member’s individual ideas for each of the buildings (Town
  30| Hall, the Ritter, TC Passios, Brooks House, and the old Primary).  The Board concurred that the
  31| TC Passios was conceptually the preferred facility for consolidated town offices.  In September
  32| the Board held another workshop on Town Facilities and were presented an initial conceptual
  33| design for consolidated municipal offices by Paul Lieneck of Haynes, Lieneck and Smith. The
  34| Board requested additional designs that eliminated unallocated space and reallocation of record
  35| storage space to another area in the design.  The architect re-presented two additional conceptual
  36| designs and estimated costs in October.  The Board held another Town Facilities Workshop in
  37| November with the School Committee to discuss the long term plan for Turkey Hill Elementary
  38| School, their input on the three

===PAGE 25=== (plain, 26 lines)
   1| 25
   2| conceptual designs to consolidate town offices and school central offices, future school
   3| building needs, and other joint planning opportunities.
   4| As of writing this report, it has been over two years since I was appointed your Town Manager
   5| and it is a responsibility that I take very seriously.  The residents of Lunenburg demonstrate the
   6| true meaning of community that I have witnessed in various acts, whether it be supporting our
   7| local businesses, non-profit groups raising funds for those in need, or words of gratitude for our
   8| public safety and public works departments.  I have the privilege of working with many talented
   9| individuals that are committed to helping the residents and business owners of Lunenburg and
  10| are consistently looking for ways to better serve the public.  The accomplishments noted above,
  11| among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without the cooperation of
  12| dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town government turn.
  13| Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson, for all your hard work and keeping the
  14| office running smoothly.  I would also like to thank the committee and board members,
  15| department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town.
  16| Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the department heads that lead our various
  17| departments:  James Marino, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire Chief; Jack Rodriquenz, DPW
  18| Director; Karen Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari,
  19| Treasurer/Collector; Louise Paquette, Assessors; Steve Malandrinos, IT Director; Adam Burney,
  20| Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA Director; Martha Moore, Library Director; Kathy
  21| Herrick, Town Clerk;  and all their staff for their talent and working together to make our town
  22| run as efficiently as possible.  Lastly, thank you to the residents and businesses that make
  23| Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live.
  24| Respectfully submitted,
  25| Heather R. Lemieux
  26| Town Manager

===PAGE 55=== (ocr, 1 lines)
   1|                                                                  TOWN SERVICES

===PAGE 56=== (plain, 41 lines)
   1| 56
   2| Architectural Preservation District Commission (APDC)
   3| 2018 marked the first year that the APD bylaw was fully in effect. The APDC accepted and re-
   4| viewed six applications for construction in the district: at 920 Massachusetts Avenue, 99 Lan- caster,
   5| 944 Massachusetts Avenue, 71 Highland, 136 Leominster, and 15 Oak. Two applications were like-
   6| for-like roof replacements, and the Committee considered ways to streamline the application process
   7| for such cases. Through the review of real and mock-applications, the APDC solidified its under-
   8| standing of the process and criteria for approval.
   9| Two cases of Demolition by Neglect were addressed: at 53 Lancaster and The Old Primary School.
  10| In the former case, volunteers were organized to clear vegetation away from the house and repair
  11| gutters. The owner was encouraged to move into assisted living. In the latter case, the APDC notified
  12| the Board of Selectmen and Town Manager of the violation.
  13| A warrant article came before town meeting to secure public funds for the demolition of the Old Pri-
  14| mary School. The APDC opposed the article and helped to defeat it.
  15| Chairman McGrath offered to volunteer his time and skills to restore the Old Town Pound in co- op-
  16| eration with the DPW. The project was still in planning stages at the end of 2018.
  17|                                        Building Department
  18| The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massa-
  19| chusetts Avenue.  Garry Rhodes serves as Building Commissioner and Lisa Normandin serves as the
  20| Administrative Assistant to the Building Department.  Lisa also serves as the Administrative Assistant
  21| to the Zoning Board of Appeals.  Andrew Hudson, Local Inspector resigned and will be missed.
  22| Building Permits for 2018 have increased slightly from 538 in 2017 to 544.  Solar permits continue to
  23| be strong.  We continue to see permits for weatherization which can be contributed to support provided
  24| by “Mass Save”.  In many cases Mass Save provides financial support to homeowners who upgrade
  25| insulation and do air sealing.
  26| The state of Massachusetts as of January 1 st 2018 has now implemented the 9 th Edition of the Building
  27| Code, CMR 780.  The changes will impact both residential and commercial construction.  The ninth
  28| edition is based on 2015 International Building Code.
  29| The Town Clerk issues Business Certificates.  Before the Town Clerk will issue a Business Certificate
  30| you must first fill out a “Form of Intent”.  The “Form of Intent ” is used to ensure that you will be in
  31| compliance with Lunenburg Zoning Bylaws.  A copy of the “Form of Intent ” can be obtained either
  32| online at lunenburgma.gov or by coming by the Building Department Office.  Once the form is re-
  33| viewed and signed by the Building Commissioner it can be presented to the Town Clerk.
  34| All 120 apartment units known as Whalom Luxury Apartments located off Whalom Road have been
  35| completed.  Occupancy permits have been issued for all the building.
  36| Applications for Building, Electrical, Mechanical and Sheet Metal, Plumbing and Gas and Driveway
  37| permits can now be applied for online at lunenburgma.viewpointcloud.com.  Hard copy forms to apply
  38| for these permits can be obtained on -line at lunenburgma.gov or by stopping by at 960 Massachusetts
  39| during normal business hours.
  40| Garry A. Rhodes
  41| Building Commissioner & Zoning Official

===PAGE 59=== (plain, 49 lines)
   1| 59
   2| CONSERVATION COMMISSION
   3| The Conservation Commission is a seven-member commission, appointed to 3-year terms by the Board of
   4| Selectmen, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL Chapter 40 s 8C.
   5| The Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL Chapter 131 s 40 and Article
   6| XXII of the local bylaws for the protection of wetlands.
   7|      The year 2018 was, as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings
   8| and meetings for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single
   9| family upkeep projects and larger scale industrial/commercial proposals along the Electric Avenue section
  10| of Lunenburg. The Conservation Commission worked to spell out goals of preparing to use 2019 to
  11| complete revising its regulations and land use policies to provide consistency with the original bylaw and
  12| to find those sections that may be out of date and need to be modified. This effort has been methodically
  13| approached and very comprehensive and is estimated to be completed by summer of 2019.
  14|      The commission also held numerous inspections with the commission administrator on weekends prior
  15| to meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda.
  16| The commission continues to work with the Town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming stormwater
  17| mandates from the EPA and working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary
  18| codification as well as aiding with the community Notice of Intent to the EPA under the “MS4”
  19| regulations.
  20|      The Conservation Commission implemented its forest stewardship planning after receiving grants
  21| noted on last year’s annual report. These stewardship plans for five individual parcels will aid the
  22| commission in implementing environmental management plans which include timber stand management.
  23|      This March, the commission bid and completed timber and environmental management in the small
  24| town forest. Other Conservation areas plans have been completed for the Lane property, the Large and
  25| Small Town Forests, the open space on Robbs Hill and the Cowdery. The commission retained Gary
  26| Gouldrup of New England Forestry Consultants, a noted forester with many years of experience working
  27| in Lunenburg and all of the adjacent towns/cities in this region serving those communities and private
  28| landowners. The Conservation Commission also held public recreational walks through its open space
  29| parcels and plans to continue to do so. The Commission has and continues to hold public forums on the
  30| implementation of its open space policies and timber-stand management. As a result of some of the
  31| meetings and forums held, the commission applied for and received funding from the Commonwealth to
  32| author a stewardship plan with a goal of enhancing bird habitat at the Hollis Road Conservation Area,
  33| developed under the DCR "Forestry for the Birds" program in partnership with the Massachusetts
  34| Audubon Society.  The plan is being developed with a grant received from DCR.
  35|      Copies of the current environmental stewardship plans are available on the Conservation Commission
  36| website at http://www.lunenburgma.gov/content/204/222/default.aspx
  37|      The commission has also continued to work closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills in the
  38| implementations of their management plans becoming actively involved in continuation of formulating
  39| updates designed to give a better picture of the overall health of the ecosystems and the various ways to
  40| ensure continued protection of these valuable resources.
  41|      The commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!
  42| Respectfully Submitted,
  43| Richard Bursch, Chair
  44| Todd Dwyer, Vice-Chair
  45| Bob Pease
  46| Catie Childs
  47| Carl Luck
  48| Kenneth Jones, Jack Rabbitt
  49| Matthew S. Marro, Conservation Administrator

   2| COUNCIL ON AGING
   3| July 1, 2017 – June 30, 2018
   4| The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   5| supportive services, programs and education.  Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts
   6| General Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or
   7| services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   8| The FY18 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative
   9| Assistant and Transportation; Faith A. Anderson, Outreach Coordinator; Joanne Brazell, Meal Site
  10| Coordinator;  Judy Alario and Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants;  Jim McGuigan,  Paul Grunditz,
  11| MART Van Drivers.
  12| Outreach Coordinator Faith Anderson retired in November 2017 and Pauline Roy was hired as the new
  13| Outreach Coordinator in December 2017.   James Barrett was hired in February 2019 as a MART Van
  14| Driver.
  15| The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg
  16| Board of Selectmen. The FY16 Council on Aging Board members were; Chairperson Pete Lincoln,  Vice-
  17| Chairperson Jacquelyn Dwyer, Betty DiGiacomo,  Sarah Grant, Brian Guenard, Deb Lincoln, Kevin
  18| McNally, Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, Jane Rabbitt, and Judith Tarbell.
  19| The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House
  20| Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of
  21| Massachusetts, and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.
  22| We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House.  In FY18 we had 48 volunteers who
  23| contributed 2,570.5 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the meals on
  24| wheels program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, office help, library, and friendly visitors.  We greatly
  25| appreciate their service.
  26| The Eagle House offers a variety of programs, such as health and wellness, arts and crafts,
  27| intergenerational, bocce, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, and social events both at the
  28| senior center and offsite.  There were approximately 901 people who utilized the senior center in FY18.
  29| Those 901 people compiled 26,650 units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed
  30| above.
  31| In FY18 the Council on Aging delivered 4,114 Meals on Wheels to 45 clients and provided 3,244 rides to
  32| 68 clients on our senior van.
  33| We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the
  34| importance of supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders.
  35| Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc.  This group provided
  36| funding for new living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, garden
  37| upkeep and spring mulch at the Eagle House Senior Community Center.  Also special thanks to all of our
  38| outside agencies for donating time and programming to our clients.

===PAGE 63=== (plain, 39 lines)
   1| 63
   2| DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   3| The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery,
   4| Sewer, and Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing
   5| and scheduling personnel and equipment to best serve the residents of the community through the
   6| effective delivery of an extensive scope of services with a high degree of efficiency.
   7|  “The mission of the Department of Public Works is to protect, preserve, and improve the
   8| Town’s infrastructure a nd related assets in a manner which meets and exceeds the current and
   9| future social and economic needs of the community, to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality
  10| environment for the Town’s residents and visitors, and to provide a cost effective and
  11| environmentally sound means of disposing of solid waste and wastewater.”
  12|  Honesty and integrity are absolutes. They characterize the only forum in which anything
  13| worthwhile can be accomplished.
  14|  Make every effort to proceed correctly the first time. It may t ake a bit longer but will not
  15| require repeated attempts.
  16|  Loyalty up and down the chain of command is essential to success.
  17|  Every employee is essential. Those at the top are support people. The real work is done by
  18| the men and women who drive the trucks, operate the equipment, turn the wrenches, and provide
  19| a seamless flow of paperwork.
  20| DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
  21| Public Works is either working or on call twenty-four hours a day three hundred and sixty-
  22| five days a year to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related emergency
  23| that may arise.
  24| We strive to enhance the awareness of the value  Public Works provides to our Town, and
  25| ensure others are aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to
  26| make sure efforts are not forgotten as they merge into the fabric of our everyday  expectation of
  27| service.
  28| Major events , particularly in the form of devastating storms,  over the past years have
  29| highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency
  30| management. Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order
  31| to facilitate the required services of others. We must continue to build on this awareness.
  32| This department must also reach out to t he public we serve and educate them to the fact
  33| that the common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated
  34| staff of individuals, service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid
  35| waste collection and recycling operations, tree removal, storm water management facilities and
  36| grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries and parks are the expected services
  37| supporting everyone in the community.
  38| We will continue to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather
  39| support for annual budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.

===PAGE 64=== (plain, 41 lines)
   1| 64
   2| DEPARTMENTAL GOALS FOR FISCAL 2020
   3|  Continue to coordinate administrative directives with the activities of all public
   4| service departments and organizations within the DPW to improve service levels.
   5|  Coordinate with those who work daily in Town facilities to best prioritize capital
   6| improvements.
   7|  Increase involvement with our insurance provider to make better use of available
   8| resources in an effort to obtain credits toward lessening premiums.
   9|  Set realistic expectations. Make clear through public education that due to limited
  10| resources, particularly in the area of personnel , the level of service provided by
  11| Public Works is not always optimal.
  12|  Work diligently to establish and maintain a high level of courteous communication
  13| and interaction between members of all boards and commission.
  14| PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  15|  It has become  a high priority  goal of the BOS as Road Commissioners, to achie ve a
  16| pavement condition of 72 %-75% Town-wide. Although State funded Chapter 90 funds remains
  17| stagnant, the dedicated Pavement Management lin e in the operational budget has grown
  18| substantially.
  19| Results of the Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan displayed clearly the inequity
  20| of the distribution of paving funds toward higher use main and collector roads leaving local
  21| neighborhoods unattended. Using the PMP as evidence, residents went to the polls to vote
  22| favorably on a four-million-dollar debt exclusion targeting exclusively the local roads. Expending
  23| approximately a million dollars in the initial year, and in excess of a million the following year has
  24| resulted in a major enhancement of road conditions.
  25| The debt exclusion funding  combined with state and o perational funds has nearly tripled
  26| the total paving value of years past as we attack a nine-million-dollar backlog of repairs.
  27| Our pot hole log has and is expected to lessen exponentially as new paving increases , a
  28| clear benefit of the dollars expended. There is much work yet to do but expectations are high.
  29| FLEET MAINTENANCE
  30| The DPW is responsible for the repairs and preventative maintenance to all Town vehicles
  31| and equipment. High performance police units, first responder Fire and Rescue apparatus,  park,
  32| cemetery, and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced
  33| by department personnel. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent  and
  34| therefore costlier. Due to the volatile global oil market, the overall cost of fuel and petroleum based
  35| products continues to be a moving target and therefore difficult to estimate annually.
  36| The installation of a vehicle maintenance software program continues to be a valuable tool.
  37| Each repair is logged by department and vehicle identification number and memorialized in a data
  38| bank. Every vehicle can be tracked for part numbers, required services , as well as frequency and
  39| cost of repairs over the life of the equipment. As Director I have developed and submitted to Town
  40| Officials a vehicle replacement schedule based upon that created by the American Public Works
  41| Association in order to more clearly represent our capital needs.

===PAGE 65=== (plain, 42 lines)
   1| 65
   2| FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
   3| The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
   4| DPW Garage 520 Chase Road  Public Safety Complex
   5| Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.  Town Hall
   6| Town Beach    Town Common
   7| Ritter Bldg.    Teen Center
   8| Old Primary School                          Veterans Park
   9| The Eagle House
  10| All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding school properties
  11|   A facilities staff of one individual remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year.
  12| Staff attempts to keep  buildings compliant with current codes to insure a  safe secure  work
  13| environment. Any significant interruption of service within Town buildings  carries the potential
  14| of disrupting or disabling  necessary services provided to the community. The facilities support
  15| staff are problem solvers and technicians with a wide range of experience encompassing all of the
  16| construction trades. Together with a stable of reliable professionals they deliver services or oversee
  17| those functions provided by specialized subcontractors. Unfortunately, the creation of a  capital
  18| replacement program has fallen short of expectations. Years of neglect have created a significant
  19| backlog of necessary repairs and improvements. The process has become reactionary at best. The
  20| Building Reuse Committee reported clearly that asset management relative to our buildings
  21| requires a professionally prepared assessment and a long range capital plan. To this end our plan
  22| for 2020 is to procure the services of Project Management Consultant to survey, quantify, project
  23| building needs  and establish an annual cost . Public Works continues its use of a  building
  24| maintenance software program as a framework for day to day activities. R equests for service are
  25| acknowledged immediately, prioritized, and completed in a timely manner. Those individuals
  26| requesting service are contacted upon completion of the tasks a nd a history of the repair is filed
  27| electronically.
  28| ENVIRONMENTAL
  29| Storm Water
  30| As a component of the aforementioned “debt exclusion” approximately five hundred
  31| thousand dollars of the four million in total has been earmarked for storm drainage including but
  32| not limited to repair of the existing collection system, and additional construction.
  33| As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repair s are
  34| addressed to avoid paving over problems.  New Federal and State storm water regulations will
  35| likely force the Town to become more proactive albeit with no additional identified source of
  36| funding.
  37|  At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall drainage structures in close proximity
  38| to open water are cleaned and inspected. Public Works maintains an aggressive schedule of street
  39| sweeping and catch basin cleaning, the front line of storm water management.
  40| Waste Water
  41| The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system. Day to day maintenance of the
  42| overall wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations is outsourced to a specialty

===PAGE 66=== (plain, 43 lines)
   1| 66
   2| vendor but continues to be the responsibility of Public Works. Business  management,
   3| inspections, and permitting, remain in-house and the responsibility of the DPW.
   4| Tree Removal
   5|  Storm damage together with the natural attrition of every species creates a backlog of tree
   6| felling and pruning that far exceeds the allotted budget. The department is unable to fulfill each
   7| resident request and must prioritize work based upon the calculated degree of danger to residents
   8| and property. With the establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a  more efficient approach to
   9| defects has been established through  prioritization. Sadly, the amount of work far exceeds the
  10| available revenue. Until there is a substantial infusion of funds, the DPW will act in a reactionary
  11| mode. All work aside from brush removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees is
  12| outsourced due to the necessity of a bucket truck or crane and a licensed professional. Each spring
  13| DPW staff initiates the activity of removing and disposing of woody debris within the right -of-
  14| way together with  a Town -wide program of curbside mowing.  An aggressive program by our
  15| energy provider in recent years  has lessened the burden of the Town to remove those trees  that
  16| may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.
  17| Landfill
  18| The “capped” landfill off Young’s Rd. remains in compliance with the inspection protocol
  19| mandated by  the Division of Enviro nmental Protection.  The site is monitored  regularly for
  20| excessive methane gas as well as the possibility of groundwater contamination.
  21| The frequency of the testing,  reduced through a special permit , acquired as a result of
  22| consistently favorable results, remained constant throughout the year.  The landfill site including
  23| the perimeter fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  24| Adjacent to the “cap” is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both
  25| determined by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are allowed to store these materials through the
  26| procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of
  27| these materials. Other communities are required to transport these tainted materials to an
  28| appropriate site at an excessive cost.
  29| On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage
  30| the annual composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to
  31| our residents and add a valuable component to our recycling program. There has to date been no
  32| dedicated funding toward the organization or long range remediation of this area. With water and
  33| sewer at the perimeter this acreage has been proposed as a potential recreational area for the Town.
  34| As of yet this project has not gained traction.
  35| Solid Waste
  36| In the past, Public W orks would have the responsibility to monitor , order, store , and
  37| distribute trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and recyclables by our
  38| current vendor, Casella Inc. Pay as you throw… Recently we entered into an agreement allowing
  39| our “trash bag” supplier to coordinate the effort between supplier and vendor resulting in some
  40| mixed reviews.
  41| Lake Shirley Dam
  42|            In the early 1990’s, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley
  43| Association turned over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total

===PAGE 67=== (plain, 40 lines)
   1| 67
   2| reconstruction, responsibilities for maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed
   3| solely by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of
   4| the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as well
   5| as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella of the Department of Public Works.
   6| As with the storm water program and ot her EPA / DEP mandates compliance with current and
   7| future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a professional consultant to
   8| assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report.
   9| Winter Operations
  10| Snow and ice present a major challenge to our Town. The safety of the general public
  11| depends on the ability of the DPW to maintain our roadways to a high level of safety  despite the
  12| severity or the duration of snow and ice events. To that end we have developed a winter operations
  13| procedure based upon a detailed  plan with a flexibility to accommodate  the unpredictability of
  14| each storm. The expectation of immediate service is high  and must be delivered to every part of
  15| Town in a timely manner.
  16| Departmental preparedness  begins with successful implementation of the capital plan.
  17| Financial support is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of
  18| services due to breakdowns and repairs.  In the fall, material is delivered to the Chase Rd. facility.
  19| Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible
  20| price for road salt, an absolute necessity. From late summer to early fall vehicle maintenance staff
  21| inspects a nd prepares the  trucks, spreaders and plows for winter operations. During the
  22| construction season road crews address right-of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement
  23| defects in order to eliminate obstacles to snow removal. Each division within the DPW is involved
  24| in winter operations. Typically, DPW staff combines with up to 18  subcontractors to provide
  25| service.
  26| Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the
  27| overall cost. For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than
  28| a long duration freezing rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, the
  29| department operates in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average
  30| winter.
  31|  It is a dedicated group of individuals who successfully challenge the worst Nature has to
  32| offer regardless of severity or duration.
  33| “DPW workers are the original Storm Troopers”
  34| PARKS
  35| In conjunction with the three ele cted Park Commissioners DPW staff  oversees and
  36| maintains all parks and grounds exclusive of school pro perty. While resources of the DPW are
  37| available as required, much of the Tow n-wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, resulting in
  38| significant savings and increased efficiency  over an in -house operation. Repair of t he multi-use
  39| track complex is imminent. After many years, a mutual effort between the Commissioners and all
  40| users of the facility is gaining traction.

===PAGE 68=== (plain, 17 lines)
   1| 68
   2| CEMETERY
   3| One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North
   4| and South cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with
   5| the three members of the Cemetery Commission.  The DPW supplies equipment and support staff
   6| for, on average, forty burials a year and general maintenance of the facilities.
   7| “Every Town Department is, in some manner, and at some time a client of the Department

===PAGE 69=== (plain, 39 lines)
   1| 69
   2| FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   3| Lunenburg Finance Committee Report, Fiscal Year 2019
   4| The Town Budget to be presented for approval is balanced, consistent with the Town’s fiscal policies, and reflects
   5| recognition that future needs must be addressed.  Department Heads have presented ‘level service’ budgets, and requests
   6| beyond level service.  Based on the financial forecast and funding opportunities, the Town Manager is proposing increases
   7| beyond level service in historically high need areas, such as police and fire.  An assistant mechanic position, which had
   8| been removed to address past fiscal challenges, has been returned to the Department of Public Works.
   9| The Town is now in the position to add services to Public Safety through 1 Police Officer and 1 Firefighter/EMT.  There
  10| is a recognized need to continue to grow Public Safety as the Town and population demographics continue to change.
  11| The Town also remains committed to incorporating steady increases (tied to motor vehicle excise tax increases) in the
  12| Pavement Management Plan to ensure that recent improvements paid for through debt exclusion will be adequately
  13| maintained going forward.  $50,000 of the DPW budget increase addresses this.
  14| As we attempt to manage costs associated with the roads, the Town has also begun to set aside monies for other
  15| infrastructure and building needs by establishing and funding a Special Purpose Stabilization Fund.  This budget deposits
  16| $200,000 into that fund.
  17| Debt Service continues to decrease.  The debt for the purchase of a fire engine will be retired during the coming fiscal
  18| year.  This makes room for short term borrowing within the general budget to help replace the aging ladder truck, which
  19| has been on the Capital Planning radar for quite some time.  The new debt will not hit the budget until 2020, so is not
  20| reflected in the budget presented here, but is planned for going forward.
  21| The Town has made several changes to Health Insurance plans over the past few years that have helped keep increases
  22| low.  This year, the increase in premiums is coming in at about 7 percent, which affects both the ‘Unclassified’ portion of
  23| the budget, and the School Department budget.  A growing portion of Health Insurance costs is coverage for retirees.  To
  24| address this growing liability, the Town is continuing to fund the OPEB Trust Fund, this year depositing $100,000 for
  25| post-employment Health Insurance payments.
  26| The Retirement Assessment has gone up more than 10 percent.  Town employees pay into 1 of 2 retirement systems.
  27| Licensed school district employees contribute to the Massachusetts Teacher Retirement System, which requires no
  28| additional town funding.  Any additional funding of this Retirement System is done at the State level, and is reported in
  29| notes to the Town Financial Statements as an ‘On Behalf’ payment.  Non licensed school personnel and Town side
  30| employees contribute to the Worcester County Retirement System.  The Town makes two payments to this system, an
  31| employer contribution for active employees and an assessment to fund the ‘Unfunded Liability’ of future payments to
  32| retirees.  It is the future liability assessment that is driving the increase.
  33| The total budget presented incorporates the following changes:
  34|  General Government is increased $64,780, including salary adjustments, and technology
  35| increases.
  36|  Central Purchasing has no change.
  37|  Police is increased $113,408 including salary adjustments and a new police officer position.
  38|  Fire is increased $112,498 including salary adjustments, a new firefighter/EMT and
  39| overtime.

===PAGE 70=== (plain, 42 lines)
   1| 70
   2|  Radio Watch is increased $20,749
   3|  Total Protection, which includes Police and Fire, is increased $256,468, or 8.7%, which still
   4| leaves a gap to catch up to the growing needs of the town, an ongoing priority.
   5|  DPW is increased $79,207 adding $50,000 for pavement management as planned along with
   6| salary increases and the return of the assistant mechanic position.
   7|  Buildings and Facilities is decreased, but that is from the adjusted budget at last year’s
   8| Special Town Meeting which identified funds to make needed repairs and fund a full survey
   9| of maintenance and repair needs of several Town buildings.
  10|  Solid Waste is increased $3,130, following a large decrease made the previous year.
  11|  Health & Sanitation is increased $2,491.
  12|  Council on Aging is increased $5,694, which moves some funding for MART van scheduling
  13| to the town ahead of anticipated reductions in formula reimbursements from MART.
  14|  Veterans Administration is level funded.
  15|  Public Schools is increased $588,061, or 3% more than the FY2018 budget.  It is a level
  16| service budget.
  17|  Monty Tech assessment is decreased $53,516.  This reflects decreased enrollments, which
  18| fluctuate year to year.  The savings in this account were considered too unpredictable to
  19| absorb into operating costs and were thus used to offset the borrowing anticipated to fund the
  20| ladder truck.
  21|  Library is increased $18,744, a 4.9% increase representing a level service budget.
  22|  Debt Service is decreased $158,811, the second year of declining debt service.
  23|  Unclassified is increased $233,495, driven completely by health insurance increases.
  24|  Retirement Assessment is increased $114,832, about twice as much as last year.
  25| The Capital Planning Committee has recommended a plan requiring $1,455,214.  This warrant fully
  26| funds the plan, and includes $417,000 of short term borrowing, with payments expected to begin in
  27| Fiscal Year 2020, after the debt on the last Engine purchased has retired.
  28| In summary, the budget presented addresses at least current service levels, with some increase in
  29| some departments.  The Finance Committee will continue to address the question of ensuring the
  30| budget addresses the priorities of the Town of Lunenburg.  We congratulate the Town
  31| administration’s work to maintain the AA+ bond rating, and thank all of the town employees for their
  32| continued service.  We are grateful for the continued cooperation of the Town Manager, each
  33| department head, and other Town Boards and Committees to ensure that every budget question is
  34| answered.

===PAGE 71=== (plain, 33 lines)
   1| 71
   2| HISTORICAL COMMISSION
   3|   The Historical Commission consists of five appointed volunteers who meet once a month from
   4| September to June. The work of the Historical Commission is preservation and appreciation of
   5| all things historic in the Town of Lunenburg. The Historical Commission has through the past
   6| year been represented on the Architectural Preservation District Commission by two of our
   7| members, Richard McGrath and Cullen Dwyer. The design standards for the APDC have been
   8| completed.
   9|  The Bronze Marker project has been completed, which was made possible through the efforts of
  10| Joshua Herrick of Troop 1728 Lunenburg and his Eagle Scout Project. All the houses in
  11| Lunenburg’s National Registered Historic District were surveyed for National Registered
  12| Historic plaques (last available in 1987) and those home’s missing plaques were purchased by
  13| the Historic Commission from the Erie Landmark Company.  Installation was completed and
  14| finally all the houses in our National Registered Historic District will get the recognition they
  15| deserve. Recognizing our Nationally Registered houses will be an ongoing effort of the
  16| Historical Commission with both ongoing registration and providing plaques to houses that meet
  17| the criteria.
  18|   Our second marker project is nearing completion with the purchase of the Arnold & Litch
  19| family cemetery marker from the Erie Landmark Company. This will mark a small family
  20| cemetery plot that was lost to neglect on West Street. The installation of this marker will be this
  21| spring.
  22|   We continue to hold hearings and enact the Demolition Delay Bylaw of the Town of Lunenburg
  23| for the destruction of Historic properties. The Demolition Delay Bylaw had the delay expanded
  24| to 18 months at the Fall 2018 Town Meeting. We are involved in preservation efforts for 1755
  25| House at 68 Elm Street, which is under demolition delay. This house was known as the Battles
  26| House and Benjamin P. Redington who owned it in 1790, served in the Revolutionary War and
  27| mustered from Lunenburg.
  28| Members of the Historical Commission:
  29| Rebecca Lantry,
  30| Richard McGrath,
  31| Cullen Dwyer,
  32| Jennifer Sanderson
  33| Bill Lakso

   4| Adult Services
   5| The addition of Muir Haman to the staff as Adult Services Librarian/Assistant Director has allowed a
   6| renewed focus on the services we offer our adult patrons and focus on a strategic approach to adult
   7| program planning. This strategic approach has included a focus on data and analysis, and increased effort
   8| to bring both thematically related topics and additional variety to the programming calendar. Between
   9| July and December, 22 adult programs were added to the schedule, and these programs brought in 443
  10| attendees. This approach has also led to a deepening of the relationship with Fitchburg State University
  11| when the library hosted its first ALFA class, with programs utilizing student expertise and talent, and by
  12| inviting faculty presenters to enrich our evening lectures. Additionally, much of the legwork has been
  13| done to present our first ever adult summer reading program, which will kick off with the visit of
  14| bestselling author Andrew Forsthoefel on June 30th, 2019. Through the adult services department, the
  15| library’s strong baseline of services for all groups by creating and providing resource lists for several
  16| specific populations: refugees, sensory needs families, nature lovers, and those who desire social services.
  17| FY20 goals:
  18|  to continue to improve our strong baseline of library services,
  19|  to make the library a place of thriving, delightful programming
  20|  finalize the components of a long-term plan for bringing a diverse, inclusive, entertaining, and
  21| educational calendar of library-sponsored programs to the community by utilizing data, analysis
  22| and experimentation.
  23|  To continue to strengthen our relationship with local institutions of higher learning.
  24|  To further deepen the collection in subject areas which resonate with the community and which
  25| connect to successful programs.
  26| Children’s Services:
  27| The Children’s Area of the Lunenburg Public Library continues to be a dynamic, entertaining
  28| destination for children and families throughout the region.  Attendance at weekly story times for
  29| children ages birth-5 remains strong.  Our afterschool offerings for school aged children, i.e.
  30| “Tuesdays with Stories Book Group” and Lego Club are also well attended.  New for F Y 2019
  31| are weekly visits from the fifth graders at Turkey Hill Elementary School.  These children enjoy
  32| the opportunity with these visits to explore and become comfortable with our wonderful library
  33| and experience first-hand all that we have to offer. We have seen many new faces with these
  34| visits and have issued lots of new library cards!
  35| Collection evaluation and development continues as we strive to provide the most up-to-date,
  36| substantive materials for our younger patrons.  The beginning chapter book area that we created
  37| for emergent readers has provided an opportunity for these readers to easily located items that
  38| are geared for their reading level and therefor, allow them to be successful with their reading
  39| choices.   The non-fiction area for preschool aged children is heavily used and we are continually
  40| adding new items.  Nonfiction books give kids a chance to learn new concepts and vocabulary,
  41| as well as broaden their view of the world.  Educators and publishers are recognizing that this

===PAGE 75=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 75
   2| interest begins at a very early age for many children; therefore, the book options are varied and
   3| well presented.
   4| We are constantly evaluating our toy/manipulative collection to keep it current, stimulating and
   5| in good shape.  We are fortunate to have had many quality items donated for our use.  Toys are
   6| more than just fun and games for kids.  Playing with toys engages a child’s senses, sparks their
   7| imaginations and encourages them to interact with others.
   8| Lastly, a shout out to parents and caregivers who faithfully bring their children to our monthly
   9| S T E M Saturdays.  Saturday is a busy time for these families yet they recognize the value of
  10| this opportunity to engage their children in fun, educational activities in the fields of Science,
  11| Technology, Engineering and Math.
  12| Every child should be familiar and comfortable with the local library and possess the skills to
  13| find their way around libraries in general.  To this end, the goals for Children’s Services for
  14| FY2020 are:
  15|  To continue to develop opportunities via passive and active programs designed to
  16| encourage children to gain independence and confidence as they explore the many facets
  17| of our collection.
  18|  To provide children with open access to all digital resources and media and to ensure that
  19| we have the most up-to-date technology that we can afford for their use.

  21| updated collection for children of all ages and to optimize shelving of this  collection for
  22| ease of access
  23|  To provide an exciting, stimulating fiction collection for children of all ages to encourage
  24| a life-long love of reading.
  25| Teen Services:
  26| The teen room saw increased summer reading attendance, new programs, and was redesigned in
  27| 2018.  The amount of hours read for the 2018 Summer Reading Program increased from 447
  28| hours read in 2017 to a whopping 4,163.5 hours read!  Program participation ran strong with 188
  29| tweens and teens attending 11 programs.
  30| A teen Dungeon’s and Dragon’s club was started in September to coincide with the start of
  31| school.  While there is only one official library D&D night, the group of 14 meet twice a week in
  32| the teen room to play their various campaigns, trading off the Dungeon Master (leader) position.
  33| Sessions range from as small as 4 players to as large as 14 depending on the teens’ availability
  34| that day.  This program has been immensely popular and continues to grow.
  35| The teen room has been updated and refurnished to make the space meet the needs of today’s
  36| teens.  White board table with outlets to charge their phone was added in addition to relocating
  37| the TV to have a designated video game area where more teens could comfortably gather.  New
  38| shelving for both books and board games was added as well.

===PAGE 76=== (plain, 28 lines)
   1| 76
   2| To continue the momentum, in the coming year the focus will be on getting the teens to buy into
   3| their new space and truly make it theirs. To accomplish this, a teen advisory group will be
   4| created.  As part of this group teens will create and lead programming that they are excited about
   5| and provide feedback on what they want going forward.  Other goals include increasing
   6| volunteer opportunities to meet the current demand and shifting the summer reading program
   7| away from the traditional model to the summer learning model.  To meet these goals beginning
   8| this summer program priority will be given to programs that encourage team work, collaboration,
   9| and incorporate learning a new skill or honing existing ones.  Beginning in the fall, a monthly
  10| community service program will be introduced with teens giving back to the community.  This
  11| will be done in a fun way through crafts, cards, and incorporating a service component to
  12| learning new skills.
