   2| LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC)
   3| FY1
   4| 8
   5| Mission: Develop, modernize, establish and operate the
   6| Town of Lunenburg’s Public and Government (P&G) Access
   7| Channels to reflect what matters to Lunenburg
   8| Public Access Cable Committee (PACC)
   9| Steve Walker, Chair
  10| Faith Beall, Vice Chair
  11| Jeff Bajko, Secretary
  12| The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage
  13| the Public Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town’s only cable-
  14| service provider.
  15| The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-
  16| broadcast programming from both town functions and meetings, as well as programming from publicly
  17| available sources.
  18| Programming
  19| CH9 - Public
  20| Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 57% of the local programming on Ch9 in FY18, down 18% from
  21| 75% in FY17.  The decrease in local programming was largely due to the lack of volunteers and paid
  22| videographers.
  23| Local programs included high school sports, band concerts and school events, just to name a few.  The
  24| remaining 43% of programming was obtained through other media sources (Access Share) and online
  25| downloads (MassAccess and PEGMedia).

===PAGE 78=== (layout, 23 lines)
   1|                                 Channel 9 – Public Channel
   2|  Several categories of local programming were provided on Ch9, among the top were Infor-
   3|  mational (43%), Entertainment (17%) and Events (17%).   As the top programming category,
   4|  Informational provided subscribers with unique presentations and overviews that helped
   5|  to familiarizes them with local resources and information.
   6|  All locally produced programming is also accessible on the Public Access YouTube Channel
   7|  and Facebook                                http://www.youtube.com/LunenburgAccess
   8|                             https://www.facebook.com/Lunenburg-Public-Access-Cable
   9|  CH8 - Government
  10|  Most content on the Government Channel (Ch8) is locally produced.  In addition to several
  11|  live weekly municipal meetings and re-broadcasts, other examples of government pro-
  12|  grams include committee/department Public Service Announcements (PSA).  One such PSA
  13|  is for the promotion of the Council on Aging’s Eagle House and their services to the Senior
  14|  community.  Each year a new PSA is created to showcase activities and events that encour-
  15|  age new members to join.  This is just one example of many, which also include past PSAs
  16|  created for the Fire Department and Safety Center.  Although, both are municipal depart-
  17|  ments (Government), these promotions have aired on the Public Channel 9 to reach a wid-
  18|  er audience.
  19|  Government programming increased in FY18 through additional monthly recordings of
  20|  School Committee and Finance Committee meetings.  In addition, Conservation Committee,
  21|  Open Space Committee, and other state sourced programs, such as “State Matters”, were
  22|  added to the monthly broadcasts.
  23|                                                        78

===PAGE 79=== (layout, 27 lines)
   1|  All recorded Government meetings are accessible on the Public Access YouTube channel.
   2|                             http://www.youtube.com/LunenburgAccess
   3|  Town committees/departments are always encouraged to take advantage of PAC to im-
   4|  prove town government to community information flow.
   5|  Contract Renewal
   6|  Contract negotiations with Comcast began in FY18 to renew the agreement between Com-
   7|  cast and the Town of Lunenburg commencing on March 1, 2018 through February FY28.
   8|  The Cable Committee, Tom Alonzo, Steve Walker, Paula Bertram, advised by IT Director
   9|  Steve Malandrinos and Town Manager Heather Lemieux, worked with KP Law to reach a
  10|  mutually acceptable contract with Comcast. The result of the negotiations, finalized in June
  11|  of FY18, brought an increase in franchise fees from 2.5% to 5.0%, and a capital infusion of
  12|  $170,000 over 3 years.   The first of these payments of $85,000 was made on June 27, 2018.
  13|  The next 2 payments will be $42,500 in FY19 and FY20 respectively.  Capital payments are
  14|  being held in an Undesignated Fund (Comcast Tech Capital Grant) revenue account for fu-
  15|  ture capital use.  Additionally, it was agreed to provide Lunenburg with a 3rd Channel exclu-
  16|  sively for educational content to be available within 24 months of the contract effective
  17|  date.
  18|  PACC reserved the Comcast infused capital of $85,000, along with the $39,796.01 already in
  19|  the Undesignated Fund from 2008, for a total of $124,796.01.  These monies can be used
  20|  for a future studio and various other capital equipment needs.
  21|  In FY20 the PAC financial structure is planned to change to an enterprise fund for all reve-
  22|  nue and expense related activity.
  23|  Legal Expenses related to contract negotiations accounted for 11% of the FY18 expenses.
  24|  Studio and Equipment Upgrades
  25|  PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013.  We encourage the public to
  26|  take advantage of the facilities and equipment offered.
  27|                                                         79

===PAGE 80=== (plain, 31 lines)
   1| 80
   2| The strategy for the PAC studio in the upcoming fiscal years includes acquiring new studio
   3| space as soon as municipal space becomes available.  A new studio would provide a more
   4| permanent and comfortable environment for producing programming.  Space is not ex-
   5| pected prior to FY21.
   6| Upgrades included portable equipment to improve remote multi-camera live (and non-
   7| live) broadcasting in FY18.  The addition of a custom-built transmitter and switcher system
   8| (3CPU) allowed a converter to transfer a live signal through the internet to the CASTUS
   9| server and out to the public on Ch8 or Ch9.  Live broadcast feeds are now possible from
  10| the PAC studio, the LMHS Auditorium, and the Public Safety Building.
  11| This system was also designed to produce recorded (non-live) high quality multi-camera
  12| remote productions as well.  “Are You Smarter than Our Fifth Graders” was the first show
  13| recorded with the 3CPU at the LHS auditorium in the Spring of FY18. And, the next month,
  14| in May, the system was first used to record a multi-camera production of the Annual
  15| Town Meeting.
  16| Other equipment upgrades and additions for FY19 will include new cameras to loan out
  17| for public use.  PAC currently has 3 loaner cameras available for use in producing local
  18| programming. The community is encouraged to borrow PAC equipment or submit their
  19| own video for Ch9 content.
  20| Improvements in live broadcasting at Town Hall included the addition of wireless micro-
  21| phones for meeting use.  These microphones made it possible to record boards/
  22| committees located at the conference table in the Bilotta Room.  An addition of 4 more
  23| wireless microphones are planned for FY19.
  24| Equipment upgrade accounted for 17% of the FY18 expenses.
  25| Staff, Producers and Volunteers
  26| In FY18, there was one full-time Program Coordinator and four part-time staff, classified
  27| as Vendors (Budget Line Item: “Purchase of Services”).  The Program Coordinator was re-
  28| classified to Public Access Manager in FY18, leaving the Program Coordinator position
  29| open.  The Manager continues to administer the day-to-day operation of the organization,
  30| including all production and programming schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and ser-
  31| vices procurement, studio and inventory administration, advertising, and

===PAGE 81=== (layout, 25 lines)
   1|  social media. Vendors (videographers) provided video recording of committee meetings
   2|  and additional video assignments, as needed.
   3|  A technical contract consultant was added in FY18 to help with studio and production
   4|  needs.  The consultant worked with the PAC manager to upgrade the studio, as well as
   5|  build a remote multi-camera system.  He also provided technical and training assistance as
   6|  needed.  It is expected that he will remain working for PAC through FY19.  However, PAC
   7|  will be searching for a permanent part-time Production Technician to add to the staff in
   8|  FY19.
   9|  Of the 57% of locally produced shows on Ch9, most were produced by public access staff.
  10|  Our resident top producer, Pete Lincoln of “Talkin’ Baseball”, continued to create new pro-
  11|  grams for airing on Ch9 through FY18.  St. Boniface congregants also produce a weekly
  12|  mass program, on-air now for over two years.
  13|  Getting volunteers has been a great effort, with little response. Social media and newspa-
  14|  pers have been used to promote volunteerism with little effect.  PAC will continue to look
  15|  for other ways to reach out to the community for volunteers.
  16|  Staff (Salaries, Health Insurance, Purchase of Services) accounted for 64% of the FY18 ex-
  17|  penses.
  18|  Financials
  19|  PAC received all of its income from Comcast in quarterly payments (received August, No-
  20|  vember, February and May). The overall payments received for FY18 were $111,045.25.
  21|  These payments were deposited to the PAC Revenue Fund, and the Operating Budget was
  22|  set at $162,800 for projected expenses and capital costs.
  23|  The starting balance in the Revenue Fund for PAC in FY18 was $ 303,551.54 with a budget-
  24|  ed amount of $162,800.

   7|   Summary
   8|   The support of the local community has helped Lunenburg Public Access (PAC) succeed.  PAC has received
   9|   numerous requests for recording, as well as positive viewer feedback.  People are watching.   In fact, the
  10|   viewership of the Government Channel (Ch8) alone, on-air and YouTube, shows a solid commitment to Public
  11|   Access as a valuable resource for local information.
  12|   The Public channel (Ch9) added new and unique programming for all to enjoy as well.  While local program-
  13|   ming on Ch9 decreased in FY18, it is expected to increase in FY19 with the addition of videographers and stu-
  14|   dio productions.  But we can’t do this alone.  Volunteers and community involvement is necessary for PAC to
  15|   grow.  Individuals are urged to bring their ideas and talent forward to help create programming.  Training and
  16|   guidance is provided for those who are interested.
  17|   With the infusion of capital from the Comcast contract, PAC can now make necessary improvements moving
  18|   forward.  It will allow for the addition of more videographers and the capital equipment necessary to in-
  19|   crease quality and content. Better technical support will also be possible with the hiring of a Production Engi-
  20|   neer in FY19.
  21|   PAC is proud of the accomplishments made in FY18.  However, there is much more work to be done.  Admin-
  22|   istrative and production needs are continually increasing, videographer demands are great, and technical
  23|   requirements are always at the forefront. Careful planning and budgeting has lead to success this year, as
  24|   well as the solid commitment to Lunenburg.
  25|   PAC’s promise, as expressed in the mission statement, sums it up best: “To Develop, modernize, establish
  26|   and operate the Town of Lunenburg’s Public and Government (P&G) Access Channels to reflect what matters
  27|   to Lunenburg.”

===PAGE 84=== (layout, 38 lines)
   1|                                SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   2|                                                          Est. 1998
   3|                          CALENDAR YEAR JANUARY 1, 2018 – NOVEMBER 1, 2018
   4|  The Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a five-member committee appointed by the Lunenburg
   5|  Board of Selectmen.
   6|  2018 Committee Members:
   7|  *Sheila Craigen
   8|  *Susan Doherty
   9|  *Nancy Forest
  10|  *Robert Hamel
  11|  *Myleen Mallari
  12|  The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off  Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an
  13|  opportunity to receive up to $1,000 off of their property tax bill in the calendar year rather than the
  14|  fiscal year.  Participants worked in various town departments to earn the tax abatement doing general
  15|  office duties, custodial, library shelving, Meals on Wheels, and kitchen assistance.
  16|  For each hour worked a person received $10 and worked up to 100 hours between January 1, 2018
  17|  and November 1, 2018 to receive the abatement.  Any hours worked beyond that were considered vol-
  18|  unteer hours.
  19|  There were eight Lunenburg residents who participated between January 1, 2018- November 1, 2018.
  20|  These individuals, as a whole, accumulated 771.25 hours ($7,712.50) towards their abatements.
  21|  Eligibility Requirements:
  22|  *Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  23|  *Own and occupy a home in Lunenburg as their primary residence for at least one year
  24|  *Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse’s name who is also 60 years of age or older
  25|  *Meet the income guidelines of :  One person household - $40,000 / Two person household - $60,000
  26|  *All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check and must re- apply and be accept-
  27|  ed annually for participation in the program.
  28|  Earnings are exempt from state taxes only.  Earnings are subject to federal and FICA tax withholdings
  29|  and are also subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Re-
  30|  gional Retirement or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement.
  31|  Departments who participated between January 1, 2018– November 1, 2018:
  32|  *Lunenburg Treasurer/Tax Collector
  33|  *Lunenburg Board of Health
  34|  *Lunenburg Building Department/Zoning
  35|  *Lunenburg Council on Aging
  36|  *Lunenburg DPW/Sewer Departments
  37|  *Lunenburg Public Library

===PAGE 85=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                                                                           SEWER COMMISSION - FY18
   2|                              The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town
   3|                              Meeting on May 6, 2006. At the May 20, 2017 election, Joe Anderson and Joan Pease
   4|                              were re-elected to 3 year terms and Troy Daniels was elected to the one-year term as a
   5|                              write-in to fill out the 3-year term vacated by Richard St. Cyr who resigned from the
   6|                              Commission in February of 2017. Usually held the first Sewer Commission meeting after
   7|                              the May Town election when the Commission elects the officers for the new fiscal year,
   8|                              the election of officers for FY 18 did not occur until August 2017 due to Charter discus-
   9|                              sions and meeting attendance. On August 15, 2017, Joe Anderson was elected as Chair-
  10|                              man, Carl Luck as Vice-Chairman, and John Reynolds to serve as Clerk. Troy Daniels
  11|                              chose not to run for re-election in May 2018 and Ryan Stober was elected to serve on the
  12|                              Sewer Commission in May 2018. We welcome Ryan and thank him for his willingness
  13|                              to serve on this Commission.
  14|                             The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise
  15|                             Fund, planning for the future, and developing and implementing rules, regulations and
  16|                             policies. Under the Town's Charter, responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and
  17|                              maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager who
  18|                              delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain the operational side of the system to
  19|                              the Department of Public Works' Director, Jack Rodriquenz. The Town of Lunenburg
  20|                              does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 74% of Lunenburg sewer is dis-
  21|                             charged to the City of Leominster, and the other 26% is discharged to the City of Fitch-
  22|                              The Sewer Commission continued contracting with Small Water Systems Services
  23|                              (SWSS) for the Operations and Management of the 10 pumping stations, emergency
  24|                              generators and sewer infrastructure. The Sewer Commission has been very satisfied
  25|                              with the personnel, the work and the arrangement with SWSS, renewing the contract
  26|                             for a second three-year term in July 2016. In FY18, we completed the 5th year with
  27|                             Small Water as our contractor for the supervision of the town's sewer system.
  28|                              There are currently 882 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 374
  29|                             which have access to Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY 18, 27 new ac-
  30|                              counts connected to Town Sewer. The Commission billed out $792,898 in quarterly
  31|                             sewer bills, and had $61,985 of prior year delinquencies added to the owners' property
  32|                              tax bills for non-payment. Six properties were assessed a privilege fee in FY18. One
  33|                              property assessed was residential. Three assessments were for 24-unit apartment build-
  34|                             ings, and 2 other properties were commercial buildings. Privilege fees help to pay down
  35|                             the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer project.
  36|                              Throughout FY18, the Commission worked on several policies, including Grease Trap reg-
  37|                              ulations, Connection Charges, and Irrigation and Manual meter policies. The Commission
  38|                              worked with several vendors in preparation for upgrading the irrigation meters currently in
  39|                              use. Current technology would allow the meters to be read by radio or cellular commu-
  40|                              nication. Investigations are still underway to find the best fit for our customers while try-
  41|                             ing to keep replacement costs low as well.
  42|                             In July of 2017, the Commission began a very satisfactory relationship with the new Town
  43|                              Counsel, Mead, Talerman and Costa and specifically Adam Costa, who has been of great
  44|                              assistance to the Commission throughout the year. He was especially helpful during
  45|                             FY18 as the Commission grew more familiar with the State's Open Meeting Law and as-
  46|                              sisted in the work to establish Communication guidelines. The Sewer Commission contin-
  47|                             ued its work in FY18 to identify and reduce Inflow and Infiltration (I/I), working with the
  48|                              engineers at Weston & Sampson. In December 2017, with their assistance, the
  49|                                                                                               85

===PAGE 86=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                            Sewer Commission filed their "Infiltration/Inflow Control Plan" with the State as required
   2|                            by the Department of Environmental Protection. The Commission worked with Weston &
   3|                            Sampson to accomplish the 2018 manhole rehabilitation project in the hopes to decrease I/I
   4|                            where there were known issues. Reducing I/I continues to be the focus of the Commission
   5|                            going into FY19.
   6|                            The Commission has continued with the annual permit program for the 20 Food Service
   7|                            Establishments with grease traps and interceptors on Town Sewer. Throughout FY 18,
   8|                            the Commission worked with multiple establishments in correcting their non-compliance.
   9|                            The "Rules & Regulations for Oil & Grease Traps/Interceptors for Food Establishments"
  10|                            continues to be a focus of the Commission as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negative-
  11|                            ly affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the sewer system. While the
  12|                            Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public disposal, individual home
  13|                            owners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which increas-
  14|                            es everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the
  15|                            Throughout FY 18 the Commission met with several different groups regarding Sewer Ex-
  16|                            tensions, specifically at Cross Rd, Lancaster, Upland, and Tilton Ave. The Commission is
  17|                            always willing to discuss a potential sewer extension when the applicant can show that
  18|                            challenges exist for standard septic systems.
  19|                            At the May 2018 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 3 articles, 2 of which related
  20|                            to the FY19 budget and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY19,
  21|                            $705,301.08 was appropriated from Sewer Enterprise revenues and the sum of
  22|                            $156,869.92 was appropriated from Retained Earnings for a total appropriation of $
  23|                            $862,171.00 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY19. The amount of $883,369.58
  24|                            was approved to cover both the Enterprise Fund's share of Town services and debt service
  25|                            on the sewer betterments. The second article transferred monies to the Capital Reserve
  26|                            Stabilization Fund in the amount of $29,000, approximately 3% of proposed revenues as
  27|                            the Commission had done for FY 18. This fund is to be used to maintain and improve the
  28|                            operating efficiency of the system or cover large emergency expenditures. A main goal of
  29|                            the Sewer Commission continues to be maintaining a balanced budget every year without
  30|                            having to raise rates for our customers. This is the fifth year that the operating budget has been
  31|                            balanced without a rate increase to the users. The third warrant article was to add Tilton Ave-
  32|                            nue to the Sewer Service AreaSSA) for future sewer extension requests, having been
  33|                            omitted when the Sewer Bylaw and original Sewer Service Area map was accepted by the
  34|                            Town at the May 2009 Town Meeting. All three articles passed at Town Meeting and
  35|                            Tilton Ave. was added to the Sewer Service Area. It was determined at the time of this
  36|                            Annual Town Meeting that any contributions to Stabilization Funds based on income re-
  37|                            ceived for their purposes would be acted on at the Special Town Meeting following the
  38|                            close of the fiscal year. The two funds affected by this change are the Sewer Bank-Inflow/
  39|                            Infiltration Stabilization Fund and the Reserve Capacity Stabilization Fund. A portion of
  40|                            the connection charges assessed at the time of an application for permit to connect go into
  41|                            these accounts and target resolving 1/1 issues and future capacity costs paid to the cities of
  42|                            Fitchburg and Leominster for increased capacities.
  43|                            During FY18, the Commission welcomed Jane Ranley as a part-time employee re-
  44|                            sponsible for the minutes of Sewer Commission meetings. She has been of great assis-
  45|                            tance to the Commission on providing accurate and timely minutes of the meetings.
  46|                            The Commission would like to thank Troy Daniels for his service on the Commission
  47|                            and good luck as he and Bob Ebersole leave Lunenburg for a new adventure. Bob not
  48|                            only served as a past Chairman of the Commission but also as the liaison with the Se-
  49|                            lectmen while he served on that board.
  50|                                                                                               86

   1|                                                 BOARD OF HEALTH
   2|  The Board of Health Office is located in the old Ritter Library building on the corner of Mass. Ave
   3|  and Leominster Road.  Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.  We can be
   4|  reached at 978-345-4146, extension 430.
   5|  The Board of Health, with the assistance of its agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, con-
   6|  tinued  its mission to maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2018, reviewing Title
   7|  V Inspection Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing in-
   8|  spections, complaints and general public health related issues as well as resident concerns.
   9|  The Board of Health oversees the Septic System Repair Program which helps low to moderate income
  10|  families replace failed septic systems or connect to public sewer.  Anyone interested in finding out
  11|  more about this program can contact the Board of Health office.
  12|  Homeland Security planning is ongoing.  Meetings continue between our agents, other Town Depart-
  13|  ments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner towns in our
  14|  region.
  15|  This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new
  16|  wells, repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits.  The Board continues its vigilance with
  17|  regard to septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its gen-
  18|  eral mandate to protect the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  19|  The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the
  20|  Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous clinics are
  21|  provided throughout the year to our residents.  These clinics provide inoculations, including Flu Shots,
  22|  Blood Pressure screenings, Well Oldster Clinics and Dental screening. Fluoride treatment and dental
  23|  checks of our youngsters in the School System are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.
  24|  The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project.  Mosquito Con-
  25|  trol monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as EEE
  26|  and West Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer times of the
  27|  year.      Residents   who   want   to   have   their   property   sprayed   can   do   so   by   emailing   to:
  28|  cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a  request on their webpage  at:  http://
  29|  www.cmmcp.org , or calling the Board of Health office.  This service is free of charge to residents.
  30|  With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to ill residents recovering
  31|  from illness and hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  32|  Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
  33|  Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the Citi-
  34|  zens of Lunenburg.
  35|  If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the Board
  36|  office at 582-4146 ext. 430.

   5|                                                          The Nashoba sanitarian is generally scheduled to be available for the
   6|                                   public twice a week on Monday and Wednesday mornings at the Lunenburg Board of
   7|                                   Health Office. Other meetings occur informally.
   8|                                               This does not reflect the daily calls handled by the three Nashoba secretar-
   9|                                   ies during daily business hours.
  10|                        Food Service Licenses & Inspections.....................………36
  11|                                   Nashoba annually mails out and receives application from both restaurants and retail
  12|                        food businesses.  Licenses are renewed for existing facilities.  Plans are submitted and re-
  13|                        viewed prior to initial licensing. Most licensees are inspected at a minimum twice a year.
  14|                        Where deficiencies are found, a re-inspection is scheduled to insure compliance.  When a
  15|                        complaint from the public is received an inspection is also conducted. During this inspection
  16|                        health factors of food handlers is also investigated, and where appropriate medical consulta-
  17|                        tion and laboratory testing may be required.
  18|                        Beach/Pool/Camp Inspections.................................................. 20
  19|                                   Camps are inspected at least annually at opening for compliance with State Sanitary
  20|                        Code, Chapter IV, 105CMR430.00.  Public swimming beaches are sampled for water quality
  21|                        weekly during the summer operation and more often if a problem is suspected.   Semi-public pool
  22|                        are permitted and inspected
  23|                        Housing & Nuisance Investigations...................................7
  24|                                   Nashoba, as agent for the local Board of Health, inspects dwellings for conformance
  25|                        with State Sanitary Code, 105 CMR 410.00, issues orders for correction, and re-inspect for com-
  26|                        pliance.  Similarly, complaints from residents concerning unsanitary conditions or pollution
  27|                        events are investigated.
  28|                        Septic System Test Applications.......................................39
  29|                                   Applications from residents proposing to build or upgrade a septic system are accepted,
  30|                        a file created, and testing dates are coordinated with the applicants engineer.
  31|                                   Septic System Lot Tests........................................112
  32|                                               Nashoba sanitarian witnesses soil evaluations, deep hole tests, and, usually on a
  33|                                   separate date, percolation tests conducted by the applicant’s engineer which serve as the
  34|                                   basis of the design of the septic system.
  35|                        Septic System Plan Applications…………………………40
  36|                                   Detailed plans created by engineers hired by residents proposing to build or upgrade a
  37|                        septic system are received, filed, and routed to the Nashoba sanitarian for review.

   7|               Community Health Nursing
   8|                          Nashoba’s Community Health Nursing program provides an essential public health service to it
   9|                          member communities guided by the American Nurses Association (ANA) 16 Standards of Pub-
  10|                          lic Nursing Practice.
  11|                          Our staff serves as a resource to the community, addressing questions and concerns from the
  12|                          public on a variety of health and safety issues (immunizations, head lice, chronic disease man-
  13|                          agement, safe drug/sharps disposal etc.) in a variety of ways.
  14|                                We conduct regular well-being clinics for health assessment, screenings and education to all,
  15|                                      especially the underserved and at-risk populations.
  16|                                We report and investigate communicable diseases to formulate and implement control
  17|                                      guidelines and strategies that protect the health of individual and the community at large
  18|                                      (mandated by the Massachusetts Department of Public Health).
  19|                                We make will-being visits to assess needs, coordinate appropriate care and services and pro-
  20|                                      vide case management as needed (health promotion).
  21|                                We provide public health education.
  22|                                We address psych-social issues that may impact general health and safety (i.e. hoarding).
  23|                                We are available to collaborate with all municipal staff to address public health nursing
  24|                                      questions, work closely with Councils on Aging to assist elders in the communities and
  25|                                      are available for consultation with school nurses caring for children and families.  The
  26|                                      nursing staff also works with the Sanitarian for your community, as needed, to address
  27|                                      issues of unhealthy living conditions.
  28|                          Listed below is summary of the activities of the Community Health Nursing program.
  29|                          Nashoba conducted 12 clinics and those clinics offered your citizens: blood pressure screening,
  30|                          flu shots and an opportunity to consult with the nurse to address questions.
  31|                          Our staff conducted 5 health promotion/well-being/social worker visits in your communities;
  32|                          there were 112 direct observation therapy visits provided in addition to the health promotional
  33|                          visits.
  34|                          We administered 38 flu shots through our annual clinics.

===PAGE 96=== (plain, 40 lines)
   1| 96
   2| The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated
   3| to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens
   4| of the Town of Lunenburg.
   5| In 2018 your Fire Department responded to 1942 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   6| Fires & Hazardous Conditions                     452                Emergency Medical      982
   7| Inspections                  506                 Other                             33
   8| 2018 saw one of the greatest changes in the history of the Lunenburg Fire Department with
   9| the achievement of 24 hour staffing in the Station.  On August 6 th, 2018 Firefighters Timothy Downey
  10| and Austin Flagg were promoted to Career Firefighters and joined the existing 4 members as Full Time
  11| Staff.
  12| Current Fire Department staffing is now 7 Career and 40-45 On Call Firefighters and Emergen-
  13| cy Medical Technicians, plus 1 full time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator.  The Station is
  14| manned 7 days per week with 2 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians, plus a 3 rd Firefighter/
  15| EMT Monday-Friday from 8am -4pm.  This coverage is a combination of Full Time personnel in con-
  16| junction with 88 hours per week of per diem coverage.  The per diem coverage is provided by our Call
  17| Firefighter/EMT’s manning the Station at their hourly rate.
  18| The call volume in 2018 was a slight overall decrease over 2017, however we.  We also expe-
  19| rienced a 6% increase in Emergency Medical related calls, which is consistent with the growth we
  20| have seen over the last 10 years.
  21| We currently operate two Ambulances at the Advanced EMT Level, but must rely on outside
  22| agencies for assistance with full Advanced Life Support (Paramedic Level) Care.  The first steps to-
  23| wards achieving our own Paramedic service were taken in 2018 with FF Downey beginning training as
  24| a Paramedic.  It is planned that FF Flagg will begin the training in 2019.  These two members, plus ad-
  25| dition of 3 more Firefighter/Paramedics over the next 3-4 years will allow us to provide service to the
  26| community at the full Advanced Life Support Level.  Hiring Paramedic Level trained Firefighters and
  27| equipping the ambulances to that level will bring a higher level of care to our residents in a faster,
  28| more efficient way.
  29| Though we have achieved 24-hour coverage in the Station, many EMS calls and nearly all fire
  30| calls still require more personnel than are on duty in the Station.  This means we still rely heavily on
  31| the dedication and commitment of our Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians.  Our Call
  32| personnel remain, and will remain for the foreseeable future a vital part of our emergency response.

  34| FIRE DEPARTMENT

===PAGE 97=== (layout, 43 lines)
   1|            A new Call Firefighter/EMT must attend over 340 hours of initial training, plus additional
   2|  time in orientation and field training.  Members qualifying to drive Fire Apparatus must complete an
   3|  additional 65 hours of classroom and practical training.  On average, it takes a new member one and
   4|  a half to two years to properly complete these requirements.  Unfortunately, for a variety of reasons
   5|  we often do not retain members for more than 5-6 years.
   6|            In 2018, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 506 Inspections.   This is in addition to multi-
   7|  ple fire drills and public education initiatives.  By law, we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon
   8|  Monoxide Detector installations when a home is sold, as well as all new oil burner and propane gas
   9|  installations.  Any business holding a liquor license is also required to be inspected for fire safety
  10|  issues as well.  In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper
  11|  fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process.  Personnel also con-
  12|  tinue to provide fire safety education to not only children in our schools but adults as well.    Other
  13|  Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2017 include:
  14|            Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with
  15|  Fire Safety Education in both the Schools and with Senior Citizens.  This Grant provides funding for
  16|  both materials and personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations.
  17|  Highlights of activity through this grant include:
  18|            Educational programs in basic fire safety in Grades K-3, including use of the Regional Fire
  19|  Safety Trailer.
  20|            Educational programs in conjunction with the Council on Aging.  These included cooking and
  21|  heating safety, a CPR Demonstration and Ice safety.
  22|            Participation in the Senior Center’s           “Sand for Seniors”       Program.  This program provides
  23|  buckets of walkway sand for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the win-
  24|  ter months.  In partnership with this program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents
  25|  and when possible, inspects their homes for Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detectors.  If the resident
  26|  was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is able to install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE
  27|  Grant.
  28|            It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or non-
  29|  existent smoke detectors.
  30|            During 2018, Call Firefighters Robert Barrett, Melissa Buchanan, Ronald Hyatt and Lorenzo
  31|  Leonardo graduated from the Massachusetts Firefighting Academy’s Call/Volunteer Training Pro-
  32|  gram.  This intensive 4-month program provides the basic skills firefighters need to operate in to-
  33|  day’s challenging environments.  All newly hired Firefighters are required to complete this program
  34|  within one year of hire.  In addition, Firefighter Barrett received the Martin McNamara Award for
  35|  Outstanding Achievement in his Recruit Class.  This award, named after a Lancaster Firefighter killed
  36|  in the Line of Duty is award to the overall top student in each Call/Volunteer Recruit Class.
  37|            I would also recognize Call Firefighter Timothy Paton.  Tim retired from the Department,
  38|  after 34 years of service to the community.
  39|  Apparatus replacement continues to be a concern.   The replacement for our 30-year-old Ladder
  40|  truck has been ordered and is expected in the late summer or early fall of 2019.  This unit is ex-
  41|  pected to serve the town for 25+ years.  The next oldest unit in the fleet, Engine 4 has been sub-
  42|  mitted for replacement on the FY’20 Capital Plan.
  43|                                                               97

===PAGE 98=== (plain, 43 lines)
   1| 98
   2| This 1995 vintage unit was our primary pumper from its delivery up until 2015.  It has served
   3| the town well but has seen increasing maintenance costs and downtime due to wear and tear.  In
   4| late 2018 the unit ’s pump failed, necessitating costly repairs just to return it to operational condi-
   5| tion.  Rescue 2, a 2008 Ambulance has also been submitted for replacement as it is near the end of
   6| its projected life span.  The high reliability required of front line emergency apparatus becomes in-
   7| creasingly difficult to maintain as these units reach the end of their life span.   It hoped that due con-
   8| sideration will be given to replacing both units.
   9| In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
  10| outstanding commitment to the Town.  Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue
  11| to impress me every day.  I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards and com-
  12| missions for their continuing help.  Most people do not realize the level of interdepartmental coop-
  13| eration that occurs in the daily course of town business.  My thanks also go out to the citizens of
  14| Lunenburg for their ongoing support of public safety.  It is that support that allows us to continue
  15| providing the services you expect from us.

   1|                                                With Much Appreciation
   2|                                                            Lt. Michael Luth
   3|                                                     Officer Whitney Mansfield
   4|  Lt. Mike Luth began his career in law enforcement with the Town of Tyngsboro as a patrol officer. He was
   5|  soon elevated to the rank of Sergeant. Mike left the Tyngsboro Police Department to take a job with the Se-
   6|  cret Service during the Clinton and Bush administrations. Having a passion for small town policing Mike
   7|  settled in Lunenburg taking a patrol officer position in 2004. Mike was promoted to the position of Patrol Ser-
   8|  geant and eventually to Administrative Lieutenant and second in command. He commanded many positions
   9|  including the Court Liaison officer, training coordinator, public records and liaison to the Council on aging
  10|  where he taught self-defense and Senior drivers just to name a few. Mike was responsible for organizing the
  11|  Citizen’s Police academy, and managed the College Internship programs. Mike left the Lunenburg Police De-
  12|  partment when he accepted the Position of Chief of Police in the Town of Groton, where he currently resides.
  13|  Mike will be missed around the Police Station, but we are extremely proud of his accomplishments and we
  14|  wish Chief Mike Luth the best of luck.
  15|  Whitney Mansfield was first introduced to the Lunenburg Police Department as a Fitchburg State University
  16|  Criminal Justice Intern. Whitney was immediately found to be highly motivated, kindhearted individual with a
  17|  high degree of integrity, and one who had a keen interest in Law Enforcement. She was immediately taken in
  18|  by the organization and offered a sponsorship to the Reserve Intermittent Police Academy. Whitney attended
  19|  the academy and was quickly offered a position within our Reserve ranks. She was appointed as a Reserve
  20|  officer shortly after graduating from the academy in July of 2017. Whitney quickly became an outstanding
  21|  candidate for a position as a full time police officer. Whitney applied and received a full time position with the
  22|  Bedford New Hampshire Police Department and is currently in training. Whitney will make of fine police
  23|  officer. She will certainly be sorely missed but we are very proud of her and wish her the best of luck with her
  24|  new career.
  25|                                            About the Police Department
  26|  The Police Department is in operation 24 hours a day 7 days a week.  The department is staffed with the Chief
  27|  of Police supported by the Public Safety Coordinator, and one Administrative Lieutenant. Three Sergeants/
  28|  Shift commanders, one of the Sergeants presently assigned to K-9/Traffic enforcement, nine Patrol Officers
  29|  and 11 Reserve Intermittent Patrol Officers make up the patrol unit. Supporting the patrol unit is the Criminal
  30|  Investigation Unit headed by a Detective Sergeant. Within the C.I.U. is the School Resource Officer who is also
  31|  assigned to Community Outreach, and who is accompanied by Hank the Therapy dog. The Lieutenant and
  32|  Public Safety Coordinator are on duty Monday thru Friday between the hours of 7Am and 3Pm. Office hours
  33|  for the Chief of Police are 8Am to 4Pm Monday thru Friday.
  34|  The Animal Control Officer responds to domestic animal calls on a 24 hour 7 day per week basis. The public is
  35|  reminded to report any animal related complaints to the Regional Call Center at (978) 582-4531. For emer-
  36|  gencies, dial 911. More information can be found on the Animal Control Web site at lunenburgma.gov.
  37|  Dogs must be licensed by April 1st annually. Dogs must have a current Rabies certificate to qualify for licens-
  38|  ing. This can be done at the Clerk's office in Town Hall, 17 Main Street Lunenburg, MA 01462. Unspayed Fe-
  39|  male/Unaltered Male $20 fee. Spayed Female/Altered Male $5 fee.  Late Fee: $25
  40|  The front desk is open Monday thru Friday from 7AM to 7PM.  Emergency, Non-emergency and 911 calls are
  41|  routed through Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District, (NVRDD).                   For all emergencies, dial
  42|  911. For general calls, please call (978) 582          -4531.
  43|  Applications for Firearms Licensing can be found on the Police Department Website at lunenburgma.gov.
  44|  Contact (978) 582-4531 for an appointment.
  45|                                                                    101

===PAGE 102=== (layout, 45 lines)
   1|                                         The State of the Police Department
   2|   The Police responded to 19383 calls for service this year, a 13.7% increase over last year; responded to 315
   3|   motor vehicle accidents, investigated 1070 incidents, 47 Field Interview Reports, wrote 1889 motor vehicle
   4|   citations, arrested 144 people, summonsed 207, and placed 8 people in protective custody. The K-9 was de-
   5|   ployed 27 times.  In the first half of fiscal 2017, staff assignments had to be increased 75 times to effectively
   6|   meet service demands.
   7|   The day shift desk officer answered 1741 calls and took 1407 lobby inquiries and complaints between July and
   8|   December of 2018. The day shift desk officer wrote 94 incident reports in 2018.  This year the day shift desk
   9|   watch officer was responsible for conducting suicide watch and assisting with the court processing of 83 de-
  10|   tainees; either taken into custody overnight or during the day shift. The day shift desk officer processed 154
  11|   Firearms Licenses, 17 Non FID related fingerprints, and registered 16 Sex Offenders. The 3pm to 7pm desk
  12|   watch officers assisted in the processing of, 50 detainees while providing suicide watch and numerous other
  13|   services including providing support for the patrol staff.
  14|   Calls for service, which will continue to rise, are affected by, not only growth in our community, but the sur-
  15|   rounding communities as well. Traffic too will continually increase and will have a direct effect on the amount
  16|   of traffic accidents the police will have to investigate. Environmental factors too play a role in the motor vehi-
  17|   cle accident picture. Incidents and arrests amounts are affected by social and economic factors.
  18|   I again emphasize the important of keeping with current growth trends. Growth invariably plays a major role
  19|   in Police Calls for Service, arrests, incidents and motor vehicle accidents. The demands for police services will
  20|   continue to rise and the police department will have to grow in kind.
  21|   Calls for service have increased by 13.6% over last year. Incidents have declined by 16.5%. Incidents are cases
  22|   that do not necessarily result in an Arrest, but require a report and investigation. Arrests are down by 2.9%,
  23|   with motor vehicle accidents increasing by 17.15%.
  24|   Group A crime against Persons are down this year. These crimes include; Rape, Statutory Rape, Aggravated
  25|   Assault, Simple Assault, Intimidation, Kidnapping/Abduction.
  26|   Group A crimes against Property is up 9% this year. Crime against Property include; Burglary/Breaking & Enter-
  27|   ing, Larceny, Shoplifting, motor vehicle theft, Forgery, Fraud, stolen property, destruction of property, vandal-
  28|   ism.
  29|   Group A crimes against Society have increased by 16%. Crimes against Society include; Drug crimes, Pornogra-
  30|   phy/Obscene Material, Weapons law violations.
  31|   Group B crimes have declined by 21% this year. Group B crimes include; Bad checks, Disorderly conduct, Driv-
  32|   ing under the influence, Drunkenness, Family nonviolent offenses, Liquor Law Violations, Trespassing, and all
  33|   other offenses.
  34|   Lunenburg is a safe community to live in thanks in large part to citizen input. Reporting crime is key to the
  35|   safety of a Town such as ours. The Police cannot provide effective protection without the citizen involvement.
  36|   I encourage every citizen to report crime as it occurs, without hesitation, as a timely response is vital to the
  37|   successfully outcome of any police investigation. “See something, say something.”
  38|   Thank you again this year for allowing me to manage your Police Department. The necessary funding to sup-
  39|   port my request to bring police staffing to a level that will keep up with the demands of growth has been over-
  40|   whelming. I ask you to please continue to support our initiatives. The police department endeavors to deliver
  41|   the service the public expects and is entitled to.
  42|   Respectfully Submitted
  43|   Chief James P. Marino
  44|   Chief James P. Marino

===PAGE 105=== (layout, 38 lines)
   1|                                                                                     PLANNING BOARD
   2| Ten ANR plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control
   3| Law, but still requiring Board endorsement) were approved.
   4| Approved residential subdivisions were in various stages of completion.  Construction was
   5| completed on Butterfly Lane (a nine-lot subdivision), Emerald Place at Lake Whalom, and
   6| Stone Farm Estates.  Tri Town Landing received Board approval for construction of Building
   7| #5, which will consist of 70 units of senior housing.  Tri Town Landing is a 40R project,
   8| which seeks to increase the supply of housing and decrease its cost by increasing the amount
   9| of land zoned for dense housing.  Tri Town Landing is a 100% rental complex.
  10| Construction continued on Massachusetts Avenue for an office and garage for the Lunenburg
  11| Water District.  Asian Imperial Restaurant received approval for a second parking lot.  Ecolog-
  12| ical Fibers, Inc. received approval for construction of additional office and warehouse space.
  13| In conjunction with the Montachusett Regional Planning Commission (MRPC), the Board be-
  14| gan work on crafting a Residential & Commercial Permitting Guidebook.  The Guidebook will
  15| be funded by the Massachusetts District Local Technical Assistance Program and will facili-
  16| tate permitting by providing easy-to-follow procedures that outline the permit review process
  17| for homeowners, property and business owners, developers, brokers and contractors.
  18| The Planning Board proposed and Town Meeting approved a collection of Zoning Changes
  19| that created a new Use Table and use definitions in Section 250-4.1 of the Code of Lunenburg.
  20| This Use Table is intended to contemporize and standardize the definition of uses while also
  21| streamlining residents’ and businesses’ ability to find information.
  22| Taking into consideration the regulations of the Cannabis Control Commission, the Board be-
  23| gan the process of crafting a recreational marijuana zoning bylaw for May 2019 Town Meet-
  24| ing.
  25| Michael-Ray Jeffreys stepped down from the Board; Tanner Cole joined the Board.  The
  26| Board reorganized in June 2018 with Gregory Bittner, Matthew Allison and Kenneth Chenis
  27| continuing in their respective positions of Chair, Vice Chair, and Clerk.
  28| The Planning Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue
  29| and can be reached at 978-582-4147 extension 5.  Various forms are available online at                     https://
  30| lunenburgma.gov/ on the Planning Board’s homepage.  The Zoning Map, Code of the Town of
  31| Lunenburg, MA (which contains the Protective {Zoning} Bylaws), Rules and Regulations
  32| Governing the Subdivision of Land, and Rules & Regulations for Site Plan Approval & Spe-
  33| cial Permits are available online.  The Board continues to work on updating the Zoning portion
  34| of the Code.

  36| Matthew Allison, Vice Chair              David Prokowiew, Member

===PAGE 106=== (layout, 21 lines)
   1|                    Ad Hoc Open Space Advisory Committee to the Planning Board
   2|   The Open Space Committee’s efforts in FY’18 revolved around updating the town’s expired Open Space and
   3|   Recreation Plan.  The Plan was drafted in accordance with Massachusetts Executive Office of Energy and Envi-
   4|   ronmental Affairs Division of Conservation Services’ (DCS) guidelines and is intended to direct Lunenburg                        ’s
   5|   efforts to improve and enhance its open space and recreation resources. The Plan is the culmination of many
   6|   months of volunteer work, built with input from all corners of town government and, most importantly, its resi-
   7|   dents.  It is not only comprehensive in its scope, but is also measured in its objectives and attainable in its goals.
   8|   The Plan has been submitted to DCS for review and, once approved, will make the town eligible for DCS-
   9|   administered grant programs through 2026.
  10|   Partly driven by the Open Space Plan update, the Planning Board also expanded and modified the composition
  11|   of the committee.  We are now comprised of seven members instead of five, with one representative each from
  12|   Planning, Conservation, and Parks and four at-large members.  The diverse representation allows for greater
  13|   collaboration and communication among related land use boards around matters of conservation and recreation
  14|   interest.
  15|   Finally, the Open Space Committee continued its role in reviewing Chapter 61 withdrawals notices for their
  16|   potential open space and recreation value to town and advising other municipal boards of the same.
  17|   Brandon Kibbe, Chair                                                      Richard Brockelman, Member At Large
  18|   David Prokowiew, Planning Board                                              Sarah Cammer, Member At Large
  19|   Bob Pease, Conservation Commission                                           Ashley Maillet, Member At Large
  20|   Karin Menard, Parks Commission
  21|                                                                  106

   1| 107
   2|                        Zoning Board of Appeals
   3| The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2018.   There were several applicants seeking
   4| special permits and special permits for dimensional variations.  The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and
   5| fourth Wednesdays of the month on an “as needed” basis.
   6| The Zoning Board consists of the following members:  Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, J ames Besarkarski, Vice Chairman,
   7| Members:  Hans Wentrup , Paul Doherty and David Blatt  and Associate member Patrick Callahan .   Lisa Normandin
   8| serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
   9| The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  10| 1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town’s Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  11| 2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town’s Protective By-laws.
  12| 3. To hear and d ecide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as
  13| amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action
  14| from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town , the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or
  15| other Administrative Official.
  16| 4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter
  17| 40B.  The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for compreh ensive permits submitted to the Board under
  18| Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.  These requests can, by law, circumvent local
  19| zoning ordinances.
  20| The o ffice of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Build ing, 960 Massachusetts Avenue ,
  21| Lunenburg.  Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday and
  22| Thursday, closed Friday.  If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978 -582-
  23| 4146 X 421 during regular business hours.  Zoning Board applications are available in the office and o nline at
  24| www.Lunenburgonline.gov.

===PAGE 110=== (layout, 47 lines)
   1|                                         Lunenburg Public Schools
   2|                                                   Superintendent’s Message
   3| It is a pleasure for me to present my first annual report for the Lunenburg Public Schools.
   4| This report reflects some of what I have learned about the promises and needs of a district I
   5| am proud to lead.        During the last 6 months, I have been executing my Entry Plan. I have
   6| conducted forums and one-on-one interviews. I have reviewed many documents.  I have at-
   7| tended numerous school and community events and activities.                     I have been able to observe
   8| the district processes and procedures, tour the facilities and grounds, and most importantly,
   9| spend time in classrooms observing teaching and learning.  While there is much to be proud
  10| of, there is also good work to be done to improve the efficiency of operations, strengthen
  11| curriculum and instruction, and support all learners.
  12| I want to acknowledge that this community has demonstrated its support for the schools by
  13| supporting two building projects in the last 15 years.  The Lunenburg Primary School and
  14| Lunenburg Middle/High School are beautiful, modern facilities for teaching and learning.
  15| A modern facility is; however, but one factor in providing students with a quality education.
  16| Rigorous programming designed to meet the academic, social and emotional needs of all of
  17| our students is foundational to a quality learning experience.  In addition to meeting a stu-
  18| dent’s basic needs, we must ensure that the curriculum is well-designed; materials are avail-
  19| able to implement that curriculum; and that teachers have the knowledge and skills requisite
  20| for effective instructional practice.  All of this must be supported by the use of data to assess
  21| and monitor student learning. Today’s classrooms also require the seamless integration of
  22| instructional technology to create engaging lessons, provide differentiated instruction and
  23| assessment, and facilitate analysis of achievement data to monitor the growth and support
  24| the success of all of our students.
  25| I also want to acknowledge that I have experienced a collaborative approach to planning and
  26| problem-solving with the Town Manager, Heather Lemieux.                       I am very grateful for that.  I
  27| appreciate the effort on the part of the School Committee and the Board of Selectmen to
  28| strengthen communication and find opportunities to work together.                        I trust that, working
  29| together, we will be able to find ways to meet the needs of the children of Lunenburg.
  30| Through my transition, the district continues to be focused on implementation of the District
  31| Strategic Plan (2018-2023).  Our Strategic Goals are:
  32| Goal 1: Social-emotional learning, prevention programs and secure schools provide safe
  33| environments, promote kindness and celebrate cultural differences.
  34| Goal 2: Civic learning and engagement enable all students to become knowledgeable citi-
  35| zens and active community members.
  36| Goal 3: Curricula and assessments in major subject areas are aligned with recent Massa-
  37| chusetts State Frameworks and fully implemented.
  38| Goal 4: Implementation of digital literacy and computer science curriculum is expanded;
  39| and, instructional technologies are more available.
  40| Goal 5: The processes for annual professional development ensure opportunities for well-
  41| targeted individual, school-based, and district-wide professional growth.
  42| Goal 6: Strategies to improve instructional support for struggling learners are enhanced.
  43| Goal 7: Enrichment opportunities are expanded to include the arts and other areas of high
  44| student interest.
  45| Goal 8: Collaboration and communications within and among the schools and the commu-
  46| nity are improved.
  47|                                                          110

===PAGE 111=== (plain, 34 lines)
   1| 111
   2| As evidence of continued progress on these goals, we are revising our social studies and his-
   3| tory curriculum to align with the new Massachusetts History and Social Science Curriculum
   4| Framework; providing professional development at the Primary School in literacy as part of a
   5| Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE) literacy grant; providing pro-
   6| fessional development in behavioral interventions and supports, and building a strong school
   7| culture.  We have created district-wide leadership in these areas with the creation of a Behav-
   8| ioral Health Advisory Council, Elementary Literacy Team, and Professional Development
   9| Committee.
  10| The MCAS data analysis conducted at the district leadership level indicates a need for con-
  11| tinued focus on curricular and instructional supports in order to increase the achievement and
  12| growth of our Students with Disabilities subgroup.  Grades 4 and 5, require resources and
  13| focus on essay writing, specifically on conventions, text types and purposes.  In grades 3 and
  14| 4, we need to target instruction on multiplication and fractions; and in grade 6 on equations
  15| and inequalities.
  16| This year DESE implemented a new accountability system.  The new system still allows the
  17| DESE to identify schools requiring assistance or intervention, as well as those demonstrating
  18| success. Highlights of the new system include: (1) the inclusion of additional accountability
  19| indicators; (2) a focus on raising the performance of each district ’s or school ’s lowest per-
  20| forming students in addition to the performance of the district or school as a whole; and (3)
  21| the discontinuation of accountability and assistance levels (Levels 1 to 5).
  22| Under the new accountability system, annual performance determinations for districts and
  23| schools will be calculated using Indicators: English language arts (ELA), Mathematics, and
  24| Science achievement; ELA and Mathematics mean student growth percentile (SGP); four -
  25| year cohort graduation rate, extended engagement rate, annual dropout rate; student progress
  26| towards attaining English language proficiency; chronic absenteeism, percentage of 11th &
  27| 12th graders completing advanced coursework.
  28| Indicators are weighted.  Substantial weight for achievement, growth, English language pro-
  29| ficiency, and graduation rate indicators are required by federal law. Federal law also requires
  30| that these indicators combined should have much greater weight than the additional indica-
  31| tors. Under the new accountability system, the weighting of achievement and growth is based
  32| on ratios instead of percentages, maintaining the achievement to growth ratio of 3 to 1.
  33| The chart below shows the Accountability of the district and schools following the 2018
  34| Spring MCAS.

   5|                                                                School Facilities
   6| Having completed the second year of operation of the new Lunenburg Middle High School, the
   7| school electrical, plumbing and mechanical systems have been operating at a high level and all
   8| post construction issues have been resolved. The focus of school maintenance for FY19 contin-
   9| ues on preventative maintenance on heating and ventilation systems and energy conservation
  10| measures. The focus of repairs has been on Turkey Hill Elementary School with minor im-
  11| provements such as switch replacement, painting, and other minor items.  All schools were ex-
  12| tensively tested for lead and copper in the drinking water in June 2018 funded by a Mass DEP
  13| assistance Program for Lead in the Drinking Water. Turkey Hill Elementary School had a num-
  14| ber of positive samples that corresponded to areas where water use was low. Directions were
  15| given to the school staff to flush taps at the start of the school day, repairs to some fixtures were
  16| made, and all areas that had positives will be retested in May- June 2019.  The Green Commu-
  17| nity grant received by the Town in June 2018 will be used to fund a weatherization project at
  18| Turkey Hill Elementary School to seal areas above the ceiling in the administration area and
  19| connecting corridors, add gasketing to windows and to close several holes from past changes to
  20| the school. Work is scheduled to be performed in February 2019 by Boston Energy Retrofit
  21| LLC at a cost of $17,379.00.  Funding was also provided for conversion of Turkey  Hill
  22|                                                          113

===PAGE 114=== (plain, 36 lines)
   1| 114
   2| Elementary School to LED lighting which was funded at roughly $36,000. This lighting retrofit
   3| will occur in April 2019.
   4| The FY 19 capital program funded additional asbestos abatement at Turkey Hill Elementary
   5| School. In July 2018, Air Quality Experts for $145,893.65 removed spray-on fire proofing above
   6| the ceiling in six classrooms on the first floor of the classroom wing.  This project included removal
   7| of the splined ceiling and the floor tiles in the classrooms. Turkey Hill Elementary School is
   8| expected to continue to have additional asbestos removal planned for a number of years.   A request
   9| for $162,000 for FY20 was submitted into the town’s capital planning process to abate the second
  10| stairway and the second floor hallway of the classroom wing.

  13| Technology Department
  14| Technology touches all our lives every day at the Lunenburg Public Schools. It is our aim in IT to
  15| serve our students, faculty and staff proactively, so that the tools used to facilitate learning function
  16| seamlessly and responsively when needed. From checking email, to accessing class information,
  17| to connecting and collaborating with students and colleagues near and far, faculty, staff, and IT
  18| are working together to help redefine what is possible for our students, so they can redefine what’s
  19| possible for the world. Our annual report highlights the IT team, as well as some of the
  20| improvements we have made this past year to achieve this goal.

   4| As planned, this year we added grade nine to our one -to-one Chromebook program, freeing up
   5| several Chromebook carts previously used by that class.  With a bit of creative redeployment, and
   6| by purchasing a very small number of additional Chromebooks, we wer e also able to add grade
   7| eight to the program, for a total of five grades and about 625 Chromebooks in the program.
   8| Feedback on the Chromebooks has been overwhelmingly positive from students and faculty alike,
   9| and we plan to continue the program for as lo ng as funds are available to sustain it.  We also
  10| deployed a new iPad cart at the Primary school for use by the Kindergarten class, as well as a new
  11| Chromebox lab in the library at Turkey Hill for use by all grades.
  12| Our ultimate goal remains to provide the tools necessary for faculty, staff, and students alike to
  13| facilitate the learning process in a secure, highly-available, and high-performing environment, and
  14| to always just ‘make IT work’.

  17| Special Services Department
  18|  FY18 (data taken from 2017-2018 school year)
  19| From the March 1, 2018 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics – District Summary),
  20| there are 266 students with disabilities receiving special education or related services in Lunenburg
  21| (last year there were 275).  This represents 16.2% of our total student population of 1,637 students
  22| (it was 16.9% last year).  The state average from the 2018 school report card is 17.7%.  From the
  23| March 1, 2018 report (SIMS Report 7-SPED Enrollment Statistics – District Summary), the total
  24| number of students receiving services outside the district was 14 (last year was 14).  Of the 14
  25| students, 6 are placed in Collaborative Placements, 5 are in Day Placements and 3 are in
  26| Residential Placements.  Our Advanced Community Experience Program (ACE), which serves
  27| students ages 18-22years old, had 9 students enrolled last year.  Additionally, from the March 1,
  28| 2018 report, 118 students identified with a disability received accommodati ons via 504 plans in
  29| Lunenburg (last year was 103).
  30| This year as a district, our overall Accountability and Assistance Level is NOT
  31| REQUIRING ASSISTANCE OR INTERVENTION with the Primary School (Insufficient data),
  32| Turkey Hill Elementary School (Not requi ring assistance or intervention), Lunenburg Middle
  33| School (Not requiring assistance or intervention), Lunenburg High School (Not requiring
  34| assistance or intervention) and the Advanced Community Experience Program (Insufficient data).
  35| The state report card  lists the district’s determination of need for Special Education technical
  36| assistance or intervention for Students with Disabilities in Lunenburg as “Meets Requirements
  37| (MR)”.  Students with Disabilities participated in MCAS this year with the following r ates:
  38| English Language Arts = 98% (it was 99% last year), Mathematics = 98% (It was 99% last year),
  39| and Science = 100% (It was 97% last year).  We “Met Target” for all areas of participation.  This
  40| data can be found at the DESE website:

   3| Indicator 4 – Suspension (more than 10 days)/Expulsion for Students with IEPs.  For Lunenburg,

   9| Substantially Separate Placements = 13.9%, and for Separate Schools/Residential
  10| Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 7.0%.  In Lunenburg, our percentages are as follows:  Full
  11| Inclusion = 64.9%, Partial = 17.3%, Subs tantially Separate = 10.9% and Separate schools,
