  13| Indicator 6 – Educational Environments for Students Aged 3 -5 with IEPs.  The 2016-2017 state
  14| rates showed the percent of students receiving a majority of their special education an d related
  15| services in a Full Inclusion early childhood program was 53.7%, in a Partial Inclusion Program
  16| was 20.1%, a Substantially Separate Placement (special education class, separate school, or
  17| residential facility) was 16.7%, and students not attending  an early childhood program but
  18| receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was 9.5%. In Lunenburg, our early childhood
  19| Full Inclusion rate was 27.5%, our Partial Inclusion rate was 30.0%, Substantially Separate rate
  20| was 27.5%, and Related Services was 15.0%.
  21| Indicator 7 – Early Childhood Outcomes.  Districts participate in Indicator 7 data collection as
  22| part of a 4 year cycle in which they report exit data on children for whom they have collected
  23| entry level data; exit may occur at any time during the subsequent three year period as the
  24| children leave the preschool program.  Lunenburg did not have a cohort completion year in
  25| 2016-2017.
  26| Indicator 8 – Parent Involvement.  This indicator measures the % of parents with a child receiving
  27| special education who report that school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving
  28| services and results for students with IEPs.  For 2016 -2017 the state target was 85.5%.
  29| Lunenburg’s rate was 91.3% and the state’s rate was 82.9% (from information collecte d in the
  30| Spring of 2017).
  31| Indicator 9 & 10 -  Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and
  32| in Special Education Categories.  In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no
  33| disproportionality found in either case.
  34| Indicator 11 – Initial Evaluation Timelines.   This indicator measures the % of children with
  35| parental consent to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline. Data is
  36| reported for all initial evaluations initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting
  37| year.  Lunenburg was rated 100% in compliance and the state average for this Indicator was
  38| 100%

===PAGE 118=== (plain, 44 lines)
   1| 118
   2| Indicator 12 – Early Childhood Transition.  This indicator measures the % of children referred
   3| by Part C, found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and
   4| implemented by their third birthdays. The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg
   5| is noted as “Suppressed”.  Data is suppressed if the number of students referred by Part C and
   6| found eligible for special education services is fewer than 10.
   7| Indicator 13 – Secondary Transition.  In all years, the state target for Secondary Transition is
   8| 100%.  This indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that
   9| includes appropriate measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updated and based upon
  10| an age appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that
  11| will reasonably enable the student to meet those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals
  12| related to the student's transition services needs.  Lunenburg was last reviewed in this area in
  13| 2015-2016 and was 100.0% in compliance.
  14| Indicator 14 – Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs.  This indicator measures the %
  15| of students with IEPs who exited high school during the 2015-2016 school year and self-
  16| reported post-school engagement in education or employment one year after leaving high
  17| school. Engagement is defined through three measurements:
  18|  Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  19|  Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year
  20| of leaving high school.
  21|  Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education
  22| or training program; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within
  23| one year of leaving high school.
  24| Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters
  25| engaged in a post -secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2016 -2017
  26| school year, state targets for the three measurements of Post-School Outcomes are Measurement
  27| A: 51%; Measurement B: 86%; and Measurement C: 93%.  Lunenburg rates are as follows (from
  28| reporting year 2014-15):  Measurement A = 50%, Measurement B = 75%, and Measurement C
  29| = 75%.
  30| The Parent Advisory Council Supporting All Learners (PACSAL) continues  to be an
  31| active support group for parents, teachers and administration.  All parents and community
  32| members can join the group, which currently consists of parents of students receiving Title I
  33| services and parents of students with disabilities.  Meetings are typically held the first Friday of
  34| each month, 11:00AM, in the Planning Room at the Middle School.  The executive board is
  35| made up of Chairperson: Kelly Harvey. Several events and presentations were organized by the
  36| PACSAL for both parents and teachers  in Lunenburg this year. For more information, go to:
  37| https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/lps/special-services/pacsal?authuser=0

  39| needs services to meet the varying needs of our students in the least restrictive environment.  We
  40| have an experienced, hard-working staff that works together with parents to provide these varied
  41| services.   Our parents are equal partners in our efforts to develop challenging and focused
  42| educational programs for children.  We remain committed to the Lunenburg Public Schools
  43| mission, “The mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills,
  44| confidence, and passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful

  24| Lunenburg Primary School
  25| The Lunenburg Primary School is an early childhood education center for students in preschool to
  26| second grade.  Our students range from three years old to eight years old. Our school community
  27| is made up of students, teachers, parents and support staff working together to maintain a learning
  28| environment that focuses on the academic and social emotional needs of the whole child.
  29| The October 1, 2018 student enrollment reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary
  30| Education was 422 students.  Currently our Preschool program services 48 three and four year old
  31| students. We have six Kindergarten classes servicing 147 students. Class si zes in our
  32| Kindergartens are at 24-25 students.  This year we  have five grade one classrooms servicing 115
  33| students with class sizes between  21-24. Five second grade classrooms have 119 students with
  34| class sizes ranging from 23 to 25 students.
  35| Our professional development has centered on literacy instruction this year.  In early October we
  36| received notification from Governor Charles D. Baker that the school was awarded a FY19 Early
  37| Grades Literacy grant of $6,520.  This grant funded teacher participation in an extensive
  38| professional development opportunity that included three statewide literacy institutes and follow-
  39| up regional meetings.  We explored the development of language in young children (birth-9); the
  40| role language plays in literacy; and the content of the Massachusetts ELA/Literacy Framework
  41| Standards for Language.

===PAGE 120=== (plain, 40 lines)
   1| 120
   2| Teachers discussed strategies to engage early grades students in reading, writing, talking, and
   3| thinking about the content they are learning in order to promote language development.  The grant
   4| team shared information with grade level teams throughout the year. In September and November,
   5| the general and special education teachers participated in two full day Project Read workshops
   6| focused on phonics. Project Read applies sound/symbol knowledge to spelling and reading
   7| comprehension using targeted multisenso ry activities and direct instruction.  This professional
   8| development was designed to enhance the instruction we provide for K -2 students each day at
   9| school.
  10|    The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system
  11| to insure that all students have the services they need to be successful.  Common assessments are
  12| completed in the fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K -2 in the areas of reading,
  13| writing, and math.  These common assessments serve to monitor  the effectiveness of teacher
  14| instruction and the progress of students.  Struggling students are identified for additional support
  15| in Reading, Math, Speech, Occupational and Physical Therapy.
  16| Our staff works to maintain a school culture which is positive,  inclusive, and supportive of each
  17| of our members. This year we have collaborated with Dr. Curt Bates of Seaside Educational
  18| Consultants to identify strengths, weaknesses, opportunities and threats in our school practices and
  19| culture.  He is guiding us to focus on a few areas that can really enhance our overall school culture.
  20| The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school related concerns.  Serving as an
  21| advisory group to the principal, the council is charged with reviewing the school building budget
  22| and developing the school improvement plan.

  12|                                              Turkey Hill Elementary School
  13| Turkey Hill Elementary is now in its third year as a 3-5 building. There are currently 372 students,
  14| with 121 third graders, 115 fourth graders, and 136 fifth graders.  We employ 24.5 teachers, 100%
  15| of whom are licensed in their assignment area. Our student/teacher ratio is 15.2 to 1, including the
  16| special area and specia l education teachers, not as an average homeroom size.  Our MCAS data
  17| from the spring of 2018 indicates we are meeting or exceeding expectations above state averages
  18| in the following areas:  Grade 3 Reading at 59%, Grade 4 Math at 51% , Grade 5 Math at 47% and
  19| Grade 5 Science at 50%.  There are teaching teams of 2 at each grade level as well as self-contained
  20| classrooms in grades 3 and 4.  Special area classes include art, music, PE, library, technology,
  21| computer skills, and for 5th graders, developmental guidance.
  22| Teachers are engaging in a variety of professional development trainings.  To address physical and
  23| mental health concerns there’s been training in Trauma, Mindfulness, Epilepsy, and Executive
  24| Functioning.  To improve literacy, teachers have taken classes in Calkins Units of Study, Reader’s
  25| Workshop, and Project Read.  Math teachers have participated in several hours of preparation in
  26| the Everyday Math platform as well as IXL math.  A full day of professional development in
  27| November was dedicated to computer literacy and software offerings.
  28| We offered many after school activities for students in the winter and spring last year.  Over 100
  29| students signed up for cooking, student council, crafts, paper making, board games, kickball and
  30| garden art.  We had the Girls on the Run program both in the spring and fall with between 15 -25
  31| participants and a chess program taught by a chess master which includes about a dozen students.
  32| $6100 was raised during our annual Penny Wars event to support funding for a  variety of field
  33| trips and in-house performances.  5th graders spent a week in Beckett, MA enjoying the Nature’s
  34| Classroom program.  The Bridges program introduced students to several senior citizen volunteers
  35| who came in to share their life experiences and learn about the younger generation.  The six week
  36| program culminated in a walking field trip to the Eagle House to celebrate the bonds that had been
  37| created and allow students to tour the senior center.  In June they attended a Worcester
  38| Braveheart’s baseball game at Hanover Park and took in the activities for Baseball in Education
  39| Day.  In the fall, grade 5 teachers instituted regular walking field trips to the public library every
  40| month so students could become familiar with one of Lunenburg’s best public resources.

===PAGE 123=== (plain, 40 lines)
   1| 123
   2| 4th graders went to Moose Hill to learn about land formations and the effects of erosion.  They
   3| welcomed the Boston Museum of Science out for a day of fossil study.  3 rd graders took a trip to
   4| the Concord Museum to learn about the early colonists as well as to Monty Tech for a performance
   5| of The Nutcracker.  They learned about weather patterns from a Museum of Science visit.  The
   6| TIGER performances from Plymouth State University provided a program to Grades 3 and 4 about
   7| how to be a good friend and how to speak up when others aren’t being well treated.   This was
   8| underwritten by a generous grant from the Maki Foundation.  The Zoomobile came out from
   9| Southwick Zoo to give 3rd graders an up close introduction to some exotic animals.
  10| North End Subaru in Lunenburg once again gifted our school with dozens of non -fiction, high
  11| interest titles about science.  They include such titles as: This Book Stinks! Gross Garbage, Rotten
  12| Rubbish, and the Science of Trash; Try This! 50 Extreme  Fun & Safe Experiments for the Mad
  13| Scientist in You; and Magnets Push, Magnets Pull.  We are grateful for their continued support as
  14| well as several other community organizations:    the PTO , whose fund s allow all students to
  15| participate in field trips and financed the purchase of a six month trial program to IXL Learning ,
  16| the Lunenburg Turkey Hill Family Lions Club who provided scholarships for Nature’s Classroom
  17| and regularly provides food donations fo r students at Extended Day, BoxTop collectors and
  18| counters, Hannaford Helps supporters, Community Reader day volunteers, parents (both those
  19| who support their children at home as well as those who donate hours to school), our Veteran’s
  20| Day and Memorial Day guests and guest speakers.  In December with the blessing of our School
  21| Advisory Council, we agreed to participate in the Kylee’s Kare Kits for Kids program in
  22| Leominster.  This offers our students in need weekend bags of food, including winter and spring
  23| break and summer vacation.  This officially piloted in January 2019.
  24| With the addition of a new music teacher in September, we have created a band program for grade
  25| five students.  There are over 30 students who participate and learn their instruments in sectionals
  26| and as part of a full band twice weekly.  Their holiday concert in December was a rousing success
  27| and included a small but melodic chorus.  In the future we hope to expand that program to include
  28| 4th graders as well.  3rd graders are introduced to instruments through recorder instruction.  Students
  29| also have the opportunity to participate in an after school music program to learn piano, guitar, etc.
  30| As an elementary school we understand that our mission extends far beyond the academic.  We try
  31| to promote positive and civil interchange and empathy for others.  We teach and reinforce coping
  32| strategies, self-regulation skills, and calming techniques.   We welcome new students and staff
  33| who join us throughout the year on our hallway bulletin board.  We use age appropriate picture
  34| books at each grade to introduce students to diverse cultures and expand their understanding of the
  35| world.  We teach responsible digital citizenship in an era where soon they will be inundated with
  36| social media.  We celebrate the positive role models that exist in our school community with
  37| student-of-the-month acknowledgements.
  38| Respectfully Submitted,
  39| Heidi Champagne

   2| Lunenburg Middle School
   3| Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs
   4| that support students’ social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child
   5| becomes a self -motivated, creative, lifelong learners in a safe, stimulating environment that
   6| encourages diversity and open honest communication.   The dedicated and accomplished staff of
   7| professionals within the school embraces the philosophy of educating the whole child
   8| academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a means by which all students become
   9| positive community members that possess the skills to be successful in high school and beyond.
  10|  Lunenburg Middle School is in its third year as a co -located middle- high school serving grades
  11| six through eight in a beautiful facility. Our effective middle school model is student centered and
  12| provides opportunities to explore a variety of different interests and talents through special area
  13| and enrichment classes and extracurricular activit ies. One of our goals is for students to discover
  14| something they are passionate about and want to continue exploring as they move through high
  15| school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for students that foster critical
  16| thinking and prob lem solving skills that prepare them for high school and beyond or what their
  17| passion may lead them to.
  18| % of Students scoring in the meeting or exceeding expectations
  19| range Spring 2018 MCAS
  20|              GRADE                 SUBJECT    LMS            STATE   DIFF                SGP
  21|       6       ELA                   50%            51%                 -1                      50
  22|       6      Math                   60%         47%    +13                   59
  23|       7       ELA                   51%         46%                  +5                    53
  24|       7      Math                                 56%             46%    +10                    48
  25|                     8                        ELA                                    61%            51%                 +10                   50

===PAGE 126=== (plain, 39 lines)
   1| 126
   2| In reviewing the results of the 2018 Spring M.C.A.S. tests, our percentage of students scoring in
   3| the meeting or exceeding expectations range remains strong and above the state average in 6 of
   4| the 7 tested areas in the middle school. The Department of Elem entary and Secondary Education
   5| provides an accountability rating for all schools and districts in the state based upon next
   6| generation MCAS testing. Lunenburg Middle School accountability rating for 2018 is designated
   7| as not requiring intervention, partially meeting goals.
   8|  Although we are proud of our students’ performance, we continue to analyze data in our monthly
   9| results faculty meetings designed to improve our instruction for all students. Our faculty has
  10| identified areas of improvement based upon the data and implemented strategies to improve their
  11| instructional practices that will help students succeed.
  12| We continue to utilize intervention and power blocks during the school day to meet the needs of
  13| each individual student. These blocks of inst ructional time that have been built into our master
  14| schedule to allow teachers time to work with students at their own individual skill level.  The
  15| philosophy is to challenge students who are high academic achievers as well as strengthen skills
  16| for students who have identified weaknesses. Teachers are able to work with students in a small
  17| group to answer individual questions or provide more individualized support.  We design this
  18| support model to allow students to feel more confident, thus, perform better in the classroom.  We
  19| will continue to refine our intervention block times to best meet the needs of our students. One
  20| option for students during this intervention block is the use of our web based program called IXL.
  21| IXL allows students to work at their ow n skill level, build confidence and moved forward with
  22| work that is aligned with the state standards. IXL allows teachers a tool for true differentiation for
  23| every student allowing them to work and grow at their own pace and level.
  24| Lunenburg Middle School  continues to offer enrichment blocks for all students. Enrichment
  25| classes provide students a block of time in which they receive instruction in the areas of
  26| developmental health and guidance.   Students also receive instruction in physical education, art,
  27| and music classes that allows them to explore their strengths and talents. Students also have the
  28| opportunity to sign up for and participate in band and chorus during this block. This enrichment
  29| block is in addition to the traditional special area classes  students receive, and this block offers
  30| students additional physical movement throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands on
  31| activities and more exposure to the arts, theatre and technology throughout the school day.
  32| We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different programs
  33| developed to promote social and emotional growth, self -awareness, and community service. We
  34| will continue to utilize Developmental Design program to deliver our social emotional curriculum.
  35| Students meet with teachers in small groups to teach social responsibility and problem solving, as
  36| well as developing empathy and promoting positive self -esteem and strong teacher/student
  37| relationships. Our social emotional programs have been developed to allow students to showcase
  38| their talents and abilities, grow and improve as individuals, and promote community outreach for
  39| the younger generation.

===PAGE 127=== (plain, 15 lines)
   1| 127
   2| This year, we have been able to offer foreign language exploratory to our sixth and seventh grade
   3| students, giving them the opportunity to explore several different foreign language options in order
   4| to make an informed decision on which language they would  like to take in grade eight and high
   5| school. The addition of the foreign language instruction has been well received and the students
   6| enjoy exploring different cultures and languages. We also provide students the opportunity to
   7| direct their own learning w ith a program called Genius Hour. Genius Hour fosters a passion for
   8| learning back into the curriculum by having students facilitate their own learning based on their
   9| interests.
  10| Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and supports that
  11| ensure student success and promote a passion for lifelong learning. The key components to our
  12| success is our talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and
  13| community who support our teaching and learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us
  14| through their commitment to learning. Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of
  15| students.

   2| Lunenburg High School
   3| 2018-2019
   4| Lunenburg High School has been a hub for academic and social activity in 2018 -2019.  Teachers
   5| continue to provide our students rigorous coursework and hold our students to high standards.  Our
   6| students meet these challenges through collaboration, self -advocacy, and strong work ethic.  All
   7| students in grades 9 -12 have been issued a Chrome Book and there are wireless capabilities
   8| throughout the building.  Every classroom and instructional space has an interactive projector and
   9| voice-lift capabilities. Our students are fortunate to be in a building that can meet the needs of the
  10| 21st century learner.
  11| During the fall sports season, our students practiced and competed on our three grass fields and
  12| new artificial turf field.  The youth sports teams in the community were scheduled on  the fields,
  13| as well.  During the fall season, most of our fields were being used from 3:00 pm to 8:00 pm.
  14| The winter season has been very busy, as well.  The gym and weight room are being used for a
  15| large portion of the afternoon and evening by our basketball teams, indoor track and field teams,
  16| and students and community members in the Adult Education Program.  Additionally, we have
  17| athletics teams practicing and competing at: Wallace Civi c Center, The Groton School, Nashoba
  18| Valley Ski Area, and at Fitchburg High School.  Our actors and actresses have been rehearsing
  19| since January for the Competitive Class Plays.
  20| NEASC ACCREDITATION
  21| Lunenburg High School is currently going through the accre ditation process.  Lunenburg High
  22| School is currently working on the five priority areas for the NEASC school growth plan. The five
  23| Priority Growth Areas are: Curriculum Maps, Professional Growth/Development, School Civic
  24| Engagement, Instructional Practices, and Emergency Response Procedures. The five areas we are
  25| currently working on to produce a report for our NEASC accreditation. The final accreditation
  26| visit will be conducted in 2020 and will look at how well we are making progress on our growth
  27| plan.
  28| SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
  29| Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of fundraising
  30| and collection endeavors. In the spring, a canned goods drive will be held and admission to the
  31| PowderPuff game will cost two canne d goods.  Everything collected will be donated to the
  32| Lunenburg Food Pantry.  Around the holiday season, advisories, LEA, STUCO, NHS and
  33| numerous other agencies collected items for the Lunenburg Gift Card Mart Drive.
  34| A Senior English class called community service learning component is a class in which students
  35| are tasked with researching and identifying needs in the community and then forming an action
  36| plan to address the needs.  For example, we have students working on: careers that do not require
  37| college level degrees.  This is to help students make a career choice that does not require a college
  38| degree.

   2| In March, the National Honor Society will be having its annual Blood Drive.  Students sixteen and
   3| over (and staff members) will be able to participate in the Blood Drive with parent permission.
   4| LHS has held evening presentations for parents/guardians on the following topics:
   5|  Planning for High School (grade 8)
   6|  Early College Planning (grades 9-10)

  12| Mount Wachusett Community College.
  13| MCAS RESULTS
  14| We are pleased to announce that Lunenburg High School is recognized by the Department of
  15| Elementary and Secondary Education as a Level 1 school.  As a Level 1 school, we continue to be
  16| among the highest performing schools in the Commonwealth.  School percentiles (1 -99) indicate
  17| how a school is performing overall compared to other schools that serve the same or similar grades.
  18| Our school's percentile is ranked in the 69 th percentile. We are very proud of the work that our
  19| students and teachers have done to prepare for the MCAS test.  While we are recognized as a high
  20| performing school, we understand this is only one measure used to show student growth and that
  21| there is constantly room for improvement.
  22| Lunenburg High School - 2018 MCAS Results (Grade 10)
  23| Subject Test Advanced/Proficient State Average (Adv/Pro)
  24| ELA 98% 91%
  25| Math 83% 78%
  26| Science/Technology 87% 74%
  27| We are extremely proud of our students and teachers for their continued improvement, and we will
  28| work to move all students to the level of proficiency as measured by MCAS and our goal of college
  29| and career readiness as they move through our curriculum.
  30| We were also very pleased to learn that 29 seniors at Lunenburg High School received the state’s
  31| John and Abigail Adams Scholarship this year.  The scholarship is available to students whose
  32| MCAS performance puts them at the top 25 percent of their district.  To be eligible, students must
  33| either score Advanced on one exam and Proficient on the other, or Advanced on both the English
  34| and Math exams.

===PAGE 135=== (layout, 49 lines)
   1|  Schools across North Central Massachusetts are filled with eager students, talented educators and sup-
   2|  portive administrators, all working together toward common goals:  student achievement and academic
   3|  excellence.  While students and teachers at Monty Tech work together toward these goals daily, they
   4|  also strive to achieve vocational-technical proficiency, assuring our graduates have a choice when they
   5|  graduate:  to pursue college or a career or both.  As college costs continue to rise, and the need for
   6|  qualified tradespeople is expanding across our region, having this choice is becoming increasingly im-
   7|  portant.  Preparing our students to make this choice is a privilege, and so we remain committed to
   8|  providing the best possible educational programs for our students.
   9|  Monty Tech academic programs are effectively serving students at all levels, as indicated on the most
  10|  recent MCAS scores:  95% of students earned Advanced or Proficient scores in ELA, 85% earned Ad-
  11|  vanced or Proficient scores in mathematics, and 82% earned Advanced or Proficient scores in Biology.
  12|  Student  performance  on  this  exam  met  or  exceeded  state-determined  targets  in  every  subgroup.
  13|  Teachers continue to refine curriculum, incorporate new instructional strategies, and challenge every
  14|  student to reach new levels, and we couldn’t be prouder of the results.
  15|  School leaders also closely monitor each of the school’s 21 vocational programs, to ensure they align
  16|  with current industry standards, and that they continue to prepare students for entry-level careers in
  17|  their chosen field.  Whether it means purchasing new equipment, incorporating new certifications or
  18|  credentials into the curriculum, or seeking new educational partners, vocational instructors and school
  19|  leaders work collaboratively to ensure vocational programs at Monty Tech are among the most com-
  20|  prehensive, current, and innovative programs across the Commonwealth.
  21|  The 2017-2018 school year was marked by countless reasons to celebrate our students and staff, who
  22|  consistently demonstrate vocational excellence and innovation.
  23|  Students  and  instructors  in  our  Carpentry,  Electrical,  Plumbing,  Masonry,  HVAC  and  Property
  24|  Maintenance, and Cabinetmaking trades completed a two-year long project – a 7,500 sq. ft. building,
  25|  now home to the school’s newest vocational program, Veterinary Science.  This project was funded
  26|  entirely by private sector donations, gifts, local, state and federal grants, and in-kind support in the
  27|  form of donated materials and labor, and deep discounts on building materials and supplies.  School
  28|  officials raised an unprecedented $2.6M to support the establishment of the Monty Tech Veterinary
  29|  Science program - a tremendous accomplishment that reflects the support our community continues to
  30|  provide our students, faculty and staff.
  31|  The Monty Tech Veterinary Clinic is not the only new and exciting opportunity available to our stu-
  32|  dents.  The school was recently awarded a $500,000 Skills Capital Grant, to improve the Engineering
  33|  Technology, Electrical, and CAD/Drafting & Design programs.  This generous grant has allowed in-
  34|  structors to create two innovative learning spaces, intended to replicate working and learning environ-
  35|  ments maintained by industry leaders in advanced manufacturing and automation.  Using this new
  36|  equipment, students will prepare to become manufacturing technicians, robotics technicians, automa-
  37|  tion technicians, moldmakers, and CNC machinists at local corporations, filling the existing skills gap
  38|  across these industries.
  39|  While students and instructors across the school continue to demonstrate creativity, innovation, and
  40|  leadership, school leaders continue to keep an eye on emerging trends in vocational education.  As we
  41|  prepare these talented students to make important life choices, we are reminded that in the end, the
  42|  choice is theirs to make:  college or career or both.  It is our job to support their aspirations and to edu-
  43|  cate and train them to achieve at the highest possible level.  On behalf of our talented educators and
  44|  administrators,  who  remain  focused  on  delivering  the  highest  quality  academic  and  vocational-
  45|  technical education possible, I am delighted to present the District’s 2017-2018 annual report to you,
  46|  providing a snapshot of the wonderful experiences happening on a day-to-day basis here at Monty
  47|  Tech.
  48|  Respectfully submitted,
  49|                                                               135

   1| 136
   2| Our Mission
   3| Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School with the skills, knowledge, and
   4| abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.
   5| Our District
   6| Montachusett Regional Vocational  Technical School is a four -year career and technical high school serving the
   7| member towns of:
   8| Ashburnham
   9| Ashby
  10| Athol
  11| Barre
  12| Fitchburg
  13| Gardner
  14| Harvard
  15| Holden
  16| Hubbardston
  17| Lunenburg
  18| Petersham
  19| Phillipston
  20| Princeton
  21| Royalston
  22| Sterling
  23| Templeton
  24| Westminster
  25| Winchendon
  26| Leadership
  27| The leadership team at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is comprised of ten talented
  28| administrators whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas  of expertise contribute
  29| to the success of the school. Working collaboratively, and under the direction of the Superintendent and
  30| Principal, the team has transformed the scho ol into one of the most sought -after high schools in North Central
  31| Massachusetts.
  32| Sheila M. Harrity, Superintendent-Director
  33| Tom Browne, Principal
  34| Dayana Carlson, Assistant Principal
  35| Tammy Crockett, Business Manager
  36| Pamela Pothier, Director of Technology
  37| Christina Favreau, Director of Academic Programs
  38| Jim Hachey, Director of Vocational Programs
  39| Michael Gormley, Director of Facilities
  40| Katy Whitaker, Development Coordinator
  41| Victoria Zarozinski, Director of Student Support Services
  42|                                                       Enrollment
  43| On June 1, 2018, student enrollment at Monty Tech included 1,387 students in grades nine through twelve, rep-
  44| resenting each of the district ’s eighteen sending communities. Each class of students is comprised of a relatively
  45| equal balance of male vs. female students, and each student attending Monty Tech has elected to do so, prepared
  46| to explore an interest in one of the school’s twenty-one rigorous vocational-technical programs. While the school
  47| currently offers only five programs non -traditional for male students, and twelve programs non -traditional for
  48| female students, we are proud to have a student population that is well - balanced by gender and a variety of aca-
  49| demic interests and achievements.

===PAGE 137=== (plain, 75 lines)
   1| 137
   2| Throughout 2017-2018, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and community members
   3| to learn about and visit the school. In October 2017, approximately 700 district eighth graders participated in the
   4| annual “Tour Day” event. Students toured our twenty vocational - technical areas and learned about the school’s
   5| challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered
   6| interested students the opportunity to return in the  evening with their family members to further explore the facilities
   7| and speak with staff members.
   8| The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit Monty Tech
   9| after school and participate in hands -on l earning experiences across a variety of vocational/technical areas. The
  10| program continued to attract a large number of students during the 2017 -2018 school year, serving approximately 600
  11| area students.
  12| Class of 2018 Awards
  13| Members of the Class of 2018 we re awarded approximately $97,000 in scholarships. The Monty Tech Foundation
  14| generously provided $37,250 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $100 to $2,600. The
  15| Foundation also awarded $3,240 to the Practical Nursing graduates. Once  again, local and state organizations, as well
  16| as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of
  17| financial donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates themselves, are  grateful for this
  18| support.
  19| Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher education.
  20| Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a number of articulation agre ements with
  21| public and private colleges across the country. Approximately 57% of the graduating class of 2018 reported plans to
  22| enroll at a 4 -year college/university upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these
  23| students will save both time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
  24| Financial Report
  25| A great deal of effort was put forth by the School Committee, administration, and staff to develop a cost - effective
  26| budget for the fiscal year 2017 -2018.  The final  fiscal year 2017 -2018 Educational Plan totaled $26,895,680, which
  27| represents a 2.0% increase over the 2016 -2017 Educational Plan. The District’s FY17 budget exceeds the minimum
  28| spending required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by only $41,379 or .2%.
  29| The District was audited in November 2018 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of Melanson,
  30| Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA, and received a very good report.

===PAGE 138=== (plain, 49 lines)
   1| 138
   2| Grants and Contracts
   3| Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds help provide many educational and
   4| social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2018, state and federal grant sources provided the school with
   5| $991,611. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health Services, Social Intervention and Mediation,
   6| Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins
   7| Occupational Education, and Marine Corp  Junior Reserve Officer Training.  The District also received more than
   8| $508,000 in competitive grants, including a Skills Capital Grant for $495,000 to purchase equipment for the Electrical,
   9| Engineering and CAD/Drafting and Design programs, and a grant fr om the Community Foundation of North Central
  10| Massachusetts in the amount of $13,919 to expand the school’s continuing education program to include an Oil Burner
  11| Technician training program. Using these allocation and competitive funds, the school was able to purchase a variety
  12| of instructional technology, equipment, and supplies to enhance the  learning experience across both academic and
  13| vocational programs.
  14| Academic Achievement
  15| In 2017 -2018, students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School  continued to demonstrate high
  16| academic achievement, earning commendable MCAS scores in English Language Arts, Mathematics and Biology. For
  17| Spring 2018, Monty Tech’s passing rate on the English Language Arts was 100%, Mathematics 96%, Biology 98%.
  18| Monty Tech is committed to providing students with rigorous STEM learning opportunities, and so in recent years, the
  19| curriculum has been expanded to include Advanced Placement Programs (two ELA courses, two mathematics courses,
  20| two science courses, and one comput er science course) and teachers have participated in meaningful, high -quality
  21| professional development which will enable us to roll -out two Project Lead the Way biomedical courses in the 2018 -
  22| 2019 school year.
  23| The school is in its 6th year of administering Advanced Placement exams, and students enrolled in AP courses
  24| continue to improve their performance.  In 2017-2018, Monty Tech had forty-six students achieve qualifying scores,
  25| earning advanced standing and/or college credit.  More than half of the students who tested in the following subjects
  26| received qualifying scores: AP Computer Science Principles (66%), AP Chemistry (53%), and AP BC Calculus
  27| (100%).  In addition, the AP Chemistry program experienced the most growth in terms of qualifying score as their
  28| passing rate increased by 44.7% from 2017 to 2018.

  11| Vocational Projects in the District Communities
  12| Unlike students in traditional, comprehensive high schools, students at Monty Tech are asked to put their educa-
  13| tion into practice on a daily basis.  Students across the twenty different vocational -technical education programs
  14| are building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service vehicles, making walkways more ac-
  15| cessible, and performing countless community services.
  16| The 2017-2018 school year was a busy one for our vocational educators, as each trade aimed to provide practi-
  17| cal, hands-on learning experiences for our students, while helping to improve each of the eighteen member com-
  18| munities.
  19| Auto Body Collision Repair Technology :  The Monty Tech Auto Body shop benefited from new instructional
  20| equipment this year.  Eight new welding stations were installed, allowing instructors to expand the welding cur-
  21| riculum in an effort to better align with industry standards.  New textbooks and a new nitrogen welder were criti-
  22| cal as instructors worked to implement a more extensive plastic welding program.  In addition to the 100+ com-
  23| munity service projects completed last year, students and instructors repaired a trailer for the Westminster Police
  24| and clear coated the 9/11 Memorial for the Fitchburg Fire Department.  Seniors received their EPA 6 H Certifi-
  25| cate, I-Car Pro Level 1 in Refinishing and Non -Structural Repair. And, for the fourth consecutive year, Monty
  26| Tech Auto Body students won the coveted World of Wheels Pedal car competition.  Seven students earned co -op
  27| placements, and one Senior traveled to Louisville, KY, competing in the SkillsUSA national competition in June
  28| 2018. Instructors are incredibly proud of his performance, as he was won at the national level and has qualified
  29| to compete in the Collision Repair World Competition in Russia, scheduled for summer 2019. (Total student
  30| enrollment: 65)
  31| Automotive Technology:  As in past years, the Monty Tech Automotive Technology program continues to ser-
  32| vice a variety of vehicles.  Over 400 vehicles were serviced and repaired during the 2017 -2018 school year, in-
  33| cluding vehicles brought in by faculty, staff, public, school and students.  Students performed extensive repairs,
  34| to include head gaskets, timing belts, and various other technical repairs.  Thanks for funding provided by the
  35| school’s Perkins grant, every student enrolled in the program earned at least one industry -recognized certifica-
  36| tion, and Senior students were able to participate in the widely renown ASE Testing.  A total of eight students
  37| earned co-op placements throughout the school year, which is a testament to the strength of the program, that
  38| continues to be the areas premier workforce pipeline for area automotive dealerships and businesses.  (Total stu-
  39| dent enrollment: 70)
  40| Business Technology:  Monty Tech’s Business Technology program continues to benefit from a unique partner-
  41| ship with Workers Credit Union.  The opportunity to participate in teller training and financial literacy work-
  42| shops has been met with great enthusiasm from students, who completed 9 modules and earned a financial litera-
  43| cy certificate.  As a member of the Massachusetts Schools Bank Association through the Federal Reserve Bank
  44| of Boston, Monty Tech students are able to compete in the Banking Bowl and Marketing Competitions. Monty
  45| Tech Business Technology instructors, in collaboration with their post -secondary colleagues, continue to refine
  46| and develop new articulation agreements.  These agreements, with Mount Wachusett Community College and
  47| New England Institute of Technology, will provide qualified students with college credits at no cost to the stu-
  48| dent.  Students are eligible to receive 18 credits from Mount Wachusett Community College and 16 credits from
  49| New England Institute of Technology. Opportunities to demonstrate customer service, cash handling, and ac-
  50| counting skills are ever-present as the Business Technology students successfully operate an in -house retail loca-
  51| tion, The Gear House School Store, and the Monty Tech Greenhouse.  Seven Seniors and nine Juniors were out
  52| on co-op placements during the 2017-2018 school year.  (Total student enrollment: 68)

===PAGE 140=== (layout, 55 lines)
   1| Cabinetmaking:  Students and instructors in the school’s Cabinetmaking program spent a majority of their time
   2| during the 2017-2018 school year manufacturing, assembling and installing classroom cabinets, clinic casework,
   3| and solid surface countertops in the school’s new Veterinary Science training center.   Sophomore students craft-
   4| ed 400 maple bread boards with a purple heart accent strip for guests at the 2018 Superintendent's Dinner.  Up-
   5| perclassmen fabricated two credenzas and a bench for Mount Wachusett Community College.  These unique
   6| pieces were made from the reclaimed maple trees that were cut from the site of their new science wing.  Students
   7| and instructors completed an additional 40+ projects throughout the school and surrounding communities, to in-
   8| clude:  three canopy gutter enclosures for the Veterinary Center, a wall of upper cabinets and a computer station
   9| for the Electrical shop, two base cabinets and maple doors for Machine Technology, and 36 cosmetology nail
  10| tables for Cosmetology.  A total of 14 students (9 seniors and 5 juniors) earned co-op placements.  (Total stu-
  11| dent  enrollment: 70)
  12|  CAD/Drafting & Design:  The Monty Tech CAD/Drafting & Design program is increasingly busy each year
  13| with outside projects, completing miscellaneous signs and banners for community sports events, craft fairs, and
  14| non-profit events.  Students also played an instrumental role in 2018 Superintendent's Dinner, designing the lay-
  15| out and décor for this annual fundraising event, and ended the year by designing and installing signage through-
  16| out the building for the 2018 graduation exercises.  A total of six students were awarded co                  -op placements, work-
  17| ing and learning in area businesses.  Four students advanced to the SkillsUSA states competition, representing the
  18| program well.  Over the course of the 2017-2018 school year, CAD/Drafting & Design students achieved over
  19| 100 industry-recognized certifications in AutoCAD, Inventor, Revit, and Solidworks.  Program instructors were
  20| instrumental in the success of the school’s 2018 Skills Capital Grant application, and, as a result, the school was
  21| awarded $500,000.  A portion of these funds will be used to establish a fabrication lab for 3D printing, 3D scan-
  22| ning, Injection molding, CNC work, and Laser cutting. (Total student enrollment: 62)
  23|  Cosmetology:  The Monty Tech Cosmetology program welcomed a new instructor for the 2017-2018 school
  24| year, Stephanie Delphia, whose considerable experience in the field, and business acumen as a salon owner,
  25| brought fresh perspective and valuable lessons to the students enrolled in the program.  Students and instructors
  26| in the program provided numerous community services, including basic manicures to the residents at Heywood
  27| Wakefield Commons, services to parents who work with disadvantaged children, and numerous walk                              -in clients.
  28| Senior students held three staff appreciation nights, raising funds so that they could travel to New York's Interna-
  29| tional Hair show.  Eleven seniors earned co-op placements - the largest number of Cosmetology students placed
  30| in the program’s history.  Finally, for the second consecutive year, every senior successfully passed the Massa-
  31| chusetts Cosmetology State Boards, earning licenses to practice.  (Total student enrollment: 87)
  32| Culinary Arts:  Monty Tech Culinary Arts students and instructors are always busy with the daily operation of the
  33| Mountain Room Restaurant, which is open for lunch from 11:30 am to 1:00 pm Monday through Friday.                               In addi-
  34| tion to operating a full-service restaurant and bakery, serving 90-120 patrons daily, culinary students showcase
  35| their talents throughout the year, providing outstanding service at events that include the following: two Program
  36| Advisory Committee dinner meetings, four Monty Tech Foundation breakfasts, Monty Tech’s Retirees holiday
  37| luncheon, a graduation reception, the Women in Technology event, Principal and Counselors’ Day, the Monty
  38| Tech homecoming dance, MAVA meetings, all School Committee meetings and sub                            -committee meetings, Gard-
  39| ner AARP luncheon and MHCC volunteer luncheon.                    Community service opportunities for students are always a
  40| highlight, and this year our students prepared meals for Our Father               ’s House, and NEADS events.  Students and
  41| instructors also prepared and donated pastry and baked goods to the House of Peace and Education in Gardner.
  42| The program’s greatest undertaking is always the Annual Superintendent                  ’s Dinner scholarship fundraiser, during
  43| which students work side       -by-side with some of the area’s finest chefs, preparing a six-course dinner with exten-
  44| sive hors d’oeuvres for more than three hundred guests.  Culinary Arts students value industry-recognized creden-
  45| tials, and were proud to earn Allergen Awareness and ServeSafe Certifications. A senior student was recognized
  46| by the Massachusetts Restaurant Association with a $10,000 Scholarship to Johnson & Wales University. (Total
  47| student enrollment: 95)
  48|  Dental Assisting:  A critical component of the Monty Tech Dental Assisting curriculum is the preparation for the
  49| Dental Assisting National Board certification exams.  Every sophomore passed the DANB Infection Control ex-
  50| am, and every senior passed the DANB Radiology exam.  In addition, all senior students successfully completed
  51| two full-mouth x-rays on live patients during the affiliation experience, meeting a state requirement for program
  52| completion.  Three students (one senior and two juniors) earned co-op placements, while the remaining 22 stu-

  32| Co-operative Education and Student Placement

===PAGE 144=== (layout, 53 lines)
   1|  The Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student’s technical education that combines class-
   2|  room instruction with on-the-job-training. The Co-op Program provides students the opportunity to further de-
   3|  velop academic, technical and employability skills in an industry work environment. All students are eligible to
   4|  participate in Co-op Program, provided they satisfy state and school grade, attendance and performance require-
   5|  ments. All Co-op students have completed the OSHA recognized Career Safe online health and safety course.
   6|  The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both student and employer. For the student, it is a chance
   7|  to gain on-the job training that improves both their technical and employability skills. Additionally, making in-
   8|  dustry connections enhances post-graduate employment opportunities. The Co-op Program allows students to
   9|  earn while they learn.
  10|  Employer benefits include addressing workforce needs in an efficient manner. Coop students provide a pool of
  11|  temporary and potential full-time employees who are already trained, thus reducing employer training costs. Co-
  12|  op work hours may be tailored to suit the needs of partnering employers. During school year 2017-2018, approx-
  13|  imately 25% of qualified students (Juniors and Seniors) earned Co-op placements, working, learning and apply-
  14|  ing their technical skills related in area businesses.
  15|                                                        Student Support Services
  16|  During the 2017-2018 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided spe-
  17|  cial services to approximately three hundred students              – measuring progress of over two hundred students on
  18|  Individual Education Plans (IEPs) and just under one hundred students adhering to individualized Section 504
  19|  plans. While the Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses special education, the department
  20|  provides support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
  21|  The department includes a full        -time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state              -mandated health
  22|  screening exams, and provides, when necessary, health information to the special education team for a student                            ’s
  23|  IEP meeting. The department benefits from a full             -time school social worker that participates in departmental
  24|  meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family issues, homelessness, maternity,
  25|  health issues, and proper food and clothing. The school is also fortunate to have on staff a full                   -time psychologist,
  26|  whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an initial evaluation or who require a three                 - year re-
  27|  evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time speech language pathologist, who is available to assist students with
  28|  disabilities, assess these students and consult with teachers. Our students also have access to the services of a
  29|  full-time adjustment counselor and part          -time school psychologist. All of these individuals are available for
  30|  scheduled counseling sessions, mental health emergency treatment, medical/mental health re                         -entry and transition
  31|  support and services, as well as crisis intervention.
  32|  The school’s Director of Student Support Services oversees the District                 ’s Special Education Program, which is
  33|  reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The comprehensive review and evalua-
  34|  tion are done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the evaluation are used to
  35|  improve the special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical
  36|  School.

  39|  workforce.  Through the school’s association with SkillsUSA, our students develop job skills, leadership and
  40|  professional skills, as well as provide community service.  The 2017                 -2018 school year was an extraordinary one
  41|  for our chapter of Skills USA. The students met the challenges of district competitions, performed well at the
  42|  state level, and prepared for the national conference.  They also conducted several community service projects
  43|  and raised money for various deserving charities.   Serving as co               -advisors were Michael Forhan, Auto Body

  45|  Instructor.  Highlights of the year include:

  49|       the Annual Fall State Leadership Conference, where they participated in workshops and leadership exercis-

  15|                                                                Technology
  16| The 2017-2018 school year brought increased access to technology for all students, through the school’s popular
  17| 1:1 Chromebook initiative.  Google Cloud Print and numerous printers located throughout the building provide
  18| students with options to print or share their work with classroom instructors.  Two Chromebook charging stations,
  19| located in the cafeteria and library, give an added layer to support to students who may need to charge their devic-
  20| es throughout the day.
  21| An outstanding Instructional Technologist, Ellen Gammel, was hired, bringing expertise across a broad spectrum,
  22| to include educational technology, software, and troubleshooting tips.  Several members of the school’s leadership
  23| team joined Ms. Gammel, traveling to Orlando, FL to attend the annual Future of Education Technology Confer-
  24| ence, with the goal of identifying new solutions, technologies, and best practices that will enhance the school’s
  25| technology-driven approach to education.
  26| To support the 1:1 Chromebook initiative and other technology needs, Monty Tech successfully completed a num-
  27| ber of necessary infrastructure upgrades, using federal e-rate dollars totaling $109,114.70.  The school’s external
  28| bandwidth speeds have been increased to 1.5 GB and internal bandwidth to 10GB to better ensure continued con-
  29| nectivity and speeds appropriate with a high–use learning community.
  30|                                                              Marine Corps JROTC
  31| The Monty Tech Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officer Training Corps (MCJROTC) had an extraordinary 2017-
  32| 2018 school year.  Perhaps the most noteworthy accomplishment was the cadet corps’ remarkable response to
  33| Hurricane Harvey.  With the support of the surround communities, the cadets were able to raise almost $100,000
  34| in cash and materiel for relief efforts.  With the assistance of the United States Marine Corps, 56 cadets and 15
  35| adults flew to the Houston area where they spent 7 days working on homes that were damaged by the storm.
  36|  The Monty Tech JROTC Cadet Corps was honored to host the Wreaths Across America Convoy with a remem-
  37| brance ceremony as it made its way down to Arlington National Cemetery.
  38| The 2017-2018 Corps of Cadets completed over 10,000 hours of community service, conducting a major food
  39| drive with United Way, working side-by-side with the Marine Corps Reserve in a national Toys for Tots cam-
  40| paign, and spending six weekends working with the local Salvation Army helping to raise over $25,000 for needy
  41| families.
  42| During the summer of 2018, our Cadet Cyber Team was asked to support a Cyber STEM camp for the National
  43| Marine Corps JROTC program.  The Cyber Team coordinated the 8-day camp that focused on defending cyber
  44| networks from attacks. The camp also offered an introduction to robotics programming using the VEX Robotics
  45| System and computer programming using the CanaKit Raspberry Pi 3’s. The camp was attended by 120 students,
  46| representing twelve states, and was a wonderful success for all involved.

   1| 147
   2|                                      Monty Tech School of Continuing Education
   3| The Monty Tech School of Continuing Education continues to update and add courses that emphasize a com-
   4| mitment to excellence by offering affordable, quality, and enjoyable educational experiences. For the Fall of
   5| 2017, Monty Tech offered 140 classes with 871 registrations, and during the Spring 2018 semester, there were
   6| 114 post-graduate and continuing studies courses, with 671 registrations.
   7| The program is on track to support North Central Massachusetts workforce readiness pipeline with an increase
   8| in career and licensure courses.  The students earn industry-recognized credentials, and participate in extern-
   9| ships to support the hands on learning component.  We continue to increase our small business partnerships,
  10| and the number and quality of personal enrichment classes - sewing, knitting, acrylic painting and glass fusing,
  11| to name a few. Catalog distribution has more than doubled, from 80,000 to 170,000 copies, and the relatively
  12| new online registration feature continues to add value to our students and instructors.
  13| Spring 2019 will see an Oil Burner Technician certification prep courses come to fruition. This will provide
  14| our community members with a high quality, affordable entry into a viable career pathway. Upon completion
  15| of the 120-hour program, students will have the opportunity to sit for the oil burner technician state licensing
  16| exam, and begin their work in the oil burner industry.
  17| The continued success and sustainability of an adult education program is based on hiring quality instructors
  18| and meeting the needs of the community and supporting workforce development. Our goal in the coming year
  19| includes increasing our adult education course offerings, with a continued concentration on career programs.
  20| Stay tuned for Pharmacy Technician, Medical Billing and Coding, Manufacturing Boot Camp and Apprentice-
  21| ship opportunities at the Monty Tech School of Continuing Education.
  22|                                                            Practical Nursing Program
  23| The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely, ethically and in
  24| a caring manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status in diverse health care
  25| settings.  On June 28, 2018, a graduating class of 35 students completed the Practical Nursing Program and
  26| entered the nursing profession. The class achieved an initial pass rate of 77%, with 27 of the 35 graduates pass-
  27| ing the NCLEX-PN exam.  Five graduates are waiting to take their NCLEX- PN exam, and three graduates are
  28| expected to retake the examination in the coming months.
  29| All of the 2018 graduates are currently employed in the health care profession throughout Massachusetts, and
  30| many are working within the eighteen cities and towns of the Monty Tech school district as LPNs in various
  31| health care settings, ranging from long term care, sub-acute care to mental health facilities.
  32| The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program continues to develop the “LPN to BSN Bridge” relationship with
  33| Fitchburg State University. Several 2018 graduates are pursuing seats in the LPN to BSN program at FSU and
  34| will be continuing their education to the Bachelors in Nursing. Many class applicants cited their attraction to
  35| Monty Tech as the opportunity to complete a ten-month accelerated program and then bridge to Fitchburg
  36| State University.
  37| Monty Tech Practical Nursing students are now completing patient scenarios in the Sim Lab on a weekly basis
  38| in Terms 2 and 3. The Faculty Sim team has implemented National League of Nursing (NLN) patient scenarios
  39| consistent with our curriculum frameworks, and have also utilized the Sim lab setting to instruct students in
  40| developing nursing skills that they may not be experiencing in the clinical setting.
  41| Instructors have successfully developed and implemented a Maternity Sim lab boot camp where students uti-
  42| lize the new simulation models to simulate the birthing process. Student feedback has been positive regarding
  43| these new maternity scenarios, stating it better prepares them for an unexpected outcome during a delivery.
  44| Substance abuse and the mental health setting job opportunities have increased with more funding being pro-
  45| vided to combat the opiate crisis.  Having a clinical rotation at AdCare Hospital in Worcester, and performing
  46| simulation scenarios involving substance abuse clients, has better prepared our graduates to work in a sub-
  47| stance abuse clinical setting, as well.
  48|                                                                  Looking Ahead
  49| While the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District educational community is certainly
  50| proud of the achievements of our talented students, faculty, and staff, we continue to have an eye toward the
  51| future, always committed to improving our vocational and academic programming, strengthening key partner-
  52| ships, and maintaining facilities that contribute to student success and achievement. As we look ahead, there

===PAGE 148=== (layout, 49 lines)
   1| are a number of programs and initiatives that we expect will have a positive impact on our school and students for
   2| years to come.
   3| Expand science offerings to include new biomedical courses:  For several months, school officials worked to
   4| launch an exciting new biomedical science program.  Using grant funds, a science lab was renovated,                        transform-
   5| ing it into a biomedical science laboratory, aligned with current industry standards.  In addition, one talented in-
   6| structor, Mr. Dylan Hager, attended four weeks of intensive teacher training (2 weeks during summer 2017 at the
   7| University of San Diego, and 2 weeks during the summer of 2018 at the University of Washington).  While Mr.
   8| Hager spent weeks preparing this exciting new coursework, school officials purchased the supplies and materials
   9| necessary to deliver two rigorous Project Lead the Way courses, Human Body Systems and Principles of Biomed-
  10| ical Sciences.  These courses, which will be available to students Fall 2018, are expected to engage students in a
  11| variety of hands-on learning opportunities in a newly renovated laboratory environment that replicates industry
  12| standards.  Students will be trained in the safe and effective use of the same tools that professionals in hospitals
  13| and laboratories are currently using and will work together to find solutions to real-world problems by analyzing
  14| medical histories, reading and interpreting autopsy reports, and understanding basic biology and human physiolo-
  15| gy.
  16| Celebrate the completion of the school’s newest instructional space:  For two years, students and instructors in our
  17| Carpentry, Electrical, Plumbing, Masonry, HVAC and Property Maintenance, and Cabinetmaking trades worked
  18| to complete an incredible project – a 7,500 sq. ft. building, which would become home to the school’s newest vo-
  19| cational program, Veterinary Science.  With construction of this state-of-the-art facility completed during the
  20| summer months, school officials celebrated the finished product with local and state officials, donors, and com-
  21| munity members who helped bring the innovative animal science program to Monty Tech.  While the facility will
  22| not be open to serve pets until Fall 2019, Monty Tech Veterinary Science students will work and learn in the new
  23| space in the 2018-2019 school year, preparing for a successful opening September 2019.
  24| Expand post-secondary and continuing education opportunities:  Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical
  25| School is home to a very popular evening program that, in recent years, has expanded to include countless work-
  26| force readiness programs.  A generous grant provided by the Community Foundation of North Central Massachu-
  27| setts has enabled school officials to begin developing a new, meaningful workforce training program that will
  28| have an immediate and positive impact on regional workforce needs.  In 2018-2019, an Oil Burner Technician
  29| Certification course will be made available to non-traditional students with an interest in pursuing entry-level ca-
  30| reers in HVAC trades.   This evening program, the only such course in North Central Massachusetts, will include
  31| three industry-recognized certification opportunities and will undoubtedly benefit dislocated workers who have
  32| been interested in related careers but unable to participate in the required training, due to lack of offerings or high
  33| costs associated with similar training programs.
  34| Expand the school’s marketing efforts: A Monty Tech high school experience is one students must choose, and to
  35| ensure that every interested student in our sending district has access to information needed to make sound deci-
  36| sions regarding his/her high school experience, it is imperative that the school’s marketing efforts are expanded to
  37| include a variety of social media platforms.  While the school currently hosts an updated website and Facebook
  38| page, school officials are exploring adding Twitter and Instagram accounts, in an effort to continue to engage in-
  39| terested students and parents.
  40|                                                      Respectfully Submitted By:
  41|                                        Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D., Superintendent-Director

   1|                                   ANNUAL TOWN MEETING WARRANT May 5, 2018
   2| At 9:06 a.m. the Annual Town Meeting was called to order. The Moderator declared warrant properly served
   3| and a quorum was declared. After the pledge, the National Anthem was sung by the Lunenburg High School
   4| acappella choir. The Moderator reminded the audience to be sure to pick up the handouts available in the hall-
   5| way for various articles; he also mentioned that the 130 pages of the Planning Board article were available to
   6| look at by the left side of the stage. Laura Brzozski from the Cultural Council spoke about the Arts Festival hap-
   7| pening on June 29, 2018. Chairman Toale recognized Paula Bertram for her service to the Town as a member of
   8| the Board of Selectmen for four terms. Paula then thanked Town Meeting members. The School Committee rec-
   9| ognized retired Supt. Calmes for her service to the town. At 9:15 a.m., the Moderator went over the ground rules
  10| (cell phones, use of the microphone, etc.) using Town Meeting Time.
  11| ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to hear and/or accept the regular written reports of the Town Officers
  12| and Committees; or take any other action relative thereto. (Submitted by Board of Selectmen) (Board of Select-
  13| men recommends approval.) (Finance Committee reports no direct financial impact) Article 1 was PASSED
  14| OVER. (Reports already accepted)
  15| ARTICLE 2.  To see if the Town will vote to amend the general by-laws of Town by adding a new section to
  16| establish and authorize revolving funds for use by certain town departments, boards, committees, agencies or
  17| officers under Massachusetts General Laws Chapter 44, § 53E½, or take any other action relative thereto.
  18| (Submitted by the Town Manager) (Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee recommends approval)
  19| Amend the Bylaws of the Town by adding the following new section:
  20| Departmental Revolving Funds
  21| 1. Purpose.  This by-law establishes and authorizes revolving funds for use by town, departments, boards, com-
  22| mittees, agencies and officers in connection with the operation of programs or activities that generate fees,
  23| charges or other receipts to support all or some of the expenses of those programs or activities.  These revolving
  24| funds are established under and governed by General Laws Chapter 44, §53E ½.
  25| 2. Expenditure Limitations.  A department or agency head, board, committee or officer may incur liabilities
  26| against and spend monies from a revolving fund established and authorized by this by-law without appropriation
  27| subject to the following limitations:
  28|     A. Fringe benefits of full-time employees whose salaries or wages are paid from the fund shall also be paid f
  29|         from the fund.
  30|     B. No liability shall be incurred in excess of the available balance in the fund.
  31|     C. The total amount spent during the year shall not exceed the amount authorized by Annual Town Meeting
  32|          on or before July 1 of that fiscal year, or any increased amount of that authorization that is later approved
  33|          during that fiscal year by the Selectmen and Finance Committee.
  34| 3.    Interest.  Interest earned on monies credited to a revolving fund established by this by-law shall be credited
  35|      to the general fund.
  36| 4.   Procedures and Reports.  Except as provided in General Laws Chapter 44, §53E ½ and this by-law, the
  37|      laws, charter provisions, by-laws, rules, regulations, policies or procedures that govern the receipt and cus-
  38|      tody of town monies and the expenditures and payment of town funds shall apply to the use of a revolving
  39|      fund established and authorized by this by-law.  The Town Accountant shall include a statement on the col-
  40|      lections credited to the fund, the encumbrances and expenditures charged to each fund and the balance
  41|      available for expenditure in the regular report the Town Accountant provides the department, board, com-
  42|      mittee, agency or officer on appropriations made for its use.
  43| 5.   Authorized Revolving Funds.  The Table establishes:
  44| A.   Each revolving fund authorized for use by a town department, board, committee, agency or officer,
  45| B.   The department or agency head, board, committee or officer authorized to spend from each fund,
  46| C.   The fees, charges and other monies charged and received by the department, board, committee, agency or
  47|      officer in connection with the program or activity for which the fund is established that shall be credited to
  48|      each fund by the Town Accountant,
  49| D. The expenses of the program or activity for which each fund may be used,
  50| E. Any restrictions or conditions on expenditures from each fund;
  51|                                                               152

===PAGE 153=== (plain, 92 lines)
   1| 153
   2| F. Any reporting or other requirements that apply to each fund, and
   3| G. The fiscal years each fund shall operate under this by-law.
   4| VOTED UNANIMOUSLY with Amendment to remove italicized sections E, F & G from #5.

  48| Town of Lunenburg, to be appointed by the Selectmen and Town Manager, and each member shall
  49| serve for a term of three years; provided, however, that of the members originally appointed, one shall
  50| serve a term of one year, two for a term of two years, and two for a term of three years, as designated
  51| by the Selectmen and Town Manager. However, nothing herein shall be deemed to prohibit the chair-
  52| man of the Personnel Committee from designating a member of said committee to serve as a
  53|                                                             162

===PAGE 163=== (layout, 49 lines)
   1| representative of said Committee on a study committee of the Town Meeting, nor shall such service
   2| affect the tenure of said member of the Personnel Committee. If any member of the Personnel Com-
   3| mittee shall resign or otherwise vacate the office before the expiration of their term, the successor shall
   4| be appointed by the Selectmen and Town Moderator, at their discretion, to serve for the balance of the
   5|  unexpired term.

   7| member serve as an ex officio member of the Personnel Committee and may be called upon from time
   8| to time to provide information requested by the Personnel Committee so that the Personnel Committee
   9| can make well informed decisions and proposals.

  11| other information relating to the personnel of Town departments                  other than the School Department,

  13| sonnel Committee, in such a form as said Committee may require.
  14| SECTION 17.  PAID VACATIONS
  15| No vacation time may be used in advance of the July 1 date on which it would normally be credited;
  16| except that where such advance served the needs both of the employee and of the Town, it may be re-
  17| quested by the Department Head and authorized in writing by Town Manager.  In no case shall any
  18| such advanced vacation exceed the number of days for which the employee would be entitled to com-
  19| pensation, were he/she to terminate their services in good standing on the first day of such vacation                   , in
  20| accordance with the provisions of paragraph 5, subparagraph (b), of this section                .
  21| SECTION 25.  LONGEVITY PAY
  22| After reaching ten (10) years of employment, and thereafter, each employee permanently employed,
  23| whether full or part time, shall receive an annual bonus equal to 2% of their annual salary                    payable in
  24| the first pay period after the Employeest      ’s anniversary date     , and is payable in the first pay period after
  25| July 1  . At 15 years of continuous service        – 2.5% of their annual salary, and at 20 years of continuous
  26| service – 3% of their annual salary.
