  28|  § 250-4.15 13 Solar energy systems.
  29|  A. Purpose. The purpose of this section is to provide siting for solar photovoltaic energy systems for
  30|  power generation for all home, commercial or industrial installations. Small                  -scale ground-mounted
  31|  photovoltaic installations, roof      -mounted solar energy installations and large           -scale ground-mounted
  32|  photovoltaic installations within the Solar Overlay District may be constructed without need for dis-
  33|  cretionary approval as set forth herein. Large          -scale ground    -mounted photovoltaic installations out-
  34|  side of the Solar Overlay District may be constructed by special permit, provided certain criteria as
  35|  set forth herein are met. The provisions in this section of the Zoning Bylaw shall apply to the con-
  36|  struction, operation, repair, and/or removal of all solar electric systems, and to physical modifica-
  37|  tions that materially alter the type, configuration, or size of these installations or related equipment.
  38|  B. Definitions. As used in this section, the following terms shall have the meanings indicated:
  39|  BUFFER STRIP
  40|  A strip of land between the solar photovoltaic arrays, and any structures accessory thereto, and the
  41|  boundary of the parcel, reserved for plant material, berms, walls or fencing to serve as a visual barri-
  42|  er.
  43|  LARGE-SCALE GROUND                  -MOUNTED PHOTOVOLTAIC INSTALLATION
  44|  A solar photovoltaic system that is structurally mounted on the ground and has a nameplate capacity
  45|  of 10 kW or greater.
  46|                                                              210

===PAGE 211=== (plain, 53 lines)
   1| 211
   2| PLANT MATERIAL
   3| Trees or shrubs of a type and height that sufficiently screen the arrays without blocking necessary
   4| sun that would hinder the product performance, including ground cover that would screen the lower
   5| portion of the arrays.
   6| RATED NAMEPLATE CAPACITY
   7| The maximum rated output of electric power production of the photovoltaic system in direct current
   8| (DC).
   9| ROOF-MOUNTED SOLAR ENERGY INSTALLATION
  10| Solar photovoltaic arrays placed on the roof of residences, or commercial, industrial or institutional
  11| buildings and Town-owned municipal buildings.
  12| SMALL-SCALE GROUND-MOUNTED PHOTOVOLTAIC INSTALLATION
  13| A solar photovoltaic system that is structurally mounted on the ground and has a nameplate capacity
  14| under 10 kW.
  15| SOLAR PHOTOVOLTAIC ARRAY
  16| An arrangement of solar photovoltaic panels.
  17| C. Small-scale ground-mounted solar photovoltaic installations and roof-mounted solar energy instal-
  18| lations.
  19| (1) Purpose. The purpose of this section is to promote the creation of renewable energy for individual
  20| 2 residences, commercial enterprises and municipal buildings, as-of-right.
  21| (2) Roof-mounted solar energy installations. Roof-mounted solar energy installations shall be located
  22| so as not to increase the total height of the structure more than one foot above the applicable zoning
  23| regulations related to height in the district in which it is located, or such other height as is determined
  24| by the Building Commissioner to be essential for proper operation, but in no case no more than four
  25| feet.
  26| (3) Small-scale ground-mounted solar energy installations.
  27| (a) Small-scale ground-mounted photovoltaic installations are not permitted in the zoning dimension-
  28| al setbacks as listed in § 250-5.2 of the Zoning Bylaw and may not be located closer than 25 feet to
  29| residential side yard lines.
  30| (b) Dimensional setbacks for small-scale ground-mounted photovoltaic installation shall have a front
  31| lot setback no closer than the existing foundation of the primary dwelling to the front property line,
  32| except when the existing foundation of the primary dwelling is more than 125 feet from the front
  33| property line or otherwise if determined appropriate by the Building Commissioner.
  34| (c) Small -scale ground -mounted photovoltaic installations shall be adequately screened from the
  35| neighboring lot line as determined by the Building Commissioner.
  36| (d) A small -scale ground-mounted photovoltaic installation with 20 feet or greater in height, at its
  37| maximum extension, shall require a special permit in accordance with Subsection D(3).
  38| (4) Required documents. The following documents shall be required:
  39| (a) Sun and shadow diagrams specific to the proposed installation to determine the solar access.
  40| (b) Detailed information, including maps, plans or dimensional sketches showing proposed location
  41| of the solar installation, including any setbacks from property lines or distances from structures
  42| which are used for habitation on adjacent properties, and a landscape diagram showing proposed
  43| screening.
  44| (c) Site drawings showing the building and structure footprints, property lines, location and the di-
  45| mensions of solar arrays, ridgeline of roof and description of the installation.
  46| (d) Elevation drawings showing heights of buildings and solar arrays.
  47| (5) Permitting. Small -scale ground-mounted solar photovoltaic installations and roof -mounted solar
  48| energy installations require only a building permit, except that a small -scale ground-mounted solar
  49| photovoltaic installation 20 feet in height or greater, at its maximum extension, shall require a special
  50| permit in accordance with Subsection D(3). All data listed above shall be submitted to the Building
  51| Commissioner. All other necessary permits shall be obtained before a building permit is issued.
  52| (6) As built plans. As -built plans shall be submitted prior to final inspection to the Building Com-
  53| missioner and copied to the Planning Board.

===PAGE 212=== (layout, 53 lines)
   1| D. Large-scale ground-mounted solar photovoltaic installations.
   2| (1) Purpose.
   3| (a) The purpose of this subsection is to facilitate the creation of new large-scale ground-mounted solar
   4| photovoltaic installations by providing standards for the placement, design, construction, operation,
   5| monitoring, modification and removal of such installations that address public safety, minimize 3 im-
   6| pacts on environmental, scenic, natural and historic resources, and to provide adequate financial assur-
   7| ance for the eventual decommissioning of such installations.
   8| (b) Subject to the requirements below, large-scale ground-mounted solar photovoltaic installations are
   9| permitted as-of-right in the Solar Overlay District and by special permit in any other district. The spe-
  10| cial permit granting authority for these purposes shall be the Planning Board.
  11| (2) Solar Overlay District. Large-scale ground-mounted solar photovoltaic installations are allowed by
  12| right in the following designated overlay district:
  13| (a) 27 Youngs Road, 42 acres, Map 115, Parcel 016 (Commercial District).
  14| (b) 671 Lancaster Avenue, 132 acres, Map 110, Parcel 003 (Residence B District).
  15| (c) 500 Leominster Shirley Road, 17.4 acres, Map 144, Parcel 001 (Office Park and Industrial Dis-
  16| trict).
  17| (3) Siting by special permit. Except where permitted as-of-right in the Solar Overlay District, large-
  18| scale ground-mounted solar photovoltaic installations may be located on any lot containing more than
  19| 20 acres in any district by special permit issued by the Planning Board; provided, however, that the
  20| requirements of this § 250-4.15 and the following criteria are met:
  21| (a) Standards. In granting the special permit, the Planning Board shall find that the permit may be
  22| granted without substantial detriment to the public good and without nullifying or substantially dero-
  23| gating from the intent and purpose of this bylaw, and shall find that:
  24| [1] The specific site is an appropriate location for such an installation;
  25| [2] The proposed installation will not adversely affect the existing neighborhood by the following:
  26| [a] Historical significance.
  27| [b] Scenic vistas.
  28| [c] Natural and rural character.
  29| [d] Public safety.
  30| [3] The granting of the special permit will not reasonably diminish the available light, air, sunlight and
  31| other amenities; and
  32| [4] There will be no nuisance or serious hazard to vehicles or pedestrians.
  33| (b) Conditions. The Planning Board may impose any conditions deemed necessary to achieve the
  34| purpose of this bylaw, such as, but not limited to, the following:
  35| [1] Greater than minimum setback requirements;
  36| [2] Modification of exterior appearance;
  37| [3] Limitation of size or extent of facilities;
  38| [4] Regulation of traffic and site plan features;
  39| [5] Screening of premises from view by use of appropriate walls, fencing or buffer strips; 4
  40| [6] Control of the number, location, size and lighting of signs;
  41| [7] Additional design and siting modifications where appropriate.
  42| (4) Development plan review.
  43| (a)  All large-scale ground-mounted solar photovoltaic installations shall require development plan
  44|      review, under § 250-8.4 of the Zoning Bylaws, by the Lunenburg Planning Board. A public infor-
  45|      mation meeting shall be held by said Board. The Building Commissioner shall not issue a building
  46|      permit unless, and until, the Planning Board submits an approved development plan review docu-
  47|      ment, and special permit documentation, where applicable, to the Building Commissioner.
  48| (b) A building permit will be issued by the Building Commissioner that shows evidence that the pro-
  49| ject is consistent with state and federal building codes, the findings and directives of the development
  50| plan review, and/or special permit, and local bylaws and regulations, including those set forth by the
  51| Conservation Commission. As-built plans shall be submitted prior to final inspection to the Building
  52| Commissioner, with copies to the Planning Board.
  53|                                                                212

   1|  (5) Utility notification. Evidence shall be provided at the time of the application for the development
   2|  plan review that the utility company that operates the electrical grid where the installation is to be lo-
   3|  cated has been informed of the applicant's intent to construct a solar photovoltaic installation and that
   4|  approval to connect to the grid has been granted or appropriate application(s) has or will be made to
   5|  such utilities for interconnection. Off-grid systems shall be exempt from this requirement. Reasonable
   6|  efforts should be made to place all utility connections underground, depending on appropriate soil con-
   7|  ditions, shape and topography of the site.
   8|  (6) Fees. An application for a development plan review shall be accompanied by the required fee and a
   9|  tri-party account (in-house escrow account with the Planning Board, developer and Town Treasurer)

  11|  accompanied by the fee required for a building permit. All other fees that shall be required by permit-
  12|  ting parties (Conservation Commission, etc.) shall be administered according to their regulations.
  13|  (7) Setbacks and buffer strips.
  14|  (a) Buffer strips in all districts. All ground-mounted installations shall be surrounded by a buffer strip
  15|  which shall be 200 feet in depth in a Residential, Conservation or Recreation District and shall be 50
  16|  feet in a Commercial or Industrial District unless it abuts a Residential, Conservation or Recreation
  17|  District, in which case the buffer strip shall be 200 feet in depth along such abutting lot lines, unless
  18|  the applicant can demonstrate, and the Planning Board finds, that adequate buffering can be provided
  19|  in a narrower buffer strip.
  20|  (b) Setbacks in all districts.
  21|  [1] Front yard. The front yard shall be at least 50 feet; provided, however, that when the lot is in a Res-
  22|  idential, Outlying or Recreation District or abuts a Residential, Conservation, or a Recreation District,
  23|  the front lot setback shall not be less than 200 feet.
  24|  [2] Side yard. Each side yard setback shall be at least 50 feet; provided, however, that when the lot is
  25|  in a Residential, Outlying or Recreation District or abuts a Residential, Conservation, or a Recreation
  26|  District, the side yard setback shall not be less than 200 feet.
  27|  [3] Rear yard. The rear yard shall be at least 50 feet; provided, however, that when the lot is in a Resi-
  28|  dential, Outlying or Recreation District or abuts a Residential, Conservation, or a Recreation District,
  29|  the rear yard setback shall not be less than 200 feet. 5 (8) Required documents. In addition to docu-

  32|  ments in its sole discretion under appropriate circumstances for large-scale ground-mounted solar pho-
  33|  tovoltaic installations between 10 kW and 249 kW.
  34|  (a) All plans and maps shall be prepared, stamped and signed by a professional engineer licensed to
  35|  practice in Massachusetts.
  36|  (b) Property lines and physical features, including roads for property sites.

   1|  (n) Names of contractors, address, telephone number, e-mail.

   3|  (p) Zoning district designation for parcel of land, map and parcel.

  13|  (v) A screening plan, in compliance with Subsection D(9)(f).
  14|  (9) Design standards.
  15|  (a) Lighting and security. Lighting of solar photovoltaic installations shall be consistent with local,
  16|  state and federal law. Lighting of other parts of the installation, such as any appurtenant structures,
  17|  shall be limited to that required for safety and operational purposes and shall be reasonably shielded
  18|  from abutting properties. Where feasible, any required lighting shall be directed downward and shall
  19|  incorporate full-cut-off fixtures to reduce light pollution. Surveillance and security cameras shall be
  20|  shielded from viewing abutting private property or invading the privacy of any abutting residential
  21|  property owner.
  22|  (b) Signage. Signage size shall comply with the Zoning Bylaw and shall not be used for displaying
  23|  any advertising except to identify the owner and/or operator of the solar installation and a twenty-four
  24|  -hour emergency contact telephone number.
  25|  (c) Land clearing. Clearing of natural vegetation shall be limited to what is necessary for construc-
  26|  tion, operation and maintenance of the installation Any land disturbance, including earth removal of
  27|  land cleared greater than one acre shall be subject to stormwater management criteria and/or as other-
  28|  wise prescribed by applicable laws, regulations and bylaws.
  29|  (d) Safety, emergency service and environmental standards. The applicant shall provide a copy of the
  30|  project summary, electrical schematic, and site plan. The applicant shall develop an emergency re-
  31|  sponse plan, including showing all means of shutting down the solar installation. The applicant shall
  32|  submit the name of the person answerable to inquires throughout the life of the installation. If the des-
  33|  ignated person changes, the name of the new designated person shall be submitted as an addendum.
  34|  (e) Monitoring and maintenance.
  35|  [1] The applicant shall submit a plan for the operation and maintenance of the installation which shall
  36|  include measures for maintaining the site, including safe access, stormwater control, structural repairs
  37|  and the integrity of security measures. These measures must be acceptable to the Fire Chief and emer-
  38|  gency medical services personnel. If needed, training of service personnel will be provided by the
  39|  applicant. The owner/operator shall be responsible for the cost of maintaining the installation.
  40|  [2] The applicant shall also submit a monitoring/inspection form under the development plan review
  41|  during construction, and shall further submit a report on the condition of the structure and site by Jan-
  42|  uary 15 of each year.
  43|  (f) Visual impact. Any large-scale ground-mounted solar photovoltaic installation shall be designed to
  44|  minimize visual impacts, including preserving natural vegetation to the maximum extent possible,
  45|  blending in equipment with the surroundings, and adding vegetative buffers to screen abutting resi-
  46|  dential properties, whether developed or not. Siting shall be such that the view of the solar electric                         -
  47|  generating installation from other areas of Town shall be as minimal as possible. Buffer strips shall
  48|  surround the proposed project. A screening plan, that assures the facility is shielded to greatest extent
  49|  possible from public view, shall be required to be reviewed under the development plan review.
  50|  (g) Height. The height of any structure associated with a large-scale ground-mounted solar photovol-
  51|  taic installation shall not exceed 35 feet. 7 (h) Roads. All access roads and interior roads shall be con-
  52|  structed to minimize grading, removal of stone wall or trees and to minimize impacts to environmen-
  53|  tal or historic resources.
  54|                                                                214

===PAGE 215=== (layout, 53 lines)
   1|  (i) Hazardous materials. Hazardous materials stored, used, or generated on site shall not exceed the
   2|  amount for a very small quantity generator of hazardous waste as defined by the Department of Envi-
   3|  ronmental Protection ("DEP") pursuant to 310 CMR 30.000 and shall meet all requirements of the
   4|  DEP, including storage of hazardous materials in a building with an impervious floor that is not adja-
   5|  cent to any floor drains to prevent discharge to the outdoor environment. If hazardous materials are
   6|  utilized within the solar electric equipment, then impervious containment areas capable of controlling
   7|  any release to the environment and to prevent potential contamination of groundwater are required.
   8|  (j) Noise. Noise generated by a large-scale ground-mounted solar photovoltaic installation, and by any
   9|  associated equipment and machinery, shall conform to applicable state and local noise regulations,
  10|  including the Department of Environmental Protection's Division of Air Quality noise regulations, 310
  11|  CMR 7.10. The site shall not produce any other vibration, harmonics, or interference which would be
  12|  perceived or impact the normal function of electronics off site.
  13|  (10) Modifications. All modification requests to a solar photovoltaic installation, including changes in
  14|  arrays, addition to number of arrays or change in placement, made after issuance of the required build-
  15|  ing permit shall require review through development plan review for compliance with this bylaw.
  16|  (11) Abandonment and removal. Absent notice of the proposed date of decommissioning or written
  17|  notice of extenuating circumstances, the solar photovoltaic installation shall be considered abandoned
  18|  when it fails to operate for more than one year without the written consent of the Planning Board. The
  19|  owner or operator shall physically remove the installation no more than 150 days after the date of
  20|  abandonment or the proposed date of decommissioning operations. The owner or operator shall notify
  21|  the Planning Board by certified mail of the proposed date of discontinued operations and plans for re-
  22|  moval. The notification shall consist of the methodology of physical removal of all structures, equip-
  23|  ment, security barriers and transmission lines, disposal of all solid and hazardous waste and stabiliza-
  24|  tion or revegetation of the site. Landscaping, etc. may be left upon approval of the Planning Board. If
  25|  the owner or operator fails to remove the installation in accordance with the above criteria, the Town
  26|  may, after the receipt of an appropriate court order or consent of the property owner, enter the property
  27|  and physically remove the installation at the owner's expense. As a condition of development plan re-
  28|  view, a property owner shall agree to allow the Town entry to remove an abandoned or decommis-
  29|  sioned installation. The cost for the removal will be charged to the property owner in accordance with
  30|  the provisions of MGL c. 139, § 3A as a tax lien on the property.
  31|  (12) Financial surety. Proponents of large-scale solar photovoltaic projects shall provide a form of
  32|  surety, either through an escrow account, bond or otherwise, to cover the cost of removal in the event
  33|  the Town must remove the installation and remediate the landscape, in an amount and form deter-
  34|  mined to be reasonable by the development plan review authority, but in no event to exceed more than
  35|  125% of the cost of removal and compliance with the additional requirements set forth herein, as de-
  36|  termined by the project proponent. Such surety will not be required for municipally or state                          -owned
  37|  facilities. The project owner/operator shall submit a fully inclusive estimate of the costs associated
  38|  with removal, prepared by a qualified engineer. The amount shall include a mechanism for calculating
  39|  increased removal cost due to inflation.
  40|  E. Inclusionary uses and conflicts.
  41|  (1) Small accessory or ornamental solar products which do not generate electricity for use in a dwell-
  42|  ing or structure are exempt from the provisions in this bylaw. 8 (2) In the event that any part of this
  43|  section conflicts with other requirements of the Zoning Bylaw, the requirements of this section shall
  44|  apply.
  45|  § 250-4.16 14 Registered marijuana dispensaries.
  46|  [Added 5-3-2014 ATM by Art. 27]
  47|  A. Purpose: to provide for the placement of registered marijuana dispensaries (RMDs), in accordance
  48|  with the Humanitarian Medical Use of Marijuana Act, MGL c. 94C, App. 1-1 - Chapter 369 of the
  49|  Acts of 2012, in locations suitable for lawful medical marijuana facilities and to minimize adverse im-
  50|  pact of RMDs on adjacent properties, residential neighborhoods, historic  districts, schools,  play-
  51|  grounds and other locations where minors congregate by regulating the siting, design, placement, secu-
  52|  rity and removal of RMDs.
  53|                                                               215

===PAGE 216=== (layout, 55 lines)
   1| B. Applicability.
   2| (1) All RMDs shall comply with the regulations promulgated by the Massachusetts Department of Pub-
   3| lic Health (DPH), 105 CMR 725.000, Implementation of an Act for the Humanitarian Medical Use of
   4| Marijuana, effective May 24, 2013, and any subsequent amendments thereto.
   5| (2) The Town reserves the right to require compliance with provisions in the DPH regulations for which
   6| the state granted waivers and/or exemptions to the RMD applicant based on the provisions of §                             250-
   7| 8.3C (as to special permits) and § 250-8.4 (as to development plan review).
   8| C. Definition. As used in this section, the following terms shall have the meanings indicated:
   9| REGISTERED MARIJUANA DISPENSARY
  10| Also known as a "medical marijuana treatment center," means a not-for-profit entity registered under
  11| 105 CMR 725.000 that acquires, cultivates, possesses, processes (including development of related
  12| products such as edible marijuana-infused products, tinctures, aerosols, oils, or ointments), transfers,
  13| transports, sells, distributes, dispenses, or administers marijuana, products containing marijuana, related
  14| supplies, or educational materials to registered qualifying patients or their personal caregivers. Unless
  15| otherwise specified, "RMD" refers to the site(s) of dispensing, cultivation, and preparation of marijua-
  16| na.
  17| D. Location.
  18| (1) RMDs that include retail, processing and cultivation are allowed in Commercial Districts (C) by
  19| special permit.
  20| (2) Cultivation and/or agricultural processing: Allowed in all Residential Districts by Special Permit but
  21| only if the applicant is otherwise eligible for protection under §250-4.1.c(2) and/or G.L. c. 40A, § 3.
  22| Cultivation and/or agricultural processing are allowed in all districts by right if the applicant is eligible
  23| for protection under § 250-4.1C(2) and MGL c. 40A, § 3. Any cultivation within a building will re-
  24| quire a special permit.
  25| (3) RMD facilities that cultivate and process, but do not do retail of marijuana, are allowed in Office
  26| Park and Industrial Districts (OP/I) by special permit.
  27| (4) All cultivation must be conducted within a building which must be located at least five hundred
  28| (500) feet from any residential property line.
  29| E. Procedure for submittal. 9 (1) The Planning Board shall be the special permit granting authority
  30| (SPGA) for a RMD special permit per MGL c. 40A, § 9. Siting shall be by special permit and develop-
  31| ment plan review per § 250-8.4 of the Lunenburg Protective Bylaw.
  32| (2) Criteria. In granting any special permit, the Planning Board shall assure that the proposed use:
  33| (a) Will not be injurious or dangerous to the public health or unduly hazardous because of traffic con-
  34| gestion, danger or fire or explosion or other reasons.
  35| (b) Will not have a material adverse effect on the value of land and buildings in the neighborhood or on
  36| the amenities of the neighborhood.
  37| (c) Will be operated with reasonable regard for order and sightliness, if an open use.
  38| (d) Will not produce noise, vibration, smoke, dust, odor, heat or glare observable at the lot lines in
  39| amounts clearly detrimental to the normal use of adjacent property.
  40| (3) It is recommended that a concept plan showing the proposed use shall be submitted to the Planning
  41| Board for review and discussion prior to the filing for development plan review.
  42| (4) The provisions of § 250-8.3C (as to special permits) and §                     250-8.4 (as to development plan re-
  43| view) shall apply.
  44| (5) Filing under §§ 250-8.3C and 250-8.4 may be simultaneous.
  45| (6)  A special permit granted under this section shall have a term limited to the duration of the appli-
  46|      cant's ownership of the premises as a RMD. A special permit may be transferred only with the ap-
  47|      proval of the special permit granting authority in the form of an amendment to the special permit
  48|      with all information required in this section.
  49| F. Conditional standards.
  50| (1) Distance. All proposed RMDs shall be sited accordingly as stated in Massachusetts Department of
  51| Public Health (DPH) 105 CMR 725.110(A)(14), which states a RMD shall not be sited within a radius
  52| of 500 feet of a school, day-care center, or any facility in which children commonly congregate. The
  53| five-hundred-foot distance under this section is measured in a straight line from the nearest point of the
  54| proposed RMD.
  55|                                                               216

===PAGE 217=== (layout, 53 lines)
   1|  (2) Setbacks and buffer strips. Cultivation facilities located outside of Retail Commercial (RC), Com-
   2|  mercial (C), or Office Park and Industrial Districts (OP/I) shall be surrounded by a buffer strip which
   3|  shall be five hundred (500)200 feet in depth unless the applicant can demonstrate, and the Planning
   4|  Board finds, that adequate buffering can be provided in a narrower buffer strip. In all other districts,
   5|  existing setbacks will apply.
   6|  (3) Design standards. In addition to requirements contained elsewhere in this bylaw, §                      250-4.6E, De-
   7|  sign standards, shall be applicable to a RMD. The applicant shall meet all requirements of the Commer-
   8|  cial District (C) or DPH guidelines, whichever is more restrictive.
   9|  (4) Waivers. The Planning Board may waive any of the non-dimensional conditional standards within
  10|  this section, provided that such waiver will not derogate from the intent established herein.
  11|  G. Security.
  12|  (1) All security measures shall be approved by the Lunenburg Fire and Police Chiefs. An active securi-
  13|  ty system shall be required for all RMD locations and approved by both the Lunenburg Fire and Police
  14|  10 Chiefs and submitted to the Planning Board for final approval.
  15|  (2) RMDs shall be open to inspection by the Fire Department, Police Department, Building Commis-
  16|  sioner and the Board of Health with 24 hours' notice of request for such inspection to be made by the
  17|  Town department or official. A property contact shall be available to such Town department or official
  18|  24 hours a day, seven days a week.
  19|  H. Documentation. The Planning Board shall be provided with all decisions or approvals, denials or
  20|  other substantive actions by DPH regarding the RMD and all submittals of information relating to such
  21|  activities between the applicant or RMD and DPH.
  22|  § 250-4.18 15 Temporary moratorium on sale and distribution of recreational marijuana.
  23|  [Added 5-6-2017 ATM by Art. 33]
  24|  A. Purpose.
  25|  (1) By vote at the state election on November 8, 2016, the voters of the commonwealth approved a law
  26|  regulating the cultivation, distribution, possession and use of marijuana for recreational purposes. The
  27|  law provides that it is effective on December 15, 2016, and the Cannabis Control Commission is cur-
  28|  rently required to issue regulations regarding implementation by March 15, 2018 (see Section 11 of
  29|  Chapter 351 of the Acts of 2016).
  30|  (2) Currently under the Zoning Bylaw, recreational marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers
  31|  are not a permitted use in the Town, and any regulations promulgated by the State Cannabis Control
  32|  Commission are expected to provide guidance to the Town in regulating recreational marijuana estab-
  33|  lishments and marijuana retailers.
  34|  (3) The regulation of recreational marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers raises novel and
  35|  complex legal, planning, and public safety issues and the Town needs time to study and consider the
  36|  regulation of recreational marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers and address such novel and
  37|  complex issues, as well as to address the potential impact of the state regulations on local zoning and to
  38|  undertake a planning process to consider amending the Zoning Bylaw regarding regulation of recrea-
  39|  tional marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers and other uses related to the regulation of recre-
  40|  ational marijuana. The Town intends to adopt a temporary moratorium on the use of land and structures
  41|  in the Town for recreational marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers so as to allow the Town
  42|  sufficient time to engage in a planning process to address effects of such structures and uses in the
  43|  Town and to adopt provisions of the Zoning Bylaw in a manner consistent with sound land use plan-
  44|  ning goals and objectives.
  45|  B. Definitions.
  46|  MANUFACTURE
  47|  To compound, blend, extract, infuse or otherwise make or prepare a marijuana product.
  48|  MARIJUANA ACCESSORIES
  49|  Equipment, products, devices or materials of any kind that are intended or designed for use in planting,
  50|  propagating, cultivating, growing, harvesting, manufacturing, compounding, converting, producing,
  51|  processing, preparing, testing, analyzing, packaging, repackaging, storing, containing, ingesting, inhal-
  52|  ing or otherwise introducing marijuana into the human body.
  53|                                                              217

===PAGE 218=== (plain, 49 lines)
   1| 218
   2| MARIJUANA CULTIVATOR
   3| An entity licensed to cultivate, process and package marijuana, to deliver marijuana to marijuana es-
   4| tablishments and to transfer marijuana to other marijuana establishments, but not to consumers.
   5|  MARIJUANA ESTABLISHMENT
   6| A marijuana cultivator, marijuana testing facility, marijuana product manufacturer, marijuana retailer
   7| or any other type of licensed marijuana-related business.
   8| MARIJUANA PRODUCT MANUFACTURER
   9| An entity licensed to obtain, manufacture, process and package marijuana and marijuana products, to
  10| deliver marijuana and marijuana products to marijuana establishments and to transfer marijuana and
  11| marijuana products to other marijuana establishments, but not to consumers.
  12| MARIJUANA PRODUCTS
  13| Products that have been manufactured and contain marijuana or an extract from marijuana, including
  14| concentrated forms of marijuana and products composed of marijuana and other ingredients that are
  15| intended for use or consumption, including edible products, beverages, topical products, ointments,
  16| oils and tinctures.
  17| MARIJUANA TESTING FACILITY
  18| An entity licensed to test marijuana and marijuana products, including certification for potency and the
  19| presence of contaminants.
  20| MARIJUANA RETAILER
  21| An entity licensed to purchase and deliver marijuana and marijuana products from marijuana establish-
  22| ments and to deliver, sell or otherwise transfer marijuana and marijuana products to marijuana estab-
  23| lishments and to consumers.
  24| C. Temporary moratorium. For the reasons set forth above and notwithstanding any other provision of
  25| the Zoning Bylaw to the contrary, the Town hereby adopts a temporary moratorium on the use of land
  26| or structures for recreational marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers. The moratorium shall
  27| be in effect through November 30, 2018. During the moratorium period, the Town shall undertake a
  28| planning process to address the potential impacts of recreational marijuana in the Town, consider the
  29| Cannabis Control Commission regulations regarding recreational marijuana establishments and mari-
  30| juana retailers and related uses, determine whether the Town will prohibit on -site consumption at rec-
  31| reational marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers and shall consider adopting new provisions
  32| of the Zoning Bylaw to address the impact and operation of recreational marijuana establishments and
  33| marijuana retailers and related uses.
  34| D. Severability. The provisions of this bylaw are severable. If any provision, paragraph, sentence, or
  35| clause of this bylaw or the application thereof to any person, establishment, or circumstances shall be
  36| held invalid.

  39| ARTICLE 6. To see if the Town will vote to hear an interim report from the Charter Review Committee; or take any other
  40| action relative thereto. (Submitted by the Charter Review Committee) (Board of Selectmen recommends approval) (Finance
  41| Committee voted no financial impact). VOTED to hear interim report from the Charter Review Committee as follows:
  42| The charter provides: At least once in every 10 years a special committee to consist of 9 members shall be established for
  43| the purpose of reviewing this charter and to make a report, with recommendations, to the town mee ting concerning any
  44| proposed amendments which said committee may determine to be necessary or desirable. We have been meeting regularly
  45| since forming in August 2017 and will submit the final proposed charter for the May 2019 Annual Town Meeting.
  46| Members include:
  47| Board of Selectmen appointees: Robert Ebersole and Jamie Toale

===PAGE 221=== (plain, 55 lines)
   1| 221
   2| Finance Committee appointees: Theresa Burchfield and Mark Erickson
   3| School Committee appointees: Heather Sroka and .Jim LaVeck (Katey Adams and Michael Mackin were school appointees
   4| until June 2018)
   5| Planning Board appointee: Michael-Ray Jeffreys
   6| Town Moderator appointees: Nancy Gray and Steven Archambault
   7| Town Boards, committees and officials were assigned to members of the committee, who then reached out to gather their
   8| opinions on the current charter. In some cases, the response was no recommended change, and in other cases, different
   9| wording was proposed. We voted to establish a supermajority vote requirement of 6 for any proposed change instead of a
  10| simple majority of 5 in order to improve on likelihood of acceptance at town meeting. The resulting draft document was
  11| presented at a public hearing on April 5th. From comments received in advance and at the hearing, it was clear that our
  12| attempt to clarify some sections did not work and other sections went further than desired. We have eliminated many of the
  13| proposed changes.
  14| The committee believes that while the current charter generally works, it is important to regularly review it based on evolvi ng
  15| town government and changes to state laws. The proposed draft corrects typographical errors, changed some working to be
  16| clearer without altering the meaning or intent of existing provisions, assuring that one section of the charter is consistent  with
  17| another, as well as updating requirements of the open meeting law to comply with state law. There were areas that needed
  18| clarification including town manager supervision of staff serving elected boards and assuring that we have systems in place
  19| so that all town employees are properly supervised and evaluated. We provided for a clearer due process system in the town
  20| manager removal section. The major changes are as follows:
  21| 1. Change the name of the board of selectmen to select board.
  22| While not a substantive change, this recognizes that women have and do serve on the board.
  23| 2. Change the manner of selection of the town clerk from elected to appointed by the town manager subject to
  24| ratification by the select board.
  25| The town clerk is currently an elected position to a three year term. As the responsibilit ies and qualifications have
  26| grown, many towns have changed to a town clerk position appointed by the town manager and ratified by the board of
  27| select board. This is the only position that is still an elected employee, as other positions (i.e., treasurer an d tax
  28| collector) were changed long ago to appointed positions. The town clerk’s duties are administrative and do not rise to
  29| the level of policy making like those of elected boards. While we recognize that this position has been answerable
  30| directly to the town, an appointment will ensure that someone with appropriate qualifications is hired.
  31| 3. Providing that persons appointed to town committees be residents registered to vote in town.
  32| This will assure that committee members can participate and vote at town me eting.
  33| 4. Providing for a second town meeting in the fall, the time and date of which to be set by by -law.
  34| This will provide an opportunity to shorten and spread out the work of town meeting to make it more likely to have
  35| greater participation of town residents.
  36| 5. Providing that conduct of town meeting to be governed by “Town Meeting Time”.
  37| This to clarify that these are the town meeting rules of order that we follow now.
  38| 6. Clarify and confirm the appointment powers of the select board and town manager to provide for appointment
  39| of various department heads be appointed by the town manager subject to ratification by the select board.
  40| The town manager appoints the majority of department heads subject to ratification of the select board, and this shifts
  41| the rest of those positions. Town Accountant remains appointed solely by the selectmen.
  42| 7. Clarifying the duties of the town manager relating to personnel management, jurisdiction over town facilities,
  43| property and day to day operations, the budget process and enforcement of charter provisions and town by-
  44| laws.
  45| The charter provides that elected boards set policy and then town employees under the direction of the town manager
  46| implement that policy and perform day to day operations. These changes clarifies or shift duties to the town manager
  47| to assure that the town is in compliance with state and federal law, follows good management practices and meets
  48| customer service standards.
  49| 8. Providing more specifics in the process for removal and suspension of the town manager.
  50| This provides for the town moderator to be the hearing officer and provides other due process provisions.
  51| 9. Requiring a minimum number of votes for write in candidates where there are no names on the ballot.
  52| This assures that in order to be elected, a person has to receive at least the number of signatures required to get on
  53| the ballot. Currently about 40-50.
  54| 10. Make certain other grammatical and phrasing changes that do not alter the meaning or intent of existing
  55| provisions, as well as updating requirements of the open meeting law to comply with state law.

===PAGE 222=== (plain, 64 lines)
   1| 222
   2| These changes help clarify how the charter works so that all involved understand its provisions.
   3| There were several changes that we considered but voted not to adopt including representative town meeting, term limits for
   4| elected officials, and ranked choice voting.
   5| The charter review committee will continue to meet with committees and town officials and will hold two public hearings which  will
   6| be publicized in advance. They will be televised over our PACC television channel, and then posted to YouTube. The proposed
   7| charter will be presented for approval at the May 2019 Annual Town Meeting. Changing the name of the board of selectmen to
   8| select board and appointing the town clerk will be presented in articles separate from the simpler proposed changes, and we will
   9| provide a pro and con statement for each.

   1| 223
   2| Earned vacation time up to and including 80 hours must be used in the year granted.  No unused portion of this allowance shal l
   3| be carried forward into a subsequent vacation year, nor shall any monetary compensation be given for such unused portion.
   4| Earned vacation time in excess of 80 hours should be used in the year granted, and no portion of such excess shall be carried
   5| forward into a subsequent year.  If departmental scheduling or other serious extenuating circumstances preclude the use of al l
   6| such excess in the year granted, the employee may request to shall receive compensation equivalent to such unused portion in a
   7| lump sum payable on the  last first payroll warrant in Julyne, in addition to their regular salary , or may be allowed to carry over
   8| such ex cess as the Town may elect in its sole discretion. Any employee asserting a serious extenuating circumstance must
   9| submit a written request to his/her direct supervisor and such request must be approved by the direct supervisor and the Town
  10| Manager before t he employee shall be compensated for, or allowed to carry over, such excess ; provided, however, that such
  11| payment must be approved by the Department Head and written authorization signed by the Town Manager in advance of such
  12| payment.
  13| The vacation year sh all begin July 1.  In the first year of employment, employees shall accrue one (1) day of vacation leave
  14| (prorated based on number of hours worked each week) per month of service completed to a maximum of 80 hours to be
  15| available for use by the employee on  the following July 1 st.  Vacation leave accrued each fiscal year will be available for use on
  16| the following July 1 st.  Any change in the rate of accrual shall occur on the employee’s anniversary date.  When an employee’s
  17| anniversary date for the completio n of the 5 th, 10th-20th years occurs after July 1 st, the employee shall receive vacation leave for
  18| the remainder of the year on a pro-rated basis.
  19| SECTION 21. FAMILY AND MEDICAL LEAVE
  20| The Town agrees to abide by the provisions of the 1993 Family and Medical Leave Act (FMLA). All eligible employees are
  21| entitled to up to twelve (12) weeks of unpaid leave for certain family and medical reasons in a one (1) year period.  Employe es
  22| are eligible for FMLA if they have worked for the Town for at least one (1)  year and at least for one thousand two hundred and
  23| fifty (1,250) hours over the previous twelve (12) months.
  24| Unpaid FMLA leave must be granted for any of the following reasons:
  25|  To care for the employee’s child after birth, or placement for adoption or f oster care
  26|  To care for the employee’s spouse, son, daughter, or parent, who has a serious health condition
  27|  For a serious health condition that makes the employee unable to perform the employee’s job
  28| In accordance with the past practiceTown’s policy, the Town allows, at the option of the employee  requires, the use of paid leave
  29| time during the FMLA absence including accumulated Sick Leave, Vacation Leave, and Personal Leave.  The Town requires all
  30| employees applying for an FMLA leave to notify their Department Head, in writing, thirty (30) days prior to the leave, if possible.
  31| SECTION 28.  DEFINITIONS
  32| Hourly Employee shall mean any employee of the Town who by virtue of their classified position is compensated at an hourly
  33| rate for services rendered. Hourly employees are not eligible for benefits (i.e. vacation, sick time, insurance, etc.).

  62| ARTICLE 16 . To see if the Town will vote to amend the Code of Lunenburg, Chapter 250, entitled “Zoning”, to modify
  63| §250-4.15.C by deleting November 30, 2018 and inserting May 31, 2019 and §250 -4.15.D by inserting a comma at the end
  64| thereof followed by “the remaining portions shall remain in full force and effec t” both regarding the temporary moratorium on
  65| the sale and distribution of recreational marijuana, with additions to the Bylaw underlined and deletions stricken through
  66| below; or take any other action relative thereto. (Submitted by the Planning Board reco mmends approval 4 to 0) (Board of
  67| Selectmen recommends approval) (Finance Committee voted no financial impact).  VOTED (DECLARED 2/3rds) to amend
  68| the Code of Lunenburg, Chapter 250, entitled “Zoning”, to modify §250-4.15.C by deleting November 30, 2018 and inserting
  69| May 31, 2019 and §250-4.15.D by inserting a comma at the end thereof followed by “the remaining portions shall remain in
  70| full force and effect” both regarding the temporary moratorium on the sale and distribution of recreational marijuana, with
  71| additions to the Bylaw underlined and deletions stricken through as follows:
  72| § 250-4.15 Temporary moratorium on sale and distribution of recreational marijuana.
  73| [Added 5-6-2017 ATM by Art. 33]
  74| C. Temporary moratorium. For the reasons set forth above and notwithstanding any other provision of the Zoning Bylaw
  75| to the contrary, the Town hereby adopts a temporary moratorium on the use of land or structures for recreational
  76| marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers. The moratorium shall be in effect throug h November 30May 31,
  77| 20182019. During the moratorium period, the Town shall undertake a planning process to address the potential
  78| impacts of recreational marijuana in the Town, consider the Cannabis Control Commission regulations regarding
  79| recreational marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers and related uses, determine whether the Town will
  80| prohibit on-site consumption at recreational marijuana establishments and marijuana retailers and shall consider
  81| adopting new provisions of the Zoning Bylaw to address the impact and operation of recreational marijuana
  82| establishments and marijuana retailers and related uses.
  83| D. Severability. The provisions of this bylaw are severable. If any provision, paragraph, sentence, or clause of this
  84| bylaw or the application thereof to any person, establishment, or circumstances shall be held invalid the  remaining
  85| portions shall remain in full force and effect.
  86| ARTICLE 17. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Town Bylaws by adding a Bylaw for the reduct ion of
  87| plastic bags, or take any other action relative thereto. (Citizen Petition) (Board of Selectmen recommends approval)
  88| (Finance Committee voted no financial impact). VOTED to amend the Code of Lunenburg by adding a new Chapter 92,
  89| entitled Reduction of Plastic Bags as follows:

===PAGE 232=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 232
   2| Section 1. Purpose and Intent
   3| The production and use of thin-film single-use plastic checkout bags have significant impacts on the
   4| environment, including, but not limited to: contributing to the potential death of marine animals through
   5| ingestion and entanglement; contributing to pollution of the land environment; creating a burden to solid
   6| waste collection-and recycling facilities; clogging storm drainage systems; and requiring the use of
   7| millions of barrels of crude oil nationally for their manufacture. The purpose of this bylaw is to  eliminate
   8| the usage of thin -film single-use plastic bags by all retail and grocery stores in the Town of
   9| Lunenburg, by June 1, 2019.
  10| Section 2. Definitions
  11| 2.1 Checkout bag: A carryout bag provided by a store to a consumer at the
  12| point of sale. Checkout bags shall not include bags, whether plastic or
  13| not, in which loose produce or products are placed by the consumer to
  14| deliver such items to the point of sale or checkout area of the  store.
  15| 2.2 Grocery Store: A retail establishment where  more than fifty percent (50%)
  16| of the gross  floor area is devoted to the sale of food products for home
  17| preparation and consumption, which typically also offers home care and
  18| personal care products.
  19| 2.3 Retail Store: An establishment that offers the sale and display of
  20| merchandise within a building.
  21| 2.1 Reusable checkout bag: A bag, with handles, that is specifically
  22| designed for multiple  use and is made of thick plastic, cloth, fabric or
  23| other durable materials.
  24| 2.2 Thin-film single-use plastic bags: Typically with plastic handles, these
  25| are bags with a thickness of 2.5 mils or less and are intended for
  26| single-use transport of purchased  products.
  27| Section 3. Use Regulations
  28| 3.1 Thin-film single-use plastic bags shall not be distributed, used, or sold for
  29| checkout or other purposes at any retail or grocery store within the Town of
  30| Lunenburg.
  31| 3.2 Customers are encouraged to bring their own reusable or biodegradable
  32| shopping bags  to stores. Retail or grocery stores are strongly
  33| encouraged to make reusable checkout bags available for sale to
  34| costumers at a reasonable  price.
  35| 3.3 Thin-filmed plastic bags used to contain dry cleaning, newpapers, produce,
  36| meat, bulk foods, wet items and other similar merchandise, typically without
  37| handles, are still permissible.
  38| Section 4. Enforcement Process
  39| Enforcement of this bylaw shall be the responsibility of the Town Manager or his/her
  40| designee. The Town Manager shall determine the inspection process to be followed,
  41| incorporating the process into other town duties as appropriate. Any retail or grocery st ore
  42| distributing plastic grocery bags in violation of this bylaw shall be subject to a non -criminal
  43| disposition fine as specified in Appendix A of the Regulations for Enforcement of Town
  44| Bylaws under M.G.L
  45|      Chapter 40, 21D and the Bylaw for Non-Criminal Disposition of Violations. Any such

===PAGE 233=== (plain, 18 lines)
   1| 233
   2| fines shall be paid to the Town of Lunenburg.
   3| And to amend Appendix A of the Non-Criminal Disposition Bylaw by adding the following:
   4| Bylaw Fine Schedule Fine Allowed Enforcement Agency
   5| Plastic Bag 1st Offense Warning Town Manager’s
   6| Reduction Bylaw 2nd Offense $25.00 Designee
   7| 3rd  & each
   8| subsequent $50.00 Offense
